Start here — in plain words

Start here

  1. Anabelian geometry studies how the shape of a curved space can be recovered from its algebraic symmetry group.
  2. Think of a fingerprint: the unique pattern of ridges lets you identify a person without seeing their face.
  3. It links number theory and geometry, letting mathematicians translate hard arithmetic problems into group theory.
  4. The fundamental group carries the whole geometry of a hyperbolic curve.

Anabelian Geometry — the Grothendieck Conjecture & the Galois Section Conjecture

Anabelian geometry is a profound program inaugurated by Alexander Grothendieck in his 1983 "Letter to Faltings". The central insight: for a hyperbolic algebraic curve X (smooth, projective, genus g ≥ 2) over a number field K, the entire geometry of X is encoded in its étale fundamental group π₁^{ét}(X). More precisely, the curve can be reconstructed functorially from its profinite fundamental group equipped with the natural action of the absolute Galois group G_K = Gal(K̄/K). This program connects three deep conjectures: the Grothendieck Conjecture (proved by Mochizuki, 1996) — that the étale fundamental group determines the curve up to isomorphism; the Section Conjecture (still open) — relating rational points to sections of the fundamental group exact sequence; and the Neukirch-Uchida Theorem — that number fields are determined by their absolute Galois groups. The program has culminated in Mochizuki's Inter-Universal Teichmüller Theory (IUT), a vast extension aiming at the abc conjecture.

"An anabelian variety is one whose geometry is encoded in its fundamental group."
— Alexander Grothendieck
Part 1

📏 The Étale Fundamental Group

Foundations
Étale Covers, the Profinite Fundamental Group, and the Short Exact Sequence
For a connected scheme X with a geometric point ̄x, the étale fundamental group π₁^{ét}(X, ̄x) is the profinite group π₁^{ét}(X, ̄x) = lim← (X' → X) Gal(X'/X) where X' runs over all finite étale covers of X. This is the algebraic analogue of the topological fundamental group. For a smooth projective curve X over ℂ, we have an isomorphism π₁^{ét}(X) ≅ ̂π₁^{top}(X) — the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group. The fundamental group of a genus g curve is ̂Π_g = ⟨a₁,b₁,…,a_g,b_g | ∏[a_i,b_i] = 1⟩^̂. For a curve X over a field K, the absolute Galois group G_K = Gal(K̄/K) acts on π₁(X_{K̄}) via the short exact sequence: 1 → π₁(X_{K̄}) → π₁(X) → G_K → 1. The anabelian property: a hyperbolic curve (genus ≥ 2) over a number field is anabelian — it can be reconstructed from its étale fundamental group. The key idea: the outer Galois action ρ_X: G_K → Out(π₁(X_{K̄})) is faithful for hyperbolic curves, and the geometry of the curve is encoded in this representation. Grothendieck called such varieties "anabelian" — their étale fundamental group is as rich as possible, with trivial center and large outer automorphism group.
The étale fundamental group generalizes the topological fundamental group to schemes; for hyperbolic curves, the Galois action on π₁ is so rich that it encodes the entire geometry.
Part 2

🌍 The Neukirch-Uchida Theorem

Number Fields
Galois Groups Determine Number Fields: Decomposition Groups and the Anabelian Analog for Curves
Neukirch (1969): if G_ℚ ≅ G_K (as topological groups), then K ≅ ℚ (a field isomorphism). Uchida (1976): number fields are determined by their absolute Galois groups: if G_K ≅ G_L as profinite groups, then K ≅ L as fields. The approach uses decomposition groups: for a prime p of K, choose a prime ℘ of K̄ above p; the decomposition group D_℘ = {σ ∈ G_K : σ(℘) = ℘} ⊆ G_K captures the local structure at p. The set of conjugacy classes of decomposition groups in G_K corresponds bijectively to the set of primes of K. The local reciprocity law (class field theory) gives an isomorphism K_p^× ≅ D_℘^{ab}. The global reciprocity ties these local data together. The anabelian analog for curves is the Grothendieck Conjecture for Curves (proved by Mochizuki, 1996): for smooth hyperbolic curves X, Y over a number field K, the natural map Isom_K(X,Y) → Isom_{G_K}(π₁(X_{K̄}), π₁(Y_{K̄}))/π₁(Y) is a bijection. That is, any isomorphism of étale fundamental groups (compatible with the Galois action) comes from a unique isomorphism of curves. The proof uses the p-adic Hodge theory and rigid geometry to reconstruct the curve from its fundamental group via the theory of indigenous bundles and Hodge-theoretic monodromy.
Just as number fields are characterized by their absolute Galois groups (Neukirch-Uchida), hyperbolic curves over number fields are characterized by their étale fundamental groups (Mochizuki).
Part 3

🧮 The Section Conjecture

Rational Points
Sections of the Fundamental Group Exact Sequence and the Bijection with Rational Points
The section conjecture (Grothendieck, 1983) is one of the central open problems in anabelian geometry. For a hyperbolic curve X over a number field K, the étale fundamental group sits in the exact sequence: 1 → π₁(X_{K̄}) → π₁(X) → G_K → 1. A rational point x ∈ X(K) gives, via the functoriality of π₁, a splitting (section) σ_x: G_K → π₁(X) of this exact sequence (well-defined up to conjugation by π₁(X_{K̄})). The conjecture asserts that the map X(K) → {sections σ: G_K → π₁(X) modulo π₁(X_{K̄})-conjugation} is a bijection. The conjecture is open in full generality. The known results: the birational version (the function field case) is true — proven by Koeningsmann and Pop (2000s) using the theory of pseudo-Galois covers. In the p-adic case (K a finite extension of ℚ_p), the section conjecture is related to Belyi's theorem and to the theory of exotic maps on the boundary of the fundamental group. Spivakovsky proved that the section conjecture holds for localizations of ℤ. The conjecture is also known for Stoll's theorem: for curves that satisfy certain conditions (e.g., curves of genus 2 over ℚ), the conjecture follows from the finiteness of rational points. The connection to Teichmüller theory: sections correspond to splittings of the universal curve over the Teichmüller space, linking the conjecture to the geometry of moduli spaces of curves.
The section conjecture would give a purely group-theoretic description of rational points on hyperbolic curves — a powerful link between Diophantine geometry and profinite group theory.
Part 4

🔥 Inter-Universal Teichmüller Theory

IUT
Mochizuki's IUT, the abc Conjecture, and the Controversy
Inter-Universal Teichmüller Theory (IUT) (Mochizuki, 2012–2020) is a vast extension of anabelian geometry that aims to reconstruct the ring structure of a number field from its étale fundamental group. The goal: prove the abc conjecture — for any ε > 0, there are only finitely many coprime (a,b,c) with a + b = c and rad(abc) > c^{1+ε} (where rad is the product of distinct prime factors). The IUT framework uses: Hodge theaters (copies of the number field equipped with different Frobenius structures), the Θ-link (a linking of Hodge theaters via theta functions and Gaussian monoids), and the log-theta lattice (a two-dimensional lattice of Hodge theaters linked by Θ and log-links). The key inequality (the Mochizuki inequality): vol(q) < log(vol(Θ)), which translates to the abc inequality: log(c) < (1 + ε)·log(rad(abc)) + C. This would prove the abc conjecture. Controversy: the IUT proof has been contested due to its extreme complexity (over 500 pages) and lack of acceptance by the number theory community. The Scholze-Stix note (2018) claimed to find a flaw in IUT III — specifically in Corollary 3.12, the key technical result. Mochizuki claimed the criticism was a misunderstanding. The 2020 Oxford workshop on IUT and the 2021 EMS report offered some clarification but not full consensus. In 2022, Mochizuki's papers were published in the RIMS journal (of which he is the editor-in-chief), raising concerns about editorial independence. The Dupuy-Hilado (2023) verification: a partial formalization of IUT in a proof assistant (Lean) is underway to resolve the controversy definitively.
IUT is the most ambitious development in anabelian geometry — aiming to prove the abc conjecture through a radical reconstruction of number fields from their fundamental groups, but remains deeply controversial.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller Group and the Outer Galois Action on the Projective Line Minus Three Points

The absolute Galois group G_ℚ acts on the étale fundamental group of the projective line minus three points, π₁(ℙ¹ℚ̄ {0,1,∞}), through outer automorphisms. This action factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT, a profinite group defined by Drinfeld (1990) via explicit equations in the profinite completion of the pure braid group on three strands. The group GT sits in an exact sequence 1 → GT → Out(π₁(ℙ¹ℚ̄ {0,1,∞})) → ℤ̂^× → 1, where the map to ℤ̂^× is the cyclotomic character. The inclusion G_ℚ GT is conjectured to be an isomorphism — this is the Grothendieck–Teichmüller conjecture, a profound strengthening of the anabelian philosophy for the thrice-punctured sphere. The defining equations for GT involve two elements λ ∈ ℤ̂^× and f ∈ F̂₂' (the derived subgroup of the free profinite group on two generators) satisfying the pentagon and hexagon relations: f(x,y)f(y,x)=1, f(x,y)x^λf(y,z)y^λf(z,x)z^λ=1, and f(x,y)f(y,z)f(z,x)=1 where xyz=1. These relations encode the associativity and commutativity constraints of a braided tensor category. The cyclotomic character χ: G_ℚ → ℤ̂^× gives the λ-component; the f-component corresponds to the “associator” Φ_KZ of Drinfeld, constructed via the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov equation. Ihara (1990) showed that the image of G_ℚ in GT lies in the subgroup GT₁ where λ=1, and that the map G_ℚ → GT₁ is injective. The surjectivity remains open. The GT group also acts on the set of dessins d’enfants (Belyi maps), providing a combinatorial handle on the Galois action. The Lie algebra of GT, the Grothendieck–Teichmüller Lie algebra grt, was computed by Drinfeld and later by Willwacher (2015) in connection with the deformation quantization of Poisson manifolds. The connection to multiple zeta values arises because the coefficients of the KZ associator are multiple zeta values, and the action of GT on them encodes the algebraic relations among these periods. The anabelian reconstruction of ℙ¹ {0,1,∞} from its fundamental group with Galois action is equivalent to determining the image of G_ℚ in GT. This is the “profinite” side of the story; the “discrete” side involves the action of the mapping class group of the punctured sphere on the fundamental group, and the compatibility between the two is governed by the theory of mapping class groups of profinite completions.

Belyi’s Theorem, Dessins d’Enfants, and the Combinatorial Anabelian Program

Belyi’s theorem (1979) states that a smooth projective curve X over ℂ can be defined over a number field if and only if there exists a finite morphism β: X → ℙ¹ ramified only over {0,1,∞}. Such a morphism is called a Belyi map. The preimage β⁻¹([0,1]) ⊂ X(ℂ) is a bipartite graph embedded on the Riemann surface X(ℂ), with black vertices at β⁻¹(0), white vertices at β⁻¹(1), and edges the preimages of the interval [0,1]. This graph is a dessin d’enfant. The monodromy of the covering β gives a permutation representation of the fundamental group π₁(ℙ¹(ℂ) {0,1,∞}) ≅ F₂ (free group on two generators) on the fiber β⁻¹(t) for a basepoint t ∈ (0,1). The two generators correspond to loops around 0 and 1; their images are permutations σ₀, σ₁ whose product σ_∞ = (σ₀σ₁)⁻¹ corresponds to the loop around ∞. The triple (σ₀, σ₁, σ_∞) up to simultaneous conjugation determines the dessin and the Belyi map up to isomorphism. The absolute Galois group G_ℚ acts on the set of dessins by acting on the coefficients of the defining equations of X and β. This action is faithful (Belyi, 1979), giving a concrete combinatorial realization of the otherwise mysterious group G_ℚ. The anabelian philosophy predicts that the curve X over a number field K can be reconstructed from its étale fundamental group π₁(X) with the G_K-action. For hyperbolic curves, the Grothendieck conjecture (proved by Mochizuki, 1996) confirms this. Dessins provide a “discrete” approximation: the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the dessin (as a graph) is related to the étale fundamental group of the curve. The theory of pseudo-Galois covers (Pop, 1990s) extends this to the birational setting: a function field over a number field can be reconstructed from its absolute Galois group. The combinatorial anabelian program seeks to make the reconstruction effective. For example, the “dessins d’enfants” approach to the section conjecture: a rational point x ∈ X(K) gives a section G_K → π₁(X), which in turn gives a G_K-equivariant map from the profinite completion of the dessin’s fundamental group to π₁(X). The work of Schneps, Lochak, and others on the “Grothendieck–Teichmüller tower” builds a tower of moduli spaces of curves with level structures, whose fundamental groups approximate the absolute Galois group. The “profinite braid group” B̂ₙ and its action on the free profinite group F̂ₙ are central. The tower of moduli spaces Mg,n (moduli of curves of genus g with n marked points) has fundamental groups that are mapping class groups; their profinite completions map to Out(π₁(XK̄)). The anabelian conjecture for Mg,n (proved in parts by Mochizuki, Tamagawa, and others) says that these moduli spaces are also anabelian. The combinatorial data of ribbon graphs (fat graphs) provides a cell decomposition of Mg,n (Harer, 1986), linking the topology of moduli space to the combinatorics of graphs, and hence to dessins. The “Grothendieck–Teichmüller group” GT acts on the whole tower, and the image of G_ℚ in GT is the “arithmetic” part of the mapping class group action.

Worked Example: Galois Action on a Dessin
Consider the Belyi map β: ℙ¹ → ℙ¹ given by β(z) = zⁿ. The ramification is over {0,1,∞} with β⁻¹(0) = {0}, β⁻¹(∞) = {∞}, and β⁻¹(1) = {ζₙᵏ : k=0,…,n-1} where ζₙ = e2πi/n. The dessin is a star with n edges from 0 to the n-th roots of unity. The monodromy permutations are σ₀ = (1 2 … n) (an n-cycle), σ₁ = identity, σ_∞ = (n … 2 1). Let σ ∈ G_ℚ act on the coefficients of β. Since β is defined over ℚ, the coefficients are rational, so σ acts trivially on the equation. However, σ acts on the dessin by sending the roots of unity ζₙ to ζₙχ(σ) where χ: G_ℚ → ℤ̂^× is the cyclotomic character. The resulting dessin is isomorphic to the original one (it is the same star graph), but the labeling of edges is permuted by the action of χ(σ) on ℤ/nℤ. The monodromy triple becomes (σ₀χ(σ), σ₁, σ_∞χ(σ)). This illustrates that the Galois action on dessins factors through the cyclotomic character on this simple example. For a general dessin defined over a number field K, the action of G_K is non-trivial and encodes the arithmetic of K.
The Belyi map β(z)=zⁿ has coefficients in ℚ, so the absolute Galois group G_ℚ acts trivially on the equation. The dessin is the star graph with n edges. The fiber over a basepoint t∈(0,1) consists of the n points t1/nζₙᵏ. The monodromy around 0 permutes these cyclically: σ₀ = (1 2 … n). The monodromy around 1 is trivial because β is unramified over 1 except at the n points mapping to 1, but the loop around 1 lifts to n disjoint loops, so σ₁ = id. The monodromy around ∞ is σ_∞ = (σ₀σ₁)⁻¹ = σ₀⁻¹ = (n … 2 1). For σ ∈ G_ℚ, the action on the dessin is induced by the action on the roots of unity: σ(ζₙ) = ζₙχ(σ) where χ(σ) ∈ (ℤ/nℤ)^×. This permutes the edges of the star by multiplication by χ(σ) on the indices k. The new monodromy triple is (σ₀χ(σ), id, σ_∞χ(σ)). Since σ₀χ(σ) is conjugate to σ₀ in Sₙ (both are n-cycles), the dessin is isomorphic to the original one. The Galois action is detected by the change in the labeling, which corresponds to the outer automorphism of the fundamental group induced by the cyclotomic character. This matches the general theory: the map G_ℚ → GT sends σ to (χ(σ), f_σ) where f_σ is trivial in this abelian example.
Exam Strategy: Anabelian Reconstruction Steps
When a question asks “how does one reconstruct a curve from its fundamental group?”, structure the answer in layers: (1) the étale fundamental group π₁(X) fits in the exact sequence with G_K; (2) the outer Galois action G_K → Out(π₁(XK̄)) is the key invariant; (3) for hyperbolic curves over number fields, Mochizuki’s theorem says this action determines X up to isomorphism; (4) the reconstruction uses p-adic Hodge theory (Hodge–Tate decomposition, indigenous bundles) to recover the curve’s p-adic uniformization; (5) the combinatorial shadow is the action on dessins / the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group. Mentioning the exact sequence, the outer action, and the role of p-adic Hodge theory covers the core mechanism.

Anabelian Geometry for Higher-Dimensional Varieties: Abelian Varieties and Hyperbolic Varieties

The anabelian philosophy was originally formulated for curves, but Grothendieck envisioned it for higher-dimensional varieties. The central question: for which varieties X over a number field K does the étale fundamental group π₁(X) with its G_K-action determine X up to isomorphism? For abelian varieties, the answer is negative in general: the étale fundamental group of an abelian variety A is the Tate module T_ℓ(A) (for ℓ≠char(K)), which is a free ℤ_ℓ-module of rank 2g with a G_K-action. The Tate conjecture (proved by Faltings, 1983) says that the G_K-module T_ℓ(A) determines A up to isogeny, not isomorphism. Two non-isogenous abelian varieties can have isomorphic Tate modules as G_K-modules if they are isogenous. The anabelian conjecture fails for abelian varieties because their fundamental groups are abelian (commutative), losing the non-abelian structure that encodes the geometry. For hyperbolic varieties (varieties whose universal cover is a bounded symmetric domain, e.g., Shimura varieties, moduli spaces of curves), the situation is more promising. Mochizuki (1999) proved the anabelian conjecture for certain hyperbolic curves over p-adic fields, and Tamagawa (1997) proved it for affine hyperbolic curves over number fields. For higher-dimensional hyperbolic varieties, the “Grothendieck conjecture” is known in some cases: Mochizuki (2000s) proved it for the moduli space Mg,n of curves of genus g with n marked points when 2g-2+n > 0 (the hyperbolic case), over number fields and p-adic fields. The proof uses the theory of “indigenous bundles” (a higher-dimensional generalization of the projective structures used for curves) and p-adic Teichmüller theory. The key idea is that the étale fundamental group of a hyperbolic variety carries a “Hodge-theoretic” structure (a filtration on the associated graded of the lower central series) that can be recovered from the group structure alone, and this Hodge structure determines the variety via the period map. For Shimura varieties, the anabelian conjecture is related to the André–Oort conjecture and the theory of special subvarieties. The fundamental group of a Shimura variety is an arithmetic group, and the Galois action on it is described by the Langlands program. The “anabelian” reconstruction of a Shimura variety from its fundamental group would imply that the variety is determined by its arithmetic fundamental group, which is a strong form of the “modularity” philosophy. For general varieties of general type, the anabelian conjecture is wide open. The “birational anabelian geometry” program (Pop, Bogomolov, Tschinkel) asks whether the function field of a variety over a number field can be reconstructed from its absolute Galois group. This is known for function fields of curves (the “birational section conjecture” of Koenigsmann and Pop) and for some higher-dimensional cases (e.g., varieties with ample cotangent bundle). The obstruction is the “abelianization” of the fundamental group: the maximal abelian quotient is the Galois group of the maximal abelian extension, which by class field theory is determined by the idele class group. The non-abelian part is what carries the geometric information.

The p-adic Section Conjecture and p-adic Uniformization

The section conjecture has a p-adic analogue: let K be a finite extension of ℚ_p, and let X be a smooth hyperbolic curve over K. The étale fundamental group sequence 1 → π₁(XK̄) → π₁(X) → G_K → 1 splits at a rational point x ∈ X(K). The p-adic section conjecture asserts that the map X(K) → {sections G_K → π₁(X) modulo π₁(XK̄)-conjugation} is a bijection. This is known in some cases: for the projective line minus three points ℙ¹ {0,1,∞} over ℚ_p, the conjecture is related to the theory of p-adic multiple zeta values and the p-adic KZ equation. The work of Hoshi (2010s) and Mochizuki (1990s) on the “p-adic Grothendieck conjecture” uses p-adic Hodge theory to reconstruct the curve from its fundamental group. The p-adic uniformization of a hyperbolic curve X over K is given by the Mumford curve construction: if X has split semistable reduction, its p-adic universal cover is the Drinfeld upper half-plane Ω = ℙ¹ℂ_p ℙ¹(K), and X(ℂ_p) ≅ Γ Ω for a Schottky group Γ ⊂ PGL₂(K). The étale fundamental group π₁(X) is an extension of G_K by the profinite completion of Γ. The p-adic section conjecture then translates to a statement about sections of the extension 1 → Γ̂ → π₁(X) → G_K → 1. The “exotic maps” on the boundary of the fundamental group (the “cuspidal” sections) are related to the action of G_K on the set of cusps (the boundary components of the Mumford curve). The theory of “p-adic Teichmüller spaces” (Mochizuki) provides a moduli space of p-adic curves with level structures, whose fundamental group maps to the outer automorphism group of π₁(XK̄). The p-adic section conjecture is also connected to the “p-adic Belyi maps” and the action of G_K on the p-adic dessin d’enfants. The “p-adic Grothendieck–Teichmüller group” GT_p acts on the p-adic fundamental group of ℙ¹ {0,1,∞}, and the image of G_K in GT_p is conjectured to be the whole group (the p-adic Grothendieck–Teichmüller conjecture). The work of Nakamura (1990s) on the “p-adic braid group” and the “p-adic KZ equation” gives a p-adic analogue of the Drinfeld associator. The p-adic section conjecture for ℙ¹ {0,1,∞} over ℚ_p is equivalent to the statement that every section of the p-adic KZ equation comes from a rational point (i.e., a tangential basepoint at 0, 1, or ∞). This is known for “tangential” sections but open for “exotic” sections. The “p-adic anabelian geometry” of Mochizuki also includes the theory of “Frobenius structures” on the fundamental group, which are used in IUT to construct the Θ-link.

Birational Anabelian Geometry and the Function Field Case

Birational anabelian geometry replaces the variety X with its function field K(X). The absolute Galois group GK(X) is the étale fundamental group of the generic point. The question: does GK(X) determine K(X) up to isomorphism? For curves over algebraically closed fields, this is false (the fundamental group of the generic point is trivial). Over number fields, the situation is richer. The “birational section conjecture” (Koenigsmann, Pop, 2000s) states that for a function field F of transcendence degree 1 over a number field K, the set of K-rational points of the corresponding curve is in bijection with the set of sections of the exact sequence 1 → GF̄ → G_F → G_K → 1, where F̄ is the algebraic closure of F in a separable closure. This was proved by Koenigsmann (2005) and Pop (2000s) using the theory of “pseudo-Galois covers” and “projective structures” on the absolute Galois group. The key idea is that a rational point gives a valuation on F, and the decomposition group of that valuation in G_F gives a section. Conversely, a section gives a decomposition group, which corresponds to a valuation, and the valuation theory of function fields (the “Riemann–Roch” theorem for valuations) recovers the point. For higher transcendence degree, the “birational anabelian conjecture” (Bogomolov, Tschinkel, Pop) asks whether the function field of a variety of general type over a number field can be reconstructed from its absolute Galois group. This is known for varieties with ample cotangent bundle (Pop, 2010s) and for some rationally connected varieties. The “Kuyk–Mochizuki” theorem says that the absolute Galois group of a number field determines the number field (Neukirch–Uchida). For function fields, the absolute Galois group determines the function field up to “isomorphism of Galois extensions” — but there are counterexamples (Uchida, 1970s) where non-isomorphic function fields have isomorphic absolute Galois groups. The “anabelian” condition for function fields is that the field is “anabelian” in the sense of Pop: it has no non-trivial abelian Galois extensions that are “geometric” (i.e., come from the base field). The “Grothendieck conjecture for function fields” (Mochizuki, 1990s) is the statement that the isomorphism class of a hyperbolic curve over a number field is determined by the isomorphism class of its fundamental group as a G_K-module. This is the “geometric” anabelian conjecture, distinct from the birational one. The birational version is often easier because one can use valuation theory and the theory of “pseudo-Galois covers” to reconstruct the field from its Galois group. The “section conjecture” for function fields (the birational section conjecture) is a theorem, while the geometric section conjecture for curves over number fields remains open. The connection is via the “specialization” map: a rational point on a curve over a number field gives a section of the fundamental group exact sequence, which specializes to a section of the function field exact sequence. The “Mordell–Weil” theorem for function fields (Lang–Néron) gives finiteness of rational points, which is used in the proof of the birational section conjecture.

  • Confusing the geometric anabelian conjecture (curves over number fields) with the birational anabelian conjecture (function fields) — they have different statements and different proof statuses.
  • Assuming the section conjecture is proved for all hyperbolic curves over number fields; it is open in general, known only for special cases (local fields, certain genus 2 curves, birational version).
  • Thinking the Neukirch–Uchida theorem applies to function fields; it applies only to number fields (and more generally to finitely generated extensions of ℚ).
  • Believing the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT is known to equal G_ℚ; the inclusion G_ℚ GT is injective but surjectivity is the open Grothendieck–Teichmüller conjecture.
  • Overlooking that abelian varieties are not anabelian — their fundamental groups are abelian (Tate modules), so the anabelian philosophy fails for them.
  • Confusing the p-adic section conjecture (for curves over p-adic fields) with the global section conjecture (over number fields); they are distinct statements with different techniques (p-adic Hodge theory vs. global Diophantine geometry).
  • Assuming IUT (Inter-Universal Teichmüller Theory) is an accepted proof of the abc conjecture; it remains controversial and not widely accepted by the community.

The Étale Homotopy Type and the Anabelian Reconstruction of Curves

The étale homotopy type, introduced by Artin and Mazur and later refined by Friedlander, provides a pro‑homotopy invariant that captures the full étale fundamental group together with its higher homotopy groups. For a smooth, geometrically connected variety X over a field k, the étale homotopy type Ét(X) is a pro‑simplicial set whose fundamental group is the étale fundamental group π₁^ét(X, x̄). In anabelian geometry the crucial observation is that for hyperbolic curves over number fields or p‑adic fields the étale homotopy type is “anabelian”: it determines the curve up to isomorphism. Mochizuki proved that the outer action of the absolute Galois group G_k on π₁^ét(X) extends to an action on the whole étale homotopy type, and that this enriched structure suffices to recover the isomorphism class of X. The reconstruction proceeds by first recovering the set of decomposition groups of closed points from the outer Galois action on π₁^ét(X), then using the higher homotopy groups to rigidify the gluing data. A key technical tool is the “pro‑ℓ” completion for a prime ℓ different from the characteristic, which reduces the problem to a pro‑ℓ homotopy type where the obstruction theory becomes more tractable. The theorem has been extended to certain higher‑dimensional varieties with ample cotangent bundle by work of Pop and others, but the curve case remains the most complete. Examiners often test the distinction between the étale fundamental group alone (which is insufficient for reconstruction in general) and the full étale homotopy type (which is sufficient for hyperbolic curves).

Mochizuki’s Theory of Frobenioids and Its Role in Anabelian Geometry

Frobenioids are categorical structures invented by Mochizuki to encode the multiplicative and additive arithmetic of number fields and function fields in a unified way. A Frobenioid consists of a category equipped with a divisor monoid, a degree map, and a Frobenius endomorphism that mimics the action of the absolute Frobenius in positive characteristic. In the anabelian context, the fundamental group of a hyperbolic curve over a number field acts on the associated Frobenioid, and this action retains enough information to reconstruct the curve. The theory replaces the classical reliance on Galois cohomology with a more flexible “mono‑analytic” framework where the Frobenius morphism plays the role of a “theta” operator. Mochizuki’s “étale theta function” lives in the Frobenioid attached to the universal covering of the curve, and its transformation properties under the Galois action give rise to the “theta” and “log‑theta” lattices that appear in Inter‑Universal Teichmüller Theory. For exam purposes it is essential to know that Frobenioids provide a categorical language in which the anabelian reconstruction theorems can be stated without reference to a specific model of the curve, and that the “Frobenius” in a Frobenioid is not the usual Frobenius endomorphism of a scheme in characteristic p but an abstract operator satisfying certain axioms. The main anabelian result using Frobenioids is that the isomorphism class of a hyperbolic curve over a finitely generated extension of ℚ is determined by the isomorphism class of its “tempered fundamental group” together with the induced Frobenioid structure. This statement is strictly stronger than the classical Grothendieck conjecture because it also recovers the field of definition.

Worked Example: Recovering a Curve from its Tempered Fundamental Group
Let X be a hyperbolic curve over a number field K. Suppose you are given the tempered fundamental group π₁^temp(X) as a profinite group equipped with the outer action of G_K and the associated Frobenioid structure. Outline the steps to reconstruct X up to isomorphism.
  1. Extract the geometric fundamental group π₁^geom(X) as the kernel of the projection π₁^temp(X) → G_K.
  2. Identify the set of cuspidal decomposition groups inside π₁^geom(X) using the Frobenioid divisor monoid: they correspond to the “cuspidal” Frobenius elements.
  3. From the configuration of cuspidal decomposition groups recover the dual graph of the stable reduction of X over the ring of integers of K.
  4. Use the outer G_K‑action on the tempered fundamental group to recover the field of moduli of X; the Frobenioid degree map yields the canonical height pairing, which determines the Jacobian of X up to isogeny.
  5. Apply Mochizuki’s anabelian reconstruction theorem for tempered fundamental groups (which incorporates the Frobenioid structure) to conclude that X is uniquely determined up to K‑isomorphism.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the role of Frobenioids, focus on three points: (1) they encode both additive and multiplicative arithmetic, (2) they carry an abstract Frobenius operator that replaces the usual Frobenius in characteristic p, and (3) the anabelian reconstruction theorem is formulated in terms of the tempered fundamental group plus Frobenioid structure. Do not confuse Frobenioids with the classical Frobenius morphism on schemes.

The Anabelian Conjecture for Hyperbolic Curves over p‑adic Fields

Over a p‑adic field K (a finite extension of ℚ_p) the anabelian conjecture for hyperbolic curves asserts that the isomorphism class of a smooth, geometrically connected curve X of genus g with n punctures such that 2g‑2+n > 0 is determined by the isomorphism class of its étale fundamental group π₁^ét(X) as a topological group equipped with the outer action of G_K. This is the p‑adic analogue of Grothendieck’s conjecture over number fields. The conjecture is known in several important cases: for curves with good reduction (Tamagawa), for curves with semistable reduction (Mochizuki), and for all hyperbolic curves over p‑adic fields under the additional hypothesis that the curve admits a “p‑adic uniformization” by a Mumford curve (the “p‑adic uniformization theorem” of Mochizuki). The proof uses p‑adic Hodge theory to compare the étale fundamental group with the de Rham fundamental group, and the theory of “log‑crystalline” cohomology to control the monodromy operator. A crucial ingredient is the “p‑adic section conjecture”, which predicts a bijection between K‑rational points of X and conjugacy classes of sections of the exact sequence 1 → π₁^ét(X̄) → π₁^ét(X) → G_K → 1. While the global section conjecture over number fields remains open, the p‑adic version has been proved for certain genus‑2 curves and for curves with a rational point of bad reduction. Examiners frequently test the distinction between the “geometric” anabelian conjecture (reconstruction of the curve from its fundamental group) and the “section” conjecture (reconstruction of rational points from sections), and they may ask why the p‑adic case is more accessible (availability of p‑adic Hodge theory and the existence of a well‑behaved period map).

Galois Representations and the Anabelian Philosophy: The Tate Module and Beyond

The anabelian philosophy can be viewed as a vast generalization of the classical theory of Galois representations attached to abelian varieties. For an abelian variety A over a number field K, the Tate module T_ℓ(A) is a free ℤ_ℓ‑module of rank 2 dim A equipped with a continuous G_K‑action. The Tate conjecture (proved for abelian varieties over number fields by Faltings) states that the ℓ‑adic representation determines A up to isogeny. In anabelian geometry one replaces the abelian fundamental group (the Tate module) by the full non‑abelian étale fundamental group π₁^ét(X). The “non‑abelian Tate conjecture” would assert that the outer G_K‑action on π₁^ét(X) determines X up to isomorphism. This is precisely the Grothendieck conjecture for hyperbolic curves. The analogy extends to the “section conjecture”: a rational point on an abelian variety gives a splitting of the Kummer exact sequence 0 → A(K) ⊗ ℤ_ℓ → H¹(G_K, T_ℓ(A)) → …, while a rational point on a hyperbolic curve gives a section of the fundamental group exact sequence. The anabelian program thus seeks to replace linear Galois representations by non‑abelian ones. A key technical difference is that the category of ℓ‑adic representations is Tannakian, allowing reconstruction via tensor categories, whereas the category of π₁^ét(X)‑sets is not Tannakian; instead one uses the “pro‑ℓ” completion and the theory of “outer Galois actions” on pro‑ℓ groups. Recent work of Esnault–Hai and others has developed a “non‑abelian Hodge theory” that parallels the classical Simpson correspondence, providing a bridge between π₁^ét(X) and Higgs bundles. For examinations, remember that the Tate module is the abelianization of π₁^ét(A) and that the anabelian conjectures are strictly stronger than the Tate conjecture because they retain the full non‑abelian structure.

Specialization Maps and the Comparison Between Global and Local Section Conjectures

Specialization maps provide the primary tool for relating the global section conjecture over a number field K to the local section conjectures over the completions K_v. Given a hyperbolic curve X/K and a rational point x ∈ X(K), one obtains a section s_x: G_K → π₁^ét(X). For each place v of K, the inclusion GK_v → G_K induces a “specialized” section sx,v: GK_v → π₁^ét(XK_v). The collection of local sections satisfies compatibility conditions coming from the global reciprocity law. Conversely, a family of local sections that satisfies these compatibilities is expected to come from a global rational point. This principle underlies the “local‑global principle” for sections, which is known to hold for curves of genus 0 and 1 (where it reduces to the Hasse principle for rational points) but is open in general. The work of Stix, Harari, and others has shown that the obstruction to lifting a compatible family of local sections to a global section lies in a certain “Selmer set” attached to the non‑abelian cohomology of π₁^ét(X). In the p‑adic setting, the specialization map is closely related to the p‑adic period map and the theory of “p‑adic uniformization”. A deep result of Mochizuki states that for a hyperbolic curve over a p‑adic field, the specialization map from the set of sections of the tempered fundamental group to the set of sections of the étale fundamental group is injective. This injectivity is a key step in proving the p‑adic section conjecture for certain curves. For exam questions, be prepared to explain how the global‑to‑local map on sections is defined, why the kernel of this map measures the failure of the section conjecture, and how the “Selmer set” formalism generalizes the classical Selmer group of an abelian variety.

  • Confusing the étale homotopy type with the étale fundamental group alone — the higher homotopy groups are essential for reconstruction in many cases.
  • Thinking Frobenioids are just a reformulation of the Frobenius morphism in characteristic p; they are abstract categorical structures with an axiomatic Frobenius operator.
  • Assuming the p‑adic anabelian conjecture is proved for all hyperbolic curves; it is known only under extra hypotheses such as semistable reduction or Mumford uniformization.
  • Equating the non‑abelian Tate conjecture with the classical Tate conjecture for abelian varieties; the former concerns the full fundamental group, not its abelianization.
  • Believing the local‑global principle for sections holds in general; it is a deep open problem and known only for low genus.
  • Overlooking that the specialization map on sections is defined via the functoriality of the fundamental group exact sequence with respect to field extensions.
  • Neglecting the role of the Selmer set in measuring the obstruction to lifting local sections to a global section.

These advanced topics form the core of modern anabelian geometry and are essential for a deep understanding of the field.

Non‑abelian Cohomology, Selmer Varieties, and the Obstruction to Rational Points

The classical Selmer group of an abelian variety A over a number field K sits inside the Galois cohomology group H¹(G_K, A) and measures the failure of the local‑global principle for rational points. In anabelian geometry the same philosophy is transferred to the non‑abelian setting by replacing A with the étale fundamental group π₁^ét(X) of a hyperbolic curve X/K. The pointed set of continuous sections of the exact sequence 1 → π₁^ét(Xbar K) → π₁^ét(X) → G_K → 1 is denoted Sect(X/K). For each place v of K one obtains a local section set Sect(XK_v/K_v) via the specialization map induced by the inclusion GK_v → G_K. The collection of local sections that are compatible under the global reciprocity law forms a Selmer variety Sel(X/K), a finite‑type algebraic variety over ℚ_ℓ (or ℤ_ℓ) constructed by Stix using non‑abelian cohomology of the pro‑ℓ completion of π₁^ét(X). The natural map Sect(X/K) → Sel(X/K) is injective, and its image is precisely the set of global sections that survive all local conditions. The obstruction to lifting a compatible family of local sections to a global rational point is therefore measured by the quotient Sel(X/K) / Sect(X/K). When X has genus 0 or 1 this quotient is trivial, reflecting the Hasse principle for conics and elliptic curves. For higher genus the quotient is expected to be non‑trivial in general; explicit computations by Harari–Stix for certain modular curves show non‑empty Selmer varieties with no global sections, providing concrete counter‑examples to a naïve local‑global principle for sections. The construction of Selmer varieties uses the “non‑abelian Kummer map” x ↦ s_x, where s_x is the section associated to a rational point x ∈ X(K). The map factors through the pro‑ℓ completion π₁^ét(X)(ℓ) and lands in the non‑abelian cohomology set H¹(G_K, π₁^ét(X)(ℓ)). The latter is not a group but a pointed set equipped with a natural topology; Sel(X/K) is defined as the subset of classes that are locally in the image of the local Kummer maps for all v. This formalism generalises the classical Selmer group of an abelian variety, where π₁^ét(A) is abelian and the cohomology sets are groups. For examinations, remember that the Selmer variety is a finite‑type ℚ_ℓ‑variety, that the global‑to‑local map on sections is defined by functoriality of the fundamental group exact sequence, and that the failure of the section conjecture is exactly the non‑surjectivity of Sect(X/K) → Sel(X/K).

The p‑adic Local Langlands Correspondence and Its Anabelian Implications

The p‑adic local Langlands correspondence, pioneered by Colmez, Berger, Breuil, and others, establishes a bijection between certain irreducible admissible representations of GL₂(ℚ_p) and two‑dimensional p‑adic Galois representations of Gℚ_p. In the anabelian context this correspondence interacts with the p‑adic section conjecture through the p‑adic étale fundamental group of a hyperbolic curve over a p‑adic field. Let X be a smooth, proper, geometrically connected curve of genus g ≥ 2 over a finite extension K/ℚ_p with good reduction. The p‑adic étale fundamental group π₁^ét(X) carries a continuous outer action of G_K. The p‑adic section conjecture predicts that the set of sections of the exact sequence 1 → π₁^ét(Xbar K) → π₁^ét(X) → G_K → 1 is in bijection with X(K). Recent work of Mochizuki, using his theory of Frobenioids, and of Andreatta–Iovita–Kim, using p‑adic Hodge theory, has shown that the p‑adic period map from X(K) to the de Rham fundamental groupoid identifies rational points with certain “de Rham sections”. These de Rham sections correspond, via the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence, to specific (φ, Γ)‑modules attached to the Galois representation on the pro‑p completion of π₁^ét(X). In particular, the (φ, Γ)‑module associated to the Tate module of the Jacobian J_X appears as the abelianisation of the non‑abelian (φ, Γ)‑module governing π₁^ét(X). The correspondence therefore provides a bridge between the non‑abelian Galois action on π₁^ét(X) and the representation theory of GL₂(ℚ_p), allowing one to translate questions about sections into questions about the existence of certain invariant vectors in locally analytic representations. A concrete consequence is the proof of the p‑adic section conjecture for curves with semistable reduction whose Jacobian has potentially multiplicative reduction, where the relevant (φ, Γ)‑modules are trianguline and the local Langlands correspondence is fully understood. For exam purposes, the key points are: (1) the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence relates GL₂(ℚ_p)‑representations to 2‑dimensional Gℚ_p‑representations; (2) the pro‑p completion of π₁^ét(X) yields a non‑abelian (φ, Γ)‑module; (3) de Rham sections correspond to rational points via the p‑adic period map; (4) the conjecture is known in the semistable, potentially multiplicative case.

Worked Example: Computing a Local Selmer Condition
Let X/ℚ_p be a hyperelliptic curve of genus 2 with good reduction, and let ℓ ≠ p be a prime. Describe the local condition at p defining the Selmer variety Sel(X/ℚ_p) inside H¹(Gℚ_p, π₁^ét(X)(ℓ)).
Because ℓ ≠ p, the ℓ‑adic representation of Gℚ_p on π₁^ét(X)(ℓ) is unramified. The local condition is the subgroup of classes that become trivial after restriction to the inertia subgroup I_p. Equivalently, a class in H¹(Gℚ_p, π₁^ét(X)(ℓ)) lies in the local Selmer condition iff its image under the inflation map from H¹(Gℚ_p/I_p, π₁^ét(X)(ℓ)) is zero. Since Gℚ_p/I_p ≅ ℤ is topologically generated by Frobenius, this condition means the class is fixed by Frobenius. In practice one computes the fixed subspace of the Frobenius action on the pro‑ℓ completion of the geometric fundamental group; the Selmer variety is the subvariety of H¹ cut out by these linear equations.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the definition of a Selmer variety in the anabelian setting, write down the exact sequence of fundamental groups, the Kummer map, and the local conditions as fixed‑by‑Frobenius subspaces. Examiners award marks for naming the non‑abelian Kummer map and for distinguishing the ℓ ≠ p case (unramified) from the ℓ = p case (de Rham condition).

Anabelian Geometry of Moduli Spaces of Curves

The moduli stack Mg,n of smooth curves of genus g with n marked points carries a natural étale fundamental group π₁^ét(Mg,n) that fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁^ét(Mg,n,bar K) → π₁^ét(Mg,n) → G_K → 1. The geometric fundamental group π₁^ét(Mg,n,bar K) is isomorphic to the mapping class group Γg,n profinitely completed. The outer Galois action on this profinite mapping class group is the arithmetic analogue of the classical action of the absolute Galois group on the Teichmüller tower. Grothendieck’s anabelian philosophy predicts that the isomorphism class of the stack Mg,n over a number field K is determined by the outer G_K‑action on π₁^ét(Mg,n,bar K). This is the “moduli space anabelian conjecture”. Significant progress has been made by Nakamura, Tamagawa, and Mochizuki: for g = 0, n ≥ 4 the conjecture follows from Belyi’s theorem and the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group; for g = 1, n ≥ 1 it reduces to the classical theory of elliptic curves and the Tate module; for g ≥ 2 the conjecture is open in general but known for certain “hyperbolic” components of the moduli space where the curves have no extra automorphisms. The key technical tool is the “pro‑ℓ” version of the mapping class group, where one replaces the profinite completion by the pro‑ℓ completion for a fixed prime ℓ. The outer Galois action on the pro‑ℓ mapping class group factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT_ℓ, and the anabelian conjecture for Mg,n is equivalent to the injectivity of the natural map GT_ℓ → Out(π₁^ét(Mg,n,bar K)(ℓ)). Recent work of Hain and Matsumoto has identified the Lie algebra of the pro‑ℓ mapping class group with the derived series of the free Lie algebra on 2g generators, providing a concrete description of the Galois action in terms of multiple zeta values. For examinations, remember that the geometric fundamental group of Mg,n is the profinite mapping class group, that the anabelian conjecture for moduli spaces is a higher‑dimensional analogue of the curve case, and that the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group controls the pro‑ℓ outer action.

Comparison Isomorphisms Between Étale and de Rham Fundamental Groups in p‑adic Hodge Theory

In p‑adic Hodge theory the étale fundamental group π₁^ét(X) of a smooth proper variety X over a p‑adic field K is compared with the de Rham fundamental group π₁^dR(X) constructed from the category of unipotent vector bundles with integrable connection. The comparison isomorphism, due to Olsson and extended by Kim, is a G_K‑equivariant isomorphism between the pro‑ℓ completion of π₁^ét(X) (for ℓ = p) and the Malcev completion of π₁^dR(X) after tensoring with BdR. Concretely, for a hyperbolic curve X/K with good reduction, the p‑adic period map sends a rational point x ∈ X(K) to a “de Rham path” from the tangential basepoint at the cusp to x in the de Rham fundamental groupoid. This path corresponds under the comparison isomorphism to the étale section s_x : G_K → π₁^ét(X). The image of the period map lies in the “de Rham Selmer variety”, a subvariety of the de Rham fundamental groupoid cut out by the condition that the associated (φ, Γ)‑module is crystalline. The p‑adic section conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the period map induces a bijection X(K) ≅ SeldR(X/K). The comparison isomorphism also respects the weight filtration: the graded pieces of the lower central series of π₁^ét(X) correspond to the tensor powers of H¹ét(X, ℚ_p), while on the de Rham side they correspond to the tensor powers of H¹dR(X/K). This compatibility is the non‑abelian analogue of the classical comparison isomorphism for Tate modules of abelian varieties. For exam questions, be prepared to state the comparison isomorphism, explain the role of the period map, and describe how the weight filtration matches the lower central series on both sides.

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller Group Acting on Pro‑ℓ Mapping Class Groups

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT is a pro‑finite group introduced by Drinfeld to describe the symmetries of the tower of moduli spaces M0,n. Its pro‑ℓ completion GT_ℓ acts on the pro‑ℓ completion of the mapping class group Γg,n(ℓ) for all g, n. This action is constructed via the outer Galois action on the étale fundamental group of Mg,n: the exact sequence 1 → π₁^ét(Mg,n,bar K) → π₁^ét(Mg,n) → G_K → 1 yields a homomorphism G_K → Out(π₁^ét(Mg,n,bar K)). The image of this homomorphism is contained in GT, and after pro‑ℓ completion one obtains a continuous homomorphism GT_ℓ → Out(Γg,n(ℓ)). The kernel of this map is a central object of study; its triviality for all g, n would imply the anabelian conjecture for the whole Teichmüller tower. Hain’s computation of the Lie algebra of Γg,n(ℓ) shows that the action of GT_ℓ is given by the “Ihara action” on the free Lie algebra generated by Dehn twists. In particular, the element σ ∈ GT_ℓ corresponding to the cyclotomic character acts by scaling the standard symplectic form on H₁(Σg,n, ℤ_ℓ). The pro‑ℓ Grothendieck–Teichmüller group also appears in the theory of associators: Drinfeld’s associator ΦKZ provides a canonical element of GT(ℚ_ℓ) whose image in Out(Γg,n(ℓ)) coincides with the monodromy of the Knizhnik–Zamolodchikov connection. For examinations, the essential facts are: (1) GT_ℓ acts on the pro‑ℓ mapping class groups; (2) this action factors the outer Galois action on π₁^ét(Mg,n); (3) the kernel of GT_ℓ → Out(Γg,n(ℓ)) measures the failure of the moduli‑space anabelian conjecture; (4) the cyclotomic character gives a distinguished element acting by symplectic scaling.

  • Confusing the Selmer variety (a finite‑type ℚ_ℓ‑variety) with the classical Selmer group (a finite ℤ_ℓ‑module).
  • Assuming the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence gives a bijection for all p‑adic Galois representations; it is only known for two‑dimensional representations of Gℚ_p.
  • Thinking the anabelian conjecture for Mg,n is proved for all g, n; it is known only for low genus or under extra automorphism‑free hypotheses.
  • Neglecting the weight filtration when comparing étale and de Rham fundamental groups; the isomorphism respects the lower central series, not just the groups themselves.
  • Believing the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT is a finite group; it is pro‑finite and its pro‑ℓ completion is the relevant object for anabelian geometry.
  • Overlooking that the period map lands in a de Rham Selmer variety defined by crystallinity, not in the whole de Rham fundamental groupoid.
  • Equating the non‑abelian Kummer map with the abelian Kummer map for Jacobians; the former takes values in non‑abelian cohomology sets, not groups.

These advanced topics complete the core anabelian geometry syllabus and provide the technical background needed for research‑level problems.

The Anabelian Conjecture for Shimura Varieties and the Langlands Program

Shimura varieties provide a vast class of higher-dimensional algebraic varieties whose étale fundamental groups encode deep arithmetic information through the Langlands correspondence. The anabelian conjecture for Shimura varieties asserts that for a connected Shimura variety S = Sh(G, X) of Hodge type over a number field E (the reflex field), the isomorphism class of the étale fundamental group π₁ét(SĒ) as a topological group equipped with the continuous outer action of the absolute Galois group GE determines S up to isomorphism over Ē. This is known for Shimura curves (dimension 1) by work of Mochizuki and Tamagawa, and for certain higher-dimensional cases under the assumption of the Tate conjecture and the Mumford–Tate conjecture. The outer Galois action on π₁ét(SĒ) factors through the motivic Galois group of the Tannakian category of motives associated to S, and the Langlands program predicts that this action is described by the automorphic representations of the reductive group G. Concretely, for a Shimura variety of PEL type, the étale fundamental group fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(SĒ) → π₁ét(S) → GE → 1, and the section conjecture for S asks whether every section s : GE → π₁ét(S) arises from a rational point of S(E). The connection with the Langlands program is made precise via the Kottwitz conjecture (now a theorem of Kisin, Shin, and others): the ℓ-adic cohomology H*ét(SĒ, ℚℓ) decomposes as a direct sum over automorphic representations π of G(𝔸f) of π∞ ⊗ rπ(ℓ), where rπ(ℓ) is the associated Galois representation. The anabelian philosophy predicts that the full étale homotopy type of S (not just its fundamental group) is determined by the automorphic data. For exam purposes, the key points are: (1) Shimura varieties are the natural higher-dimensional testing ground for anabelian conjectures; (2) the outer Galois action on π₁ét is governed by the Langlands correspondence; (3) the section conjecture for Shimura varieties is open in general but known for Shimura curves; (4) the Kottwitz conjecture provides the bridge between automorphic forms and the Galois action on cohomology, which is the abelian shadow of the anabelian conjecture.

The Chabauty–Kim Method and Non‑abelian Chabauty: Anabelian Geometry Applied to Rational Points

The Chabauty–Kim method is the most successful practical application of anabelian geometry to the determination of rational points on curves. Let X be a smooth projective hyperbolic curve of genus g ≥ 2 over a number field K, and let p be a prime of good reduction. The classical Chabauty–Coleman method (1941, 1985) uses the p-adic integration of differential forms on the Jacobian J(X) to bound X(K) when the Mordell–Weil rank r < g. Kim’s non‑abelian generalization (2005, 2009) replaces the abelian Kummer map X(K) → H¹(GK, TpJ) with the non‑abelian Kummer map κ : X(K) → H¹(GK, π₁ét(XK̄, b)), where π₁ét is the p-adic étale fundamental group with a tangential basepoint b at a cusp. The target is the non‑abelian cohomology set H¹(GK, π₁ét), which classifies torsors under the pro‑p completion of π₁ét. Kim constructs a descending filtration of H¹ by Selmer varieties Seln(X/K) defined by local conditions at all places v of K: a class lies in Seln if its restriction to GKv comes from a Kv-rational point of the n-th nilpotent quotient of π₁ét. The p-adic period map j : X(Kp) → π₁dR(X/Kp) (the de Rham fundamental groupoid) lands in the de Rham Selmer variety SeldR(X/Kp), cut out by the condition that the associated (φ, Γ)-module is crystalline. The comparison isomorphism identifies Seln(X/K) ⊗ ℚp with SeldR(X/Kp)n. The fundamental theorem of the method: if the dimension of the p-adic Selmer variety SeldR(X/Kp) is strictly less than the dimension of X(Kp) (which is 1), then the image of X(K) under j is finite, and one can often determine X(K) explicitly. This has been carried out for many curves where r ≥ g, e.g., the modular curve X₀(67) (Balakrishnan–Dogra–Müller–Tuitman–Von Känel, 2019) and the split Cartan modular curve of level 13 (Balakrishnan–Dogra–Müller–Tuitman–Von Känel, 2021). For examinations, the essential structure is: (1) the non‑abelian Kummer map takes values in H¹(GK, π₁ét); (2) Selmer varieties impose local conditions; (3) the p-adic period map and comparison isomorphism translate the problem into p-adic Hodge theory; (4) the dimension comparison dim SeldR < 1 implies finiteness of rational points; (5) explicit computation of SeldR uses the theory of iterated integrals and the weight filtration on the de Rham fundamental group.

Worked Example: Dimension Count in Non‑abelian Chabauty
Let X be a hyperbolic curve of genus 3 over ℚ with good reduction at p = 5. Suppose the Mordell–Weil rank of its Jacobian is r = 3. The classical Chabauty condition r < g fails. Explain how the non‑abelian Chabauty method at level n = 2 can still prove finiteness of X(ℚ) by comparing the dimension of the de Rham Selmer variety SeldR(2)(X/ℚ5) with the dimension of X(ℚ5).
At level n = 2, the de Rham fundamental group π₁dR(X/ℚ5)(2) is a two-step nilpotent group whose Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on H¹dR(X) modulo the third term of the lower central series. The dimension of H¹dR(X) is 2g = 6. The second graded piece has dimension g(2g−1) = 15 (the dimension of ∧²H¹ minus the symplectic form). The de Rham Selmer variety SeldR(2) is the subvariety of the de Rham fundamental groupoid cut out by the crystallinity condition on the associated (φ, Γ)-module. Its dimension is given by the dimension of the Bloch–Kato Selmer group H¹f(Gℚ5, H¹ét(Xℚ̄, ℚ5)) plus the dimension of the second level obstruction space. For a generic curve, the local condition at p = 5 imposes g = 3 independent conditions on the 6-dimensional space H¹dR, leaving a 3-dimensional space. The second level adds at most 15 dimensions, but the crystallinity condition imposes further constraints. In practice, for many curves the dimension of SeldR(2) is computed to be 0 or 1. If dim SeldR(2) = 0, then the period map j : X(ℚ5) → SeldR(2) has finite image, so X(ℚ) is finite. If dim = 1, one must check whether the image of X(ℚ5) is a proper subvariety of SeldR(2) (which is 1-dimensional); if so, X(ℚ) is still finite. The explicit computation uses the Kim–Vologodsky algorithm to compute the p-adic iterated integrals that define the period map.
Exam Strategy: Non‑abelian Chabauty
When a question asks about the Chabauty–Kim method, always identify the three key objects: the non‑abelian Kummer map (target: H¹(GK, π₁ét)), the Selmer variety (local conditions), and the p-adic period map (comparison isomorphism to de Rham side). The finiteness criterion is dim SeldR < dim X(Kp) = 1. Do not confuse the classical Chabauty condition (r < g) with the non‑abelian condition (dimension of Selmer variety). Remember that the method works for curves where the Jacobian rank is ≥ genus, which is precisely where classical Chabauty fails.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Moduli Stack of Curves and the Mapping Class Group

The anabelian philosophy extends naturally from the moduli space Mg,n of smooth curves to the moduli stack ℳg,n, whose étale fundamental group is the profinite mapping class group Γ̂g,n. The stack ℳg,n is a Deligne–Mumford stack over ℤ[1/6] (or ℤ for g ≥ 2), and its geometric étale fundamental group π₁ét(ℳg,n, K̄) is isomorphic to the profinite completion of the topological mapping class group Γg,n = π₀(Diff+(Σg,n)). The exact sequence of stacks 1 → ℳg,n,K̄ → ℳg,n → Spec K → 1 yields the fundamental exact sequence 1 → Γ̂g,n → π₁ét(ℳg,n) → GK → 1. The anabelian conjecture for the moduli stack asserts that the isomorphism class of this exact sequence as an extension of profinite groups determines the stack ℳg,n over K̄. This is known for g = 0 (where ℳ0,n is the stack of n-pointed rational curves, and Γ̂0,n is the profinite pure braid group on n−3 strands) and for g = 1 (where ℳ1,1 is the stack of elliptic curves, and Γ̂1,1 ≅ SL₂(ℤ̂)). For g ≥ 2, the conjecture is open in general but has been proved for certain low-genus cases and for curves with no extra automorphisms (Harbater–Stevenson, 2019). The outer Galois action GK → Out(Γ̂g,n) factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT, and the pro‑ℓ completion GTℓ acts on the pro‑ℓ mapping class group Γg,n(ℓ). The kernel of GTℓ → Out(Γg,n(ℓ)) measures the failure of the anabelian conjecture for the stack. A key technical tool is the Harer–Ivanov stability theorem: the homology Hi(Γg,n, ℤ) stabilizes as g → ∞, and the stable homology is the homology of the infinite loop space Ω∞Σ∞CP∞−1. In the pro‑ℓ setting, the Lie algebra of Γg,n(ℓ) is the free Lie algebra on the Dehn twists modulo the Johnson relations, and the action of GTℓ is given by the Ihara action. For examinations, the critical points are: (1) the moduli stack ℳg,n has fundamental group Γ̂g,n; (2) the anabelian conjecture for stacks is stronger than for coarse moduli spaces because the stack remembers automorphisms; (3) the outer Galois action factors through GT; (4) the pro‑ℓ mapping class group and its Lie algebra are central computational objects; (5) the conjecture is known for g = 0, 1 and for low genus with no extra automorphisms.

The Anabelian Conjecture for Affine Hyperbolic Curves: Cusps, Tangential Basepoints, and the Cuspidal Section Conjecture

The Grothendieck section conjecture was originally formulated for proper hyperbolic curves. For affine hyperbolic curves U = X D, where X is a smooth projective curve of genus g and D is a reduced divisor of degree r ≥ 1 with 2g−2+r > 0, the étale fundamental group π₁ét(UK̄, b) depends on the choice of basepoint b. When b is a K-rational point of U, the section conjecture asks whether every section s : GK → π₁ét(U) arises from a rational point. However, for many applications (e.g., the Chabauty–Kim method), one must use a tangential basepoint b at a cusp x ∈ D(K). A tangential basepoint is a choice of a non-zero tangent vector in the fiber of the logarithmic tangent bundle at x. The fundamental group with tangential basepoint π₁ét(U, btang) fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(UK̄, btang) → π₁ét(U, btang) → GK → 1, but the outer action of GK on the geometric fundamental group is no longer the full story: the tangential basepoint introduces a canonical splitting of the inertia subgroup at x. The cuspidal section conjecture (Hoshi, 2015) states that for an affine hyperbolic curve U/K and a tangential basepoint at a cusp, the sections s : GK → π₁ét(U, btang) that are “cuspidal” (i.e., their restriction to the inertia group at the cusp is trivial) correspond bijectively to K-rational points of U. This is known for U = ℙ¹ {0, 1, ∞} (the projective line minus three points) by work of Ihara, Nakamura, and others, and for certain modular curves. The tangential basepoint is essential for the p-adic period map in the Chabauty–Kim method: the period map sends a rational point y ∈ U(Kp) to the de Rham path from the tangential basepoint at the cusp to y in the de Rham fundamental groupoid. The comparison isomorphism then identifies this with the étale section. For exam questions, the key distinctions are: (1) proper vs. affine hyperbolic curves; (2) rational basepoints vs. tangential basepoints at cusps; (3) the cuspidal condition on sections (trivial inertia action); (4) the role of tangential basepoints in the p-adic period map and the Chabauty–Kim method; (5) the cuspidal section conjecture is the correct formulation for affine curves with tangential basepoints.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Étale Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of Elliptic Curves and the Action of the Absolute Galois Group

The moduli stack ℳ1,1 of elliptic curves is the simplest higher-dimensional stack (dimension 1) whose anabelian geometry is completely understood and provides a template for the general case. Over a field K of characteristic 0, the étale fundamental group of ℳ1,1 fits into the exact sequence 1 → SL₂(ℤ̂) → π₁ét(ℳ1,1) → GK → 1, where SL₂(ℤ̂) ≅ Γ̂1,1 is the profinite mapping class group of the torus. The outer Galois action GK → Out(SL₂(ℤ̂)) ≅ GL₂(ℤ̂)/{±1} is given by the cyclotomic character χ : GK → ℤ̂× on the determinant and the trivial action on the projective group PSL₂(ℤ̂). Concretely, for σ ∈ GK, the action on SL₂(ℤ̂) is conjugation by the matrix (χ(σ) 0; 0 1) modulo ±1. This action factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT, and the image of GK in GT is the cyclotomic subgroup. The anabelian conjecture for ℳ1,1 is a theorem: the isomorphism class of the exact sequence as an extension of profinite groups determines ℳ1,1 over K̄. This follows from the fact that the j-invariant map ℳ1,1 → 𝔸¹ is a ℤ/2-quotient, and the fundamental group of 𝔸¹ is trivial. The section conjecture for ℳ1,1 asks whether every section s : GK → π₁ét(ℳ1,1) comes from a K-rational point, i.e., an elliptic curve over K. This is equivalent to the statement that the Galois cohomology class in H¹(GK, SL₂(ℤ̂)) defined by the section corresponds to an elliptic curve. By the Kummer theory of elliptic curves, H¹(GK, SL₂(ℤ̂)) classifies elliptic curves over K up to isomorphism, so the section conjecture holds trivially for ℳ1,1. The p-adic uniformization of ℳ1,1 is given by the Tate curve: over a p-adic field K, the p-adic period map identifies ℳ1,1(K) with the quotient of the rigid analytic space 𝔾man by the action of ℤ via q ↦ qp. The de Rham fundamental group of ℳ1,1 is the Malcev completion of SL₂(ℤ), and the comparison isomorphism is the classical p-adic Hodge theory for the Tate module of the universal elliptic curve. For examinations, the essential facts are: (1) π₁ét(ℳ1,1) = SL₂(ℤ̂) ⋊ GK with the cyclotomic action; (2) the anabelian conjecture is a theorem for ℳ1,1; (3) the section conjecture holds because H¹(GK, SL₂(ℤ̂)) classifies elliptic curves; (4) the p-adic uniformization is the Tate curve; (5) this is the base case for the anabelian geometry of ℳg,n.

  • Confusing the anabelian conjecture for the moduli stack ℳg,n (fundamental group = profinite mapping class group Γ̂g,n) with the conjecture for the coarse moduli space Mg,n (fundamental group = orbifold fundamental group, which is a quotient of Γ̂g,n by the finite automorphism groups of curves).
  • Assuming the section conjecture for affine curves with tangential basepoints is the same as for proper curves; the cuspidal condition (trivial inertia action) is essential and the bijection is with cuspidal sections, not all sections.
  • Thinking the Chabauty–Kim method requires the classical Chabauty condition r < g; the whole point of the non‑abelian method is that it works when r ≥ g by using higher nilpotent quotients of the fundamental group.
  • Neglecting the weight filtration when comparing the étale and de Rham fundamental groups in the Chabauty–Kim method; the period map respects the lower central series, and the Selmer variety is defined level by level in the nilpotent filtration.
  • Believing the anabelian conjecture for Shimura varieties is known in general; it is known only for Shimura curves (dimension 1) and certain special higher-dimensional cases under extra hypotheses (Tate conjecture, Mumford–Tate conjecture).
  • Confusing the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT (a pro‑finite group acting on the tower of moduli spaces M0,n) with the absolute Galois group GK; the outer Galois action factors through GT, but the map GK → GT is neither injective nor surjective in general.
  • Overlooking that the tangential basepoint at a cusp is not a geometric point but a choice of tangent vector in the logarithmic tangent bundle; the fundamental group with tangential basepoint is canonically isomorphic to the fundamental group with a nearby rational basepoint, but the Galois action differs by the inertia action at the cusp.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Moduli Stack of Curves with Level Structure and the Galois Action on the Profinite Mapping Class Group

Let K be a field of characteristic zero and fix an integer N ≥ 3. The moduli stack Mg,n[N] of smooth genus‑g curves with n marked points and full level‑N structure is a fine Deligne–Mumford stack, representable by a smooth quasi‑projective scheme over K. Its étale fundamental group fits into a short exact sequence of profinite groups

1 → Γ̂g,n[N] → π₁ét(Mg,n[N]) → GK → 1,

where Γ̂g,n[N] is the profinite completion of the level‑N subgroup of the mapping class group Γg,n. The outer Galois action GK → Out(Γ̂g,n[N]) factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT, and the image is the cyclotomic subgroup generated by the cyclotomic character χ : GK → ℤ̂×. Concretely, for σ ∈ GK the action on Γ̂g,n[N] is given by conjugation by the matrix diag(χ(σ),1,…,1) in the symplectic representation on the first homology of the universal curve.

The anabelian conjecture for Mg,n[N] asserts that the isomorphism class of the above exact sequence as an extension of profinite groups determines the stack over K̄. This is a theorem for (g,n) = (0,≥3) by work of Nakamura and Tamagawa, and for (g,n) = (1,1) by Mochizuki’s theory of the elliptic modular tower. For higher genus the conjecture remains open; the main obstruction is the lack of a complete description of the outer Galois action on the full profinite mapping class group Γ̂g,n.

The section conjecture for Mg,n[N] predicts a bijection between continuous sections s : GK → π₁ét(Mg,n[N]) and K-rational points of the stack, i.e. isomorphism classes of curves with level‑N structure defined over K. The cohomology set H¹(GK, Γ̂g,n[N]) classifies such curves up to isomorphism, and the section conjecture is equivalent to the statement that every section arises from a rational point. For (g,n) = (0,≥3) this follows from Belyi’s theorem and the combinatorial anabelian program; for (g,n) = (1,1) it is a consequence of the Kummer theory of elliptic curves.

In the p‑adic setting, the p‑adic uniformization of Mg,n[N] is given by the theory of p‑adic Teichmüller spaces. The de Rham fundamental group is the Malcev completion of the discrete mapping class group Γg,n[N], and the comparison isomorphism with the étale fundamental group is provided by p‑adic Hodge theory for the universal curve. The weight filtration on the de Rham fundamental group matches the lower central series filtration on the étale side, and the period map respects the GT‑action.

The Anabelian Conjecture for the Moduli Stack of Stable Curves and the Role of the Boundary Strata

The compactified moduli stack M̄g,n of stable curves of genus g with n marked points is a proper smooth Deligne–Mumford stack over K. Its étale fundamental group sits in an exact sequence

1 → π₁geom(M̄g,n) → π₁ét(M̄g,n) → GK → 1,

where the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the orbifold fundamental group of the topological moduli space of stable curves. The boundary divisor ∂M̄g,n is a normal crossing divisor whose irreducible components correspond to nodal curves obtained by pinching a non‑separating or separating simple closed curve on the underlying topological surface. Each stratum is isomorphic to a product of lower‑genus moduli stacks M̄g₁,n₁ × M̄g₂,n₂ (or a single factor when the node is non‑separating).

The anabelian conjecture for M̄g,n states that the isomorphism class of the above exact sequence determines the stack over K̄. This is known for genus zero by Nakamura’s work on the Grothendieck–Teichmüller tower, and for genus one by Mochizuki’s results on the elliptic modular tower. For higher genus the conjecture is open; a standard inductive strategy would be to prove that the fundamental group of the boundary, together with the clutching maps, reconstructs the fundamental group of the interior. The clutching morphisms induce homomorphisms on fundamental groups that are compatible with the Galois action, and the boundary strata provide a filtration whose associated graded pieces are fundamental groups of products of lower‑dimensional moduli stacks.

The section conjecture for M̄g,n is more delicate because a K-rational point may correspond to a stable curve with nodes. A section s : GK → π₁ét(M̄g,n) is called cuspidal if its composition with the inertia groups at the generic points of the boundary divisor is trivial. The cuspidal section conjecture predicts a bijection between cuspidal sections and K-rational points of M̄g,n. For genus zero this follows from the combinatorial anabelian program; for genus one it is a consequence of the Kummer theory of the universal generalized elliptic curve. In higher genus the conjecture is open and is closely related to the anabelian conjecture for the boundary strata.

In p‑adic Hodge theory, the de Rham fundamental group of M̄g,n carries a logarithmic weight filtration reflecting the normal crossing boundary. The comparison isomorphism with the étale fundamental group respects this filtration, and the period map extends to the logarithmic setting. The p‑adic uniformization of the boundary strata is given by products of p‑adic Teichmüller spaces of lower genus, and the clutching maps become analytic morphisms between these spaces.

Technique
Explain why the cuspidal condition on a section of the fundamental group exact sequence for the moduli stack of stable curves is necessary for the section conjecture to hold.
A section s : G_K → π₁^ét(M̄g,n) gives a Galois cohomology class in H¹(G_K, π₁^geom). If s is not cuspidal, its restriction to an inertia group I_v at a boundary divisor is non‑trivial, meaning the associated Galois representation has non‑trivial monodromy around the node. Such a section would correspond to a family of curves whose special fibre acquires a node with non‑trivial monodromy, i.e. a curve that is not stable over the valuation ring. The section conjecture aims to classify K‑rational points of the proper stack M̄g,n, which are precisely stable curves over K. A non‑cuspidal section would produce a point in the boundary of the compactified moduli space that does not extend to a stable model, violating the bijection. Therefore only cuspidal sections, which have trivial inertia action, correspond to genuine K‑rational points of M̄g,n.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks about the section conjecture for a compactified moduli stack, first identify whether the stack is proper (M̄) or open (M). For proper stacks, always check if the section is required to be cuspidal; the trivial inertia condition is the key technical hypothesis that makes the bijection work. Remember that the cuspidal condition is automatic for the open moduli stack Mg,n because there is no boundary, but it becomes essential once you compactify.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Étale Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of Abelian Varieties and the Symplectic Galois Representation

Let Ag denote the moduli stack of principally polarized abelian varieties of dimension g over a field K of characteristic zero. The étale fundamental group of Ag fits into a split exact sequence of profinite groups

1 → Sp2g(ℤ̂) → π₁ét(Ag) → GK → 1,

where Sp2g(ℤ̂) is the profinite symplectic group, canonically isomorphic to the profinite completion of the mapping class group of a complex torus of dimension g. The splitting is given by the action of GK on the Tate module of the universal abelian variety, which yields a continuous homomorphism GK → GSp2g(ℤ̂) with multiplier the cyclotomic character χ. The outer action GK → Out(Sp2g(ℤ̂)) factors through the quotient GSp2g(ℤ̂)/{±1} and is trivial on the derived subgroup Sp2g(ℤ̂); the only non‑trivial part is the cyclotomic multiplier on the similitude factor.

The anabelian conjecture for Ag asserts that the isomorphism class of the above exact sequence determines the stack over K̄. This is a theorem for g = 1 (the modular curve case) by work of Mochizuki and Tamagawa. For g ≥ 2 the conjecture is open; the main difficulty is that the symplectic Galois representation does not determine the full outer action on the profinite mapping class group, and the stack Ag is not hyperbolic in the sense of having a trivial geometric fundamental group.

The section conjecture for Ag predicts a bijection between continuous sections s : GK → π₁ét(Ag) and K-rational points of Ag, i.e. isomorphism classes of principally polarized abelian varieties over K. The cohomology set H¹(GK, Sp2g(ℤ̂)) classifies such abelian varieties up to isomorphism via the Galois action on their Tate modules. For g = 1 this is exactly the Kummer theory of elliptic curves; for higher genus the conjecture is equivalent to the statement that every Galois‑stable symplectic lattice arises from an abelian variety, which is a deep open problem related to the Tate conjecture for abelian varieties.

In the p‑adic setting, the p‑adic uniformization of Ag is given by the Rapoport–Zink space for p‑divisible groups. The de Rham fundamental group of Ag is the Malcev completion of the discrete symplectic group Sp2g(ℤ), and the comparison isomorphism with the étale fundamental group is provided by the p‑adic Hodge theory of the universal p‑divisible group. The weight filtration on the de Rham side corresponds to the lower central series filtration on the étale side, and the period map is equivariant for the cyclotomic Galois action.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of Curves with Marked Points and the Cuspidal Section Conjecture for Higher Genus

For integers g ≥ 0, n ≥ 0 with 2g−2+n > 0, let Mg,n be the moduli stack of smooth genus‑g curves with n distinct marked points over a field K of characteristic zero. Its étale fundamental group fits into the exact sequence

1 → Γ̂g,n → π₁ét(Mg,n) → GK → 1,

where Γ̂g,n is the profinite mapping class group of a genus‑g surface with n punctures. The outer Galois action GK → Out(Γ̂g,n) factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT, and the image is the cyclotomic subgroup. The action on the profinite completion of the pure mapping class group is given by the cyclotomic character on the abelianization (the first homology) and is trivial on the Johnson kernel.

The cuspidal section conjecture for Mg,n states that the set of continuous sections s : GK → π₁ét(Mg,n) with trivial inertia action at the boundary divisors of the Deligne–Mumford compactification M̄g,n is in bijection with the set of K-rational points of Mg,n. The inertia condition means that for each geometric generic point of a boundary divisor, the composition of s with the inertia subgroup I ⊂ GK is the identity in Γ̂g,n. Equivalently, the section does not pick up a Dehn twist around the vanishing cycle associated to the node.

This conjecture is known for (g,n) = (0,≥3) by the combinatorial anabelian program of Nakamura and Tamagawa, and for (g,n) = (1,1) by Mochizuki’s work on the elliptic modular tower. For higher genus the conjecture is open; the main evidence comes from the fact that the cuspidal condition exactly matches the condition that a section comes from a smooth curve with marked points, as opposed to a stable curve with nodes. The tangential basepoints at the boundary, which are choices of tangent vectors in the logarithmic tangent bundle, provide a canonical isomorphism between the fundamental group with a tangential basepoint and the fundamental group with a nearby rational basepoint, but the Galois action differs by the inertia action. The cuspidal condition forces this difference to be trivial.

In p‑adic Hodge theory, the de Rham fundamental group of Mg,n carries a weight filtration whose graded pieces are the Malcev completions of the lower central series quotients of Γg,n.
