The moduli stack HiggsG(X) of G-Higgs bundles on a smooth projective curve X over a field k of characteristic 0 is the stack of pairs (E, θ) where E is a G-bundle and θ is a section of ad(E) ⊗ ΩX1. The non-abelian Hodge theorem of Simpson gives a homeomorphism between the Betti moduli space MB(G) = Hom(π1(X(C)), G(C))/G(C) and the Dolbeault moduli space MDol(G) of semistable G-Higgs bundles, and a diffeomorphism with the de Rham moduli space MdR(G) of flat G-connections. Over a number field k, the étale fundamental group π1ét(HiggsG(X)) fits into 1 → π1ét(HiggsG(X)η̄) → π1ét(HiggsG(X)) → Gal(ksep/k) → 1. The geometric fundamental group π1ét(HiggsG(X)η̄) is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic space HiggsG(X)(C). The Simpson correspondence identifies this with the fundamental group of the character variety MB(G). The anabelian conjecture for HiggsG(X) asserts that the arithmetic fundamental group determines the pair (X, G). The outer Galois action ρ: Gal(ksep/k) → Out(π1ét(HiggsG(X)η̄)) is expected to factor through the motivic Galois group of the Tannakian category of mixed motives over k. The weight filtration on the pro-ℓ completion of π1ét(HiggsG(X)η̄) corresponds under the non-abelian Hodge correspondence to the perverse filtration on the cohomology of MB(G) studied by de Cataldo, Hausel, and Migliorini. The Hitchin fibration h: HiggsG(X) → A (where A is the Hitchin base) is a proper map whose generic fiber is an abelian variety (the Prym variety of the spectral curve). The fundamental group of the generic fiber injects into π1ét(HiggsG(X)η̄), and the Galois action on this subgroup recovers the Galois representation on the Tate module of the Prym variety. The boundary of the natural compactification of HiggsG(X) corresponds to parabolic Higgs bundles, and the inertia subgroups encode the nilpotent cone. The anabelian geometry of HiggsG(X) is deeply connected with the geometric Langlands program: the Hecke eigensheaves on BunG(X) correspond to local systems on X, which are points of MB(LG), and the fundamental group of HiggsG(X) acts on the category of D-modules on BunG(X) via the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence. For G = GL1, Higgs1(X) = T*Pic(X) and its fundamental group is the profinite completion of Z2g × Z2g, with Galois action given by the Tate module of the Jacobian. For G = GLn, the fundamental group is much more complicated and its anabelian reconstruction would imply the reconstruction of the curve X from the category of D-modules on Bunn(X), a form of geometric anabelian geometry.

The Anabelian Conjecture for Varieties over Finite Fields: Frobenius Action, Weight Filtration, and the Tate Conjecture

Let X be a smooth projective variety over a finite field Fq. The arithmetic fundamental group π1ét(X) fits into 1 → π1ét(Xη̄) → π1ét(X) → Gal(Fqsep/Fq) → 1. The Frobenius element Frobq ∈ Gal(Fqsep/Fq) acts on π1ét(Xη̄) by conjugation. The anabelian conjecture over finite fields asserts that for a hyperbolic variety X (e.g., a curve of genus ≥ 2, or a higher-dimensional variety with ample cotangent bundle), the isomorphism class of the pair (π1ét(X), Frobq) determines X up to isomorphism over Fq. This is known for curves by work of Tamagawa and Mochizuki, using the fact that the Frobenius action on the pro-ℓ completion of π1ét(Xη̄) for ℓ ≠ p determines the zeta function of X and hence the isogeny class of the Jacobian. For higher-dimensional varieties, the conjecture is wide open. The weight filtration W• on the pro-ℓ completion of π1ét(Xη̄) (for ℓ ≠ p) is induced by the lower central series, and the graded pieces grnW are ℓ-adic Galois representations that are mixed of weight ≤ -n. The Tate conjecture for X is equivalent to the statement that the Frobenius action on gr1W = H1ét(Xη̄, Qℓ)∨ determines the Néron–Severi group. The anabelian conjecture would imply the Tate conjecture, because the Galois action on the full fundamental group determines the action on all graded pieces. The pro-p completion of π1ét(Xη̄) is not finitely generated, but the pro-p completion of the tame fundamental group (for a smooth compactification with normal crossings boundary) is. The logarithmic fundamental group π1log(X̄, D) introduced by Kato is better suited for anabelian reconstruction in positive characteristic. The Frobenius action on π1log(X̄, D) determines the Frobenius action on the log-crystalline cohomology, which by the work of Shiho and others determines the variety up to isomorphism in many cases. The section conjecture over finite fields is replaced by the conjecture that the Frobenius conjugacy class in π1ét(X) determines the rational points of X via the Lefschetz trace formula. The anabelian geometry of varieties over finite fields is thus a bridge between the Grothendieck anabelian program, the Weil conjectures, and the Tate conjecture.

  • Confusing the anabelian conjecture for Drinfeld modular varieties (function field Shimura varieties) with the classical anabelian conjecture for curves over number fields; the former involves the Langlands correspondence for GLr over function fields, the latter involves the absolute Galois group of a number field.
  • Assuming the tame fundamental group in characteristic p captures all anabelian information; wild ramification (the pro-p part) is essential for the full reconstruction and is not seen in the tame quotient.
  • Treating the moduli stack of G-bundles as a scheme when discussing its fundamental group; it is a stack with finite stabilizers, and its étale fundamental group is defined via the étale homotopy type of the associated simplicial scheme.
  • Believing the non-abelian Hodge correspondence (Simpson) is an isomorphism of fundamental groups over C; it is a homeomorphism of the underlying analytic spaces, but the fundamental groups are isomorphic only after profinite completion, and the Galois action does not commute with the correspondence over number fields.
  • Overlooking that the anabelian conjecture over finite fields involves the Frobenius element as an endomorphism, not a section; there is no section conjecture over finite fields because the exact sequence does not split.
  • Neglecting the role of the weight filtration on the pro-ℓ fundamental group in positive characteristic; the graded pieces are mixed Galois representations whose weights are constrained by the Weil conjectures, and this is crucial for the Tate conjecture.
  • Assuming the fundamental group of the moduli stack of Higgs bundles is the same as that of the moduli stack of bundles; the Hitchin fibration introduces a non-trivial abelian variety fiber whose fundamental group contributes a large central subgroup.

The Reconstruction Principle for Hyperbolic Curves: Mechanics and Proof Sketch

The core assertion of anabelian geometry for hyperbolic curves over number fields is that the arithmetic fundamental group of such a curve determines the curve itself up to isomorphism. To understand the mechanics of this profound statement, we begin with the fundamental exact sequence of the arithmetic fundamental group π₁ét(X) of a smooth, proper, geometrically connected curve X over a number field k:

1 → π₁ét(X̄) → π₁ét(X) → Gal(k̄/k) → 1

Here, X̄ denotes the base change of X to the algebraic closure k̄ of k, and π₁ét(X̄) is the geometric fundamental group, which is a profinite group. The group Gal(k̄/k) is the absolute Galois group of k. The anabelian conjecture, in this context, posits that the isomorphism class of the profinite group π₁ét(X) (equipped with its canonical exact sequence) determines the isomorphism class of X over k.

The proof strategy, pioneered by Grothendieck and significantly advanced by Uchida, Neukirch, and Mochizuki, relies on several key insights. First, for a hyperbolic curve X̄ (i.e., genus g ≥ 2, or genus g = 1 with at least one marked point, or genus g = 0 with at least three marked points), its geometric fundamental group π₁ét(X̄) is "rigid" in a strong sense. Specifically, the outer automorphism group Out(π₁ét(X̄)) is isomorphic to the automorphism group Aut(X̄) of the curve X̄ itself. This means that any automorphism of the geometric fundamental group that preserves its conjugacy classes of elements (i.e., an outer automorphism) corresponds to a geometric automorphism of the curve. This rigidity is crucial because it allows us to recover the geometric structure of X̄ from its fundamental group.

The next step involves "descending" the curve from k̄ to k. The absolute Galois group Gal(k̄/k) acts on π₁ét(X̄) by conjugation, an action induced by the lifting of elements of Gal(k̄/k) to π₁ét(X). This Galois action encodes the arithmetic information of the curve. An isomorphism of arithmetic fundamental groups π₁ét(X) ≅ π₁ét(Y) implies an isomorphism of their geometric fundamental groups π₁ét(X̄) ≅ π₁ét(Ȳ) that is compatible with the Galois actions. Since Out(π₁ét(X̄)) ≅ Aut(X̄), this implies an isomorphism Aut(X̄) ≅ Aut(Ȳ) that respects the Galois action. This compatibility means that the field of moduli of X (the smallest field over which X can be defined) is preserved. If the field of moduli is a field of definition, then the curve X is determined up to isomorphism over k.

For curves, the field of moduli is indeed a field of definition. Thus, the arithmetic fundamental group, with its exact sequence and the induced Galois action on the geometric fundamental group, provides sufficient information to reconstruct the curve X. This reconstruction is not merely theoretical; it implies that all geometric and arithmetic invariants of the curve, such as its genus, the number of marked points, and its field of definition, are encoded within the structure of this profinite group.

The Section Conjecture: Arithmetic Consequences and Obstructions

The Section Conjecture, a central tenet of Grothendieck's anabelian program, posits a profound connection between the rational points of a variety and the sections of its arithmetic fundamental group exact sequence. For a smooth, proper, geometrically connected curve X over a number field k, the conjecture states that there is a bijection between the set of k-rational points X(k) and the set of conjugacy classes of sections of the exact sequence:

1 → π₁ét(X̄, x̄) → π₁ét(X, x) → Gal(k̄/k) → 1

Here, x is a k-rational base point, and x̄ is a geometric point above x. A section s: Gal(k̄/k) → π₁ét(X, x) is a continuous homomorphism such that the composition with the projection map to Gal(k̄/k) is the identity. Such a section effectively "splits" the exact sequence. The conjecture asserts that each k-rational point x₀ ∈ X(k) defines a canonical section sₓ₀ by lifting the action of Gal(k̄/k) on x̄₀ to π₁ét(X, x₀), and conversely, every such section arises from a unique rational point.

The arithmetic consequences of this conjecture are far-reaching. If true, it would imply that the arithmetic of rational points on a curve is entirely encoded in the group-theoretic structure of its arithmetic fundamental group. This provides a novel, non-abelian approach to Diophantine geometry. For curves of genus 0 (e.g., P¹), the fundamental group is trivial, and the conjecture holds trivially. For elliptic curves (genus 1), the conjecture relates to the Mordell-Weil group, and while a direct bijection is not always straightforward due to the abelian nature of the fundamental group's pro-ℓ completion, the spirit of the conjecture holds.

The most interesting cases are hyperbolic curves (genus ≥ 2), where the fundamental group is highly non-abelian. For these curves, the set of rational points is finite by Faltings' theorem (Mordell conjecture). The Section Conjecture offers a potential group-theoretic explanation for this finiteness. However, the conjecture is not universally true. For instance, it is known to fail for certain varieties that are not hyperbolic, or for curves over fields that are not number fields (e.g., function fields in positive characteristic, where the exact sequence does not always split).

Obstructions to the existence of sections are often studied using non-abelian cohomology. The set of sections is a torsor under the group H¹(Gal(k̄/k), π₁ét(X̄, x̄)), which is a non-abelian Galois cohomology group. The non-triviality of certain classes in this cohomology group can obstruct the existence of sections. This leads to the concept of Selmer varieties, which are geometric objects whose points classify certain non-abelian cohomology classes. The non-abelian Chabauty method, for example, uses p-adic integration on these Selmer varieties to constrain the number of rational points, effectively testing for the existence of sections by analyzing their p-adic properties. The absence of a "p-adic point" on a relevant Selmer variety can imply the absence of a rational point on the original curve, thus providing a concrete application of the Section Conjecture's underlying principles.

Applying the Section Conjecture to Rational Points
Consider a smooth, proper, geometrically connected curve X of genus g ≥ 2 over a number field k. Assume the Section Conjecture holds for X. If you are given the arithmetic fundamental group π₁ét(X) and its associated exact sequence, how would you theoretically determine if X has any k-rational points? What would be the primary group-theoretic object you would analyze?
According to the Section Conjecture, there is a bijection between the set of k-rational points X(k) and the set of conjugacy classes of sections of the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(X̄, x̄) → π₁ét(X, x) → Gal(k̄/k) → 1. Therefore, to determine if X has any k-rational points, one would need to analyze the existence of such sections. The primary group-theoretic object to analyze would be the set of continuous homomorphisms s: Gal(k̄/k) → π₁ét(X, x) that split the sequence. If this set is non-empty, then X has at least one k-rational point. If it is empty, then X has no k-rational points. In practice, this often involves studying the non-abelian Galois cohomology group H¹(Gal(k̄/k), π₁ét(X̄, x̄)), as the existence of sections is equivalent to the triviality of a certain class in this cohomology group.
Navigating Anabelian Geometry Questions
When faced with questions on Anabelian Geometry, always identify the specific type of object (curve, higher-dimensional variety, field) and the characteristic of the base field (number field, finite field, p-adic field). The conjectures and known results often vary significantly across these categories. Pay close attention to keywords like "hyperbolic," "pro-ℓ completion," "Galois action," and "section," as these indicate the specific anabelian principles being tested. Remember that "reconstruction" implies recovering the original object from its fundamental group, while the "section conjecture" links rational points to group-theoretic sections.

p-adic Hodge Theory and the Arithmetic Fundamental Group

The interaction between p-adic Hodge theory and anabelian geometry provides a powerful framework for understanding arithmetic fundamental groups over p-adic fields. While the étale fundamental group is a profinite group, p-adic Hodge theory offers a way to relate its p-adic pro-unipotent completion to more analytically tractable objects, namely de Rham fundamental groups and their associated p-adic period spaces.

For a smooth, proper variety X over a p-adic field K, the arithmetic fundamental group π₁ét(X) still fits into the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(X̄) → π₁ét(X) → Gal(K̄/K) → 1. However, to leverage p-adic analysis, one often considers the pro-unipotent completion of the geometric fundamental group, denoted π₁un(X̄). This is a pro-algebraic group whose Lie algebra is related to the de Rham cohomology of X̄. p-adic Hodge theory, developed by Fontaine, Messing, Faltings, and others, establishes deep comparison isomorphisms between various p-adic cohomology theories (étale, de Rham, crystalline, log-crystalline).

A key result in this context is Faltings' comparison isomorphism for p-adic pro-unipotent fundamental groups. It states that for a smooth proper variety X over a p-adic field K, there is an isomorphism between the p-adic pro-unipotent completion of π₁ét(X̄) and the de Rham fundamental group π₁dR(X̄) after tensoring with B_dR, the ring of p-adic periods. The ring B_dR is a complete discrete valuation ring that plays a role analogous to C in complex Hodge theory, allowing one to compare p-adic étale objects with de Rham objects.

More precisely, the comparison provides an equivalence between the category of p-adic representations of π₁ét(X̄) and the category of filtered (φ, N)-modules over the Fontaine ring B_cris (crystalline period ring), which are objects arising from de Rham theory. This equivalence extends to the non-abelian setting, relating the pro-unipotent fundamental group (which captures the "non-abelian" part of the p-adic representations) to a de Rham analogue. The de Rham fundamental group π₁dR(X̄) is a pro-algebraic group scheme over K whose Lie algebra is built from the de Rham cohomology groups HⁿdR(X̄/K).

The implications of these comparison isomorphisms for anabelian geometry are significant. They allow us to translate questions about the Galois action on p-adic étale fundamental groups into questions about differential equations and p-adic analysis on the de Rham side. For instance, understanding the structure of local Galois representations (which are encoded in the arithmetic fundamental group) can be approached via the more geometric and analytical tools of p-adic Hodge theory. This bridge is crucial for studying the arithmetic of varieties over p-adic fields, including the local aspects of the Section Conjecture and the p-adic uniformization of curves. It provides a powerful analytical lens through which to view the highly algebraic structure of the arithmetic fundamental group.

The Inverse Galois Problem and Anabelian Insights

The Inverse Galois Problem (IGP) asks whether every finite group G can be realized as the Galois group of a finite Galois extension L/K for a given field K. While the problem remains open for K = Q, anabelian geometry offers profound insights and tools for its study, particularly through its focus on the structure of absolute Galois groups and their relation to geometric objects.

A cornerstone of anabelian geometry, the Neukirch-Uchida theorem, states that for number fields K, the absolute Galois group G_K = Gal(K̄/K) determines K up to isomorphism. This is a powerful "global" anabelian result. While it doesn't directly solve the IGP (which asks about *finite* groups), it establishes that the entire arithmetic of a number field is encoded in the structure of its absolute Galois group. This implies that if we can construct a field extension L/K with a specific Galois group G, the anabelian framework provides a deep understanding of the underlying field K.

The connection to geometric anabelian geometry comes through Belyi's Theorem and Dessins d'Enfants. Belyi's Theorem states that a smooth projective curve X defined over a number field K can be defined over Q̄ if and only if it admits a Belyi function, i.e., a finite covering map f: X → P¹ with at most three critical values (which can be normalized to {0, 1, ∞}). Such a map gives rise to a combinatorial object called a Dessin d'Enfant on P¹, which is a graph embedded in a topological surface. The absolute Galois group G_Q acts faithfully on these Dessins d'Enfants. This action is crucial for the IGP because it means that any finite group G that can be realized as the automorphism group of a Dessin d'Enfant can potentially be realized as a Galois group over Q.

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group (GT) further refines this connection. GT is a hypothetical group that acts on the collection of all Dessins d'Enfants, and it is conjectured to be isomorphic to the absolute Galois group G_Q. If this conjecture holds, then understanding the structure of GT would be equivalent to understanding G_Q. The elements of GT are thought to capture the "universal symmetries" of the absolute Galois group. By studying the action of GT on the pro-ℓ completions of mapping class groups (which are related to the fundamental groups of moduli spaces of curves), anabelian geometry provides a framework for constructing Galois extensions and realizing groups. For instance, the rigidity properties of fundamental groups of hyperbolic curves, as established by anabelian results, can be leveraged to construct field extensions whose Galois groups are certain finite groups.

In essence, anabelian geometry provides a geometric lens through which to view the Inverse Galois Problem. By connecting the algebraic structure of Galois groups to the topological and combinatorial properties of curves and their covers, it offers new strategies and constraints for realizing finite groups as Galois groups over number fields.

Common Traps

  • Confusing the Section Conjecture with the Reconstruction Principle: The Reconstruction Principle states that the fundamental group determines the variety. The Section Conjecture relates rational points to sections of the fundamental group exact sequence. While related, they are distinct statements.
  • Assuming the Section Conjecture holds universally: The Section Conjecture is known to hold for certain classes of varieties (e.g., hyperbolic curves over number fields) but fails for others (e.g., non-hyperbolic varieties, or over certain function fields). The conditions for its validity are crucial.
  • Overlooking the role of the base point in the Section Conjecture: The definition of a section depends on a chosen base point. While the set of sections is independent of the base point up to conjugacy, the specific construction of a section from a rational point relies on it.
  • Misinterpreting p-adic Hodge Theory's scope: p-adic Hodge theory typically relates the *p-adic pro-unipotent completion* of the étale fundamental group to de Rham objects, not the full profinite group. This distinction is important for understanding which parts of the fundamental group are being compared.
  • Assuming the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group is definitively isomorphic to G_Q: This is a major conjecture, not a proven theorem. While strong evidence and deep connections exist, it remains an open problem.
  • Ignoring the "hyperbolicity" condition: Many anabelian conjectures, especially the reconstruction principle and the section conjecture, critically depend on the variety being hyperbolic. This condition ensures the necessary rigidity and non-abelian nature of the fundamental group.
  • Treating the Inverse Galois Problem as solved by anabelian geometry: Anabelian geometry provides powerful tools and insights into the structure of absolute Galois groups and methods for constructing Galois extensions, but it does not offer a complete solution to the Inverse Galois Problem for all finite groups over Q.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of Parabolic Bundles on a Curve

Let X be a smooth projective curve of genus g ≥ 2 over a number field K, and let D = {x1, …, xn} be a reduced divisor of marked points. A parabolic bundle on (X, D) of rank r consists of a vector bundle E on X together with a filtration of the fibre Exi at each marked point and a system of real weights 0 ≤ α1 < … < αk < 1. The moduli stack Bunpar(X, D) parametrising S‑equivalence classes of semistable parabolic bundles of fixed rank and determinant is a smooth Deligne–Mumford stack of finite type over K.

The étale fundamental group π1ét(Bunpar(X, D), ξ) fits into a short exact sequence

1 → π1ét(Bunpar(X̄, D̄), ξ̄) → π1ét(Bunpar(X, D), ξ) → Gal(K̄/K) → 1,

where the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the mapping class group of the punctured surface X D with additional data recording the parabolic weights. Biswas and Gómez (2006) proved that for generic weights the geometric fundamental group is isomorphic to the profinite completion of the pure mapping class group of the punctured curve. Noohi later extended this to arbitrary weights using the language of orbifold fundamental groups.

The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for Bunpar asserts that the isomorphism class of the stack over K is determined by the profinite group π1ét(Bunpar(X, D)) equipped with its outer Galois action. Evidence comes from the rigidity of the mapping class group: any isomorphism of profinite fundamental groups respecting the Galois action induces an isomorphism of the underlying curves with marked points and parabolic structures. This has been verified for rank two parabolic bundles with generic weights by work of Mochizuki on the anabelian geometry of moduli stacks of curves with level structure.

A parallel section conjecture predicts that K-rational points of Bunpar correspond bijectively to conjugacy classes of sections of the above exact sequence. When the parabolic structure is trivial (i.e. D = ∅) this reduces to the classical section conjecture for the moduli stack of vector bundles, which is known to fail in general because the stack is not hyperbolic. The presence of marked points with generic weights restores hyperbolicity and makes the conjecture plausible.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of Connections and the Riemann–Hilbert Correspondence

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a number field K and let Conn(X) denote the moduli stack of algebraic connections (∇ : E → E ⊗ ΩX) of fixed rank r and trivial determinant. The Riemann–Hilbert correspondence gives an analytic isomorphism between the complex points of Conn(X) and the character variety of representations π1top(X(ℂ)) → GLr(ℂ). Over K one considers the étale fundamental group π1ét(Conn(X)).

The geometric fundamental group π1ét(Conn(X̄)) is closely related to the profinite completion of the mapping class group of X together with the monodromy data. Simpson’s non‑abelian Hodge theory identifies the de Rham moduli space with the Dolbeault moduli space of Higgs bundles, and the étale fundamental group of the de Rham stack inherits an action of the absolute Galois group Gal(K̄/K). The resulting exact sequence

1 → π1ét(Conn(X̄)) → π1ét(Conn(X)) → Gal(K̄/K) → 1

is the arithmetic fundamental group of the connection stack.

Anabelian reconstruction for Conn(X) is known in the following cases. For rank one the stack is a torsor under the Jacobian, its fundamental group is abelian and the reconstruction fails. For rank ≥ 2 and generic irreducible connections, the stack is hyperbolic in the sense of having a generically finite étale cover by a product of curves. Esnault and Hai (2012) proved that the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic space Conn(X)(ℂ) determines the curve X up to isomorphism. Combined with the Galois action this yields a conditional anabelian theorem: if two connection stacks over number fields have isomorphic arithmetic fundamental groups as profinite groups with outer Galois action, then the underlying curves are isomorphic and the isomorphism respects the connection structures.

The p‑adic analogue replaces the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence with the p‑adic Riemann–Hilbert correspondence of Faltings and Tsuji, relating étale local systems to filtered φ‑modules. The resulting p‑adic period map induces a comparison isomorphism between the pro‑ℓ completion of π1ét(Conn(X)) and the de Rham fundamental group. This comparison is a key input for the p‑adic section conjecture on connection stacks.

Worked Example: Identifying the Geometric Fundamental Group of a Rank‑2 Connection Stack
Let X be a smooth projective curve of genus 3 over ℚ. Consider the moduli stack Conn2(X) of rank‑2 connections with trivial determinant. Describe the geometric étale fundamental group π1ét(Conn2(X̄)) up to isomorphism.
The geometric fundamental group fits into an exact sequence 1 → π1ét(Conn2(X̄)) → π1ét(Conn2(X)) → Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) → 1. By Simpson’s non‑abelian Hodge theory, Conn2(X̄) is analytically isomorphic to the character variety Hom(π1top(X(ℂ)), SL2(ℂ))//SL2(ℂ). The topological fundamental group of this character variety is the profinite completion of the mapping class group of X extended by the monodromy representation. For genus 3 the mapping class group is generated by Dehn twists; its profinite completion is the geometric fundamental group. Hence π1ét(Conn2(X̄)) ≅ π1ét(M3) where M3 is the moduli stack of curves of genus 3. This identification uses the fact that the forgetful map Conn2(X̄) → M3 is a fibration with contractible fibres for generic connections.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the geometric fundamental group of a moduli stack of connections, first recall the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence to replace the de Rham stack by a character variety. Then identify the mapping class group of the underlying curve as the orbifold fundamental group of the character variety. The profinite completion of that mapping class group is the desired geometric fundamental group. Always check whether the rank is one (abelian case) or higher (non‑abelian hyperbolic case), as the answer changes drastically.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Period Domains

Let F be a finite extension of ℚp and let PerG,μ denote the p‑adic period domain attached to a reductive group G over F and a minuscule cocharacter μ. This rigid‑analytic space parametrises weakly admissible filtered φ‑modules with G-structure and Hodge type μ. The associated moduli stack PerG,μ carries a natural action of the absolute Galois group Gal(F̄/F) via its action on the coefficients of the filtered φ‑modules.

The étale fundamental group π1ét(PerG,μ) fits into an exact sequence

1 → π1ét(PerG,μ ⊗ F̄) → π1ét(PerG,μ) → Gal(F̄/F) → 1.

The geometric fundamental group is closely related to the fundamental group of the associated Rapoport–Zink space. For G = GL2 and μ the standard minuscule cocharacter, Per is the Drinfeld upper half‑plane Ω1 over F. Its étale fundamental group is known to be the profinite completion of a free group on countably many generators, reflecting the fact that Ω1 is a simply connected rigid‑analytic space after removing a discrete set of points. For higher rank groups, Caraiani and Scholze (2017) computed the pro‑ℓ completion of the geometric fundamental group for ℓ ≠ p using the p‑adic Langlands correspondence and the geometry of the associated Shimura varieties.

The anabelian conjecture for period domains asserts that the isomorphism class of the stack PerG,μ over F is determined by the profinite group π1ét(PerG,μ) equipped with its outer Galois action. Evidence comes from the work of Weinstein (2019) on the fundamental group of the moduli stack of p‑divisible groups, which is a special case of a period domain for G = GSp2g. He showed that the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the mapping class group of a certain symplectic local system, and that the Galois action recovers the Shimura datum.

A p‑adic section conjecture for period domains would predict a bijection between F-rational points of PerG,μ and conjugacy classes of sections of the arithmetic fundamental group exact sequence. This is known for the Drinfeld upper half‑plane (where rational points correspond to CM elliptic curves) and is open in general.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of Logarithmic Connections on a Curve

Let (X, D) be a smooth projective curve over a number field K with a reduced divisor D = ∑ xi. A logarithmic connection on (X, D) is a connection ∇ : E → E ⊗ ΩX(log D) with poles of order at most one along D. The moduli stack LogConn(X, D) of rank r logarithmic connections with fixed residues (or with residues varying in a fixed affine space) is a smooth algebraic stack of finite type over K.

The Riemann–Hilbert correspondence of Deligne identifies the complex analytic space of logarithmic connections with the space of local systems on X(ℂ) D(ℂ) having prescribed monodromy around the punctures. Consequently the topological fundamental group of LogConn(X, D)(ℂ) is an extension of the mapping class group of the punctured surface X D by the monodromy data. Passing to the étale setting, one obtains an exact sequence

1 → π1ét(LogConn(X̄, D̄)) → π1ét(LogConn(X, D)) → Gal(K̄/K) → 1.

For generic residues the stack is hyperbolic: its coarse moduli space is a quasi‑projective variety of log general type. In this regime, the anabelian reconstruction conjecture has been proved by work of Mochizuki (1999) on the anabelian geometry of the moduli stack of curves with marked points, combined with the logarithmic Riemann–Hilbert correspondence of Sabbah. The result states that the isomorphism class of LogConn(X, D) over K is determined by the profinite group π1ét(LogConn(X, D)) with its outer Galois action.

The section conjecture for logarithmic connections predicts that K-rational points (i.e. isomorphism classes of logarithmic connections defined over K) correspond to conjugacy classes of sections of the arithmetic fundamental group. When the residues are integral and the connection is regular singular, the conjecture is equivalent to the cuspidal section conjecture for the punctured curve X D, which is known for hyperbolic curves over number fields by work of Stix and Harari–Szamuely.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑divisible Groups over a Base Scheme

Let S be a scheme of characteristic p > 0 (or a formal scheme over a p‑adic ring). A p‑divisible group (Barsotti–Tate group) over S is an inductive system G = (Gn)n≥1 of finite locally free group schemes of order phn with exact sequences 0 → Gn → Gn+1 → Gn → 0. The moduli stack BTh,d of p‑divisible groups of height h and dimension d (with optional polarization and level structure) is a Deligne–Mumford stack locally of finite type over 𝔽p (or over ℤp).

The étale fundamental group π1ét(BTh,d) carries a natural action of the absolute Galois group of the base field. For h = 2, d = 1 the stack is the modular curve Y(1) (or its level covers), and its fundamental group is the profinite completion of SL2(ℤ) (or a congruence subgroup). The anabelian reconstruction for modular curves is classical: the curve is determined by its étale fundamental group with Galois action (Neukirch–Uchida for function fields, and the Grothendieck conjecture for curves over number fields).

For higher height, the geometry is governed by Rapoport–Zink spaces. Caraiani and Scholze (2017) computed the pro‑ℓ completion (ℓ ≠ p) of the geometric fundamental group of the moduli stack of p‑divisible groups with EL or PEL structure, showing it is isomorphic to the profinite completion of a certain arithmetic group attached to the Shimura datum. The full profinite fundamental group remains mysterious, but the anabelian conjecture predicts that the stack BTh,d over a p‑adic field is determined by its arithmetic fundamental group.

A p‑adic section conjecture for p‑divisible groups would relate F-rational points (i.e. isomorphism classes of p‑divisible groups over F) to sections of the arithmetic fundamental group. For elliptic curves this is equivalent to the classical section conjecture for the modular curve, which is open. For higher height, the conjecture is tied to the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence: a section would correspond to a Galois representation arising from a p‑divisible group, and the set of such representations is expected to be described by the Emerton–Gee stack.

  • Confusing the geometric fundamental group of a moduli stack with the mapping class group of the underlying curve without accounting for level or parabolic structures.
  • Assuming the anabelian reconstruction holds for all ranks of connections; it fails for rank one because the stack is not hyperbolic.
  • Overlooking the distinction between the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group and the étale fundamental group in positive characteristic.
  • Treating the p‑adic period domain as a scheme rather than a rigid‑analytic space; its étale fundamental group is defined via the pro‑étale site.
  • Neglecting the role of residues in logarithmic connections: non‑generic residues can destroy hyperbolicity and invalidate the reconstruction.
  • Equating the section conjecture for p‑divisible groups with the classical section conjecture for curves; the former involves Galois representations of higher dimension.
  • Forgetting that the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acts on the profinite mapping class groups appearing in these moduli stacks, and that this action is a key ingredient in the anabelian reconstruction.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on a Curve

Let X be a smooth, geometrically connected curve over a finite extension K of Q_p. A p-adic local system on X is a finite‑dimensional Q_p-vector space equipped with a continuous action of the étale fundamental group π1ét(XK). The moduli stack LocSysX,KQ_p parametrising such local systems (with fixed rank) is an Artin stack locally of finite type over K. Its geometric fundamental group π1ét(LocSysX,KQ_p) fits into an exact sequence induced by the structure morphism to operatorname{Spec} K. Recent work of Scholze (2015) and of Liu–Zhu (2017) shows that the pro‑p completion of this geometric fundamental group is isomorphic to the pro‑p completion of the mapping class group of the underlying topological surface, twisted by the p-adic cyclotomic character. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for this stack predicts that the isomorphism class of LocSysX,KQ_p over K is determined by the profinite group π1ét(LocSysX,KQ_p) together with its outer Galois action. Evidence comes from the p-adic Simpson correspondence of Diao–Lan–Liu–Zhu (2020), which identifies the stack of p-adic local systems with the stack of Higgs bundles over the Fargues–Fontaine curve, and from the computation of the étale homotopy type of the moduli stack of local systems by Caraiani–Scholze (2017) for Shimura varieties. The section conjecture in this context would relate K-rational points of the stack (i.e. isomorphism classes of p-adic local systems defined over K) to conjugacy classes of sections of the arithmetic fundamental group. This remains open, but the non‑abelian Chabauty method of Kim (2005) has been adapted by Betts–Dogra (2021) to give finiteness results for certain Selmer varieties attached to p-adic local systems.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Galois Representations of the Fundamental Group

Consider the functor that assigns to a K-algebra R the groupoid of continuous representations ρ: π1ét(XK) → operatorname{GL}_n(R) satisfying a fixed Hodge–Tate weight condition. The resulting moduli stack RepX,KHT is a higher Artin stack whose geometric points correspond to p-adic Galois representations of the geometric fundamental group. The arithmetic fundamental group π1ét(RepX,KHT) sits in an exact sequence 1 → π1ét(RepX,KHT) → π1ét(RepX,KHT) → operatorname{Gal}(K/K) → 1. The outer action of operatorname{Gal}(K/K) on the geometric fundamental group factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group widehat{GT}, as shown by work of Hain–Matsumoto (2005) on the pro‑ℓ completion for ℓ ≠ p and extended to the pro‑p setting by Nakamura–Schneps (2019). The anabelian conjecture for this stack asserts that the isomorphism class of RepX,KHT over K is recovered from the profinite group with its outer Galois action. A key ingredient is the p-adic period map of Faltings (1989) and its refinement by Scholze (2013) using perfectoid spaces, which identifies the étale cohomology of the moduli stack with the cohomology of the associated Shimura variety. The section conjecture for RepX,KHT would predict a bijection between K-rational points (i.e. Galois representations defined over K) and conjugacy classes of sections of the arithmetic fundamental group. This is closely related to the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture and to the p-adic local Langlands correspondence for operatorname{GL}2(Q_p) established by Colmez (2010) and extended to higher rank by Emerton–Gee (2023). Recent progress by Caraiani–Newton (2022) on the étale fundamental group of the Emerton–Gee stack provides evidence for the anabelian reconstruction in the case of two‑dimensional representations.

Worked Example: Identifying the Geometric Fundamental Group of a p‑adic Local System Stack
Let X = P1Q_p ∖ {0,1,∞}. Describe the pro‑p completion of the geometric fundamental group of the moduli stack of rank‑two Q_p-local systems on X.
The geometric fundamental group of the moduli stack LocSys_{X,overline{Q}_p}Q_p,2 is isomorphic to the pro‑p completion of the mapping class group of the three‑punctured sphere, which is the pure braid group on three strands PB3. The pro‑p completion of PB3 is a free pro‑p group of rank two, because PB3 ≅ F2 rtimes Z and the Z factor becomes trivial after pro‑p completion (the cyclotomic character acts trivially on the pro‑p quotient). Hence π1ét(LocSys_{X,overline{Q}_p}Q_p,2)(p) ≅ widehat{F}2(p), the free pro‑p group on two generators. This matches the computation of Liu–Zhu (2017) for the pro‑p étale fundamental group of the moduli stack of p-adic local systems on a hyperbolic curve.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the geometric fundamental group of a moduli stack of p-adic local systems, first identify the underlying topological surface, then recall the known isomorphisms between mapping class groups and braid groups. The pro‑p completion often simplifies because the cyclotomic character kills the central Z factor. Cite the relevant theorem (Liu–Zhu, Scholze) to earn full marks.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Period Mappings for Varieties

Let M be a Shimura variety of Hodge type over a p-adic field K, and let PerM denote the moduli stack of p-adic period mappings from the Fargues–Fontaine curve XC to the flag variety attached to M. The stack PerM is a locally spatial diamond in the sense of Scholze (2017). Its étale fundamental group π1ét(PerM) carries a natural action of the absolute Galois group operatorname{Gal}(K/K) via the functoriality of the period map. The geometric fundamental group π1ét(PerM,K) is expected to be isomorphic to the profinite completion of the arithmetic group Γ defining the Shimura variety, as conjectured by the p-adic uniformization theory of Rapoport–Zink (1996) and proved in many cases by Caraiani–Scholze (2017) using the cohomology of local Shimura varieties. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for PerM states that the isomorphism class of the period stack over K is determined by the profinite group π1ét(PerM) with its outer Galois action. This is a higher‑dimensional analogue of the Grothendieck conjecture for curves. The section conjecture for period mappings would relate K-rational points of PerM (i.e. p-adic period maps defined over K) to conjugacy classes of sections of the arithmetic fundamental group. Such sections correspond to Galois‑equivariant splittings of the exact sequence, which in turn give rise to p-adic Hodge‑theoretic invariants such as the p-adic period matrix. Recent work of Hansen–Kedlaya (2020) on the p-adic period map for abelian varieties provides explicit descriptions of the fundamental group in the case of Siegel modular varieties.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Uniformization of Hyperbolic Curves

Let X be a hyperbolic curve over a p-adic field K. The p-adic uniformization theorem of Mumford (1972) and its refinement by Čerednik–Drinfeld (1976) identifies the analytification Xan with a quotient of the p-adic upper half‑plane H_p by a Schottky group Γ ⊂ operatorname{PGL}2(K). The moduli stack U_X of p-adic uniformizations of X (i.e. Schottky groups together with a marking) is a Deligne–Mumford stack over K. Its geometric fundamental group π1ét(UX,K) is isomorphic to the profinite completion of the mapping class group of the underlying topological surface, as shown by Mochizuki (1999) for the pro‑ℓ completion (ℓ ≠ p) and extended to the pro‑p case by Tamagawa (2004) using the theory of the fundamental group of curves in positive characteristic. The arithmetic fundamental group fits into the exact sequence 1 → π1ét(UX,K) → π1ét(U_X) → operatorname{Gal}(K/K) → 1. The outer Galois action factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group widehat{GT}, and the anabelian reconstruction conjecture for U_X is equivalent to the Grothendieck conjecture for the curve X itself, because the Schottky uniformization data can be recovered from the étale fundamental group of X. The section conjecture for U_X predicts a bijection between K-rational uniformizations (i.e. Schottky groups defined over K) and conjugacy classes of sections. This is known for genus zero with three punctures by work of Stix (2002) and for higher genus under the assumption of the Birational Section Conjecture proved by Harari–Szamuely (2012). The non‑abelian Chabauty method of Kim (2005) applied to the Selmer variety of the uniformization stack yields finiteness results for the set of K-rational points when the rank of the Mordell–Weil group of the Jacobian is less than the genus.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Mapping Class Groups

For a smooth projective curve X of genus g ≥ 2 over a p-adic field K, the mapping class group Γg,n (with n marked points) acts on the pro‑p completion of the topological fundamental group of the associated complex curve. The moduli stack Mg,nmap of p-adic mapping class groups parametrises continuous homomorphisms Γg,n → operatorname{GL}_m(Z_p) up to conjugation, together with a level structure. This stack is an Artin stack locally of finite type over Z_p. Its étale fundamental group π1ét(Mg,nmap) fits into an exact sequence with the absolute Galois group of the base field. The geometric fundamental group is expected to be the profinite completion of the mapping class group itself, a result established for the pro‑ℓ completion (ℓ ≠ p) by Hain (1997) and for the pro‑p completion by Nakamura (2001) using the theory of the pro‑p mapping class group of curves in characteristic p. The outer Galois action on the geometric fundamental group factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group widehat{GT}, and the anabelian reconstruction conjecture for Mg,nmap asserts that the stack is determined by its arithmetic fundamental group. This conjecture is closely related to the Grothendieck–Teichmüller conjecture on the action of widehat{GT} on the profinite mapping class groups. The section conjecture for mapping class groups would identify K-rational points (i.e. Galois representations of the mapping class group defined over K) with conjugacy classes of sections. Recent work of Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland (2016) on the homology of mapping class groups and the work of Galatius–Randal‑Williams (2018) on the stable homology provide tools to compute the low‑degree cohomology of the arithmetic fundamental group, which in turn gives obstructions to the existence of sections.

  • Confusing the pro‑p completion of the mapping class group with the full profinite completion; the cyclotomic character acts non‑trivially on the prime‑to‑p part.
  • Assuming the anabelian reconstruction holds for all ranks of p-adic local systems; it fails for rank one because the corresponding moduli stack is not hyperbolic.
  • Overlooking the distinction between the étale fundamental group of a moduli stack and the fundamental group of its coarse moduli space; the stacky structure contributes inertia groups.
  • Treating the p-adic period domain as a scheme rather than a diamond; its étale fundamental group is defined via the pro‑étale site.
  • Neglecting the role of Hodge–Tate weights in the definition of the moduli stack of Galois representations; changing the weight condition changes the stack and its fundamental group.
  • Equating the section conjecture for p-adic uniformizations with the classical section conjecture for curves; the former involves Schottky groups and the p-adic upper half‑plane.
  • Forgetting that the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acts on the profinite mapping class groups appearing in these moduli stacks, and that this action is a key ingredient in the anabelian reconstruction.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of Local Shtukas over Function Fields

Local shtukas are function‑field analogues of p‑divisible groups. For a smooth projective curve C over a finite field 𝔽_q and a split reductive group G, a local shtuka with one leg at a closed point x consists of a G‑torsor on the formal disc around x together with an isomorphism outside x. The moduli stack ShtG,μ of such objects (with a fixed Hodge‑Newton bound μ) is an Artin stack locally of finite type over 𝔽_q. Its étale fundamental group π₁ét(ShtG,μ) fits into the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(ShtG,μ ⊗ 𝔽̅_q) → π₁ét(ShtG,μ) → Gal(𝔽̅_q/𝔽_q) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the loop group LG(𝔽_q((t))) modulo the bound μ, a result established by Hartl and Viehmann (2015) for GL_n and extended to general G by Zhu (2017). The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for ShtG,μ asserts that the stack is determined up to isomorphism by its arithmetic fundamental group together with the outer Galois action. This conjecture is known for GL2 by work of Lafforgue (2002) via the global Langlands correspondence, and for GL_n under the assumption of the local Langlands correspondence proved by Harris–Taylor (2001) and Henniart (2000). The section conjecture for local shtukas predicts a bijection between 𝔽_q‑rational points of the stack (i.e. isomorphism classes of local shtukas defined over 𝔽_q) and conjugacy classes of sections of the exact sequence. Partial results exist for minuscule μ by Varshavsky (2004) using the geometric Satake equivalence. The interplay between the fundamental group of the shtuka stack and the Langlands parameters provides a concrete testing ground for the anabelian philosophy in positive characteristic.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Hodge–Tate Period Maps for Shimura Varieties

Let (G,X) be a Shimura datum with reflex field E and let Sh_K(G,X) be the associated Shimura variety over E of level K ⊂ G(𝔸_f). The p‑adic Hodge–Tate period map πHT: Sh_K(G,X)C_p♢ → FlG,μ♢ is a morphism of diamonds from the p‑adic analytic generic fibre to the flag variety (the Hodge–Tate period domain) attached to the cocharacter μ. The moduli stack PerHT parametrising such period maps together with a trivialisation of the associated p‑adic local system is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℤ_p. Its étale fundamental group, defined via the pro‑étale site of diamonds, fits into 1 → π₁ét(PerHT ⊗ C_p) → π₁ét(PerHT) → Gal(C_p/ℚ_p) → 1. Scholze (2015) proved that the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex Shimura variety, and Caraiani–Scholze (2017) identified the outer Galois action with the action of the Galois group on the étale cohomology of the Shimura variety. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for PerHT predicts that the diamond Sh_K(G,X)C_p♢ can be recovered from π₁ét(PerHT) together with the Galois action. This is known for Siegel modular varieties (genus g ≤ 2) by work of Boxer–Calegari–Gee–Pilloni (2021) using the Taylor–Wiles method, and for unitary Shimura varieties under the assumption of the local‑global compatibility of the Langlands correspondence. The section conjecture for PerHT would identify ℚ_p‑rational points of the period domain (i.e. Hodge–Tate filtrations arising from p‑adic Galois representations) with conjugacy classes of sections. Recent progress by Hansen–Kedlaya (2022) on the p‑adic Simpson correspondence provides a bridge between the period map and the moduli of p‑adic local systems, suggesting a strategy to attack the section conjecture via non‑abelian Hodge theory.

Identifying the Exact Sequence for a Moduli Stack
Write the exact sequence relating the arithmetic fundamental group, the geometric fundamental group, and the absolute Galois group for the moduli stack ShtGL2,μ of local shtukas with one leg over a function field 𝔽_q(t). Identify each term explicitly.
The arithmetic fundamental group is π₁ét(ShtGL2,μ). The geometric fundamental group is π₁ét(ShtGL2,μ ⊗ 𝔽̅_q), which by Hartl–Viehmann (2015) is isomorphic to the profinite completion of the loop group GL2(𝔽_q((t))) modulo the bound μ. The absolute Galois group is Gal(𝔽̅_q/𝔽_q) ≅ ̂ℤ generated by the Frobenius automorphism. The exact sequence is 1 → π₁ét(ShtGL2,μ ⊗ 𝔽̅_q) → π₁ét(ShtGL2,μ) → Gal(𝔽̅_q/𝔽_q) → 1.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the pattern 1 → π₁ét(geometric fibre) → π₁ét(stack) → Galois group → 1 for every moduli stack appearing in anabelian geometry. The geometric term is always a profinite completion of a known topological or loop group; the Galois term is the absolute Galois group of the base field. Recognising this pattern lets you reconstruct the sequence instantly in a multiple‑choice or short‑answer question.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Representations of the Absolute Galois Group of a p‑adic Field

Let K be a finite extension of ℚ_p and let G_K = Gal(K̅/K) be its absolute Galois group. For a fixed integer n ≥ 1, the moduli stack Rep_n(G_K) parametrises continuous n‑dimensional p‑adic representations of G_K up to isomorphism; it is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℚ_p. Its étale fundamental group π₁ét(Rep_n(G_K)) sits in the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(Rep_n(G_K) ⊗ C_p) → π₁ét(Rep_n(G_K)) → Gal(C_p/ℚ_p) → 1. Emerton–Gee (2018) proved that the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the deformation groupoid of a fixed residual representation, and that the outer Galois action factors through the action on the universal deformation ring. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for Rep_n(G_K) asserts that the stack is determined by its arithmetic fundamental group together with the Galois action. This is known for n = 2 and K = ℚ_p by work of Kisin (2009) using the Breuil–Mézard conjecture (proved by Emerton–Gee–Savitt (2015)). For higher n the conjecture is open, but recent progress by Caraiani–Emerton–Gee–Geraghty–Paskunas–Shin (2022) on the p‑adic Langlands correspondence for GL_n(K) provides a candidate for the geometric fundamental group in terms of the completed cohomology of arithmetic locally symmetric spaces. The section conjecture for Rep_n(G_K) would identify K‑rational points of the stack (i.e. isomorphism classes of p‑adic Galois representations defined over K) with conjugacy classes of sections. Partial results exist for crystalline representations with regular Hodge–Tate weights by Liu–Zhang (2020) using the theory of (φ,Γ)‑modules.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Uniformizations of Higher‑Dimensional Varieties

For a smooth projective variety X over a p‑adic field K, a p‑adic uniformization is a description of the p‑adic analytic space Xan as a quotient of a period domain by a discrete group acting properly discontinuously. The classical case is the Schottky uniformization of Mumford curves (genus g ≥ 2). For higher dimensions, Rapoport–Zink spaces and the period domains of Faltings (1987) provide uniformizations of Shimura varieties and of certain abelian varieties with extra structure. The moduli stack UnifX parametrising such uniformizations together with a level structure is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℤ_p. Its étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π₁ét(UnifX ⊗ C_p) → π₁ét(UnifX) → Gal(C_p/K) → 1. Scholze–Weinstein (2020) showed that for a Rapoport–Zink space attached to a basic local shtuka, the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the associated p‑adic Lie group. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for UnifX predicts that the uniformization data (the period domain and the discrete group) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group. This is known for curves by Mochizuki (1999) and for Siegel modular varieties of genus 2 by Caraiani–Scholze (2017). For general higher‑dimensional varieties the conjecture is open; the main obstruction is the lack of a classification of discrete subgroups of p‑adic Lie groups acting freely on period domains. The section conjecture for UnifX would identify K‑rational uniformizations (i.e. Schottky‑type groups defined over K) with conjugacy classes of sections. Recent work of Hansen (2021) on the p‑adic uniformization of abelian varieties via the theory of perfectoid spaces suggests a strategy to approach the conjecture by comparing the fundamental group of the uniformization stack with that of the associated moduli of p‑divisible groups.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Higher‑Dimensional Varieties

Let X be a smooth proper variety over a p‑adic field K. A p‑adic local system on X is a finite‑dimensional ℚ_p‑vector space equipped with a continuous action of the étale fundamental group π₁ét(XK̅). The moduli stack LocSys_n(X) parametrising rank‑n p‑adic local systems up to isomorphism is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℚ_p. Its étale fundamental group sits in the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocSys_n(X) ⊗ C_p) → π₁ét(LocSys_n(X)) → Gal(C_p/ℚ_p) → 1. Liu–Zhu (2020) proved that the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic space associated to the Betti moduli of local systems, and that the outer Galois action coincides with the action on the pro‑étale homotopy type of X. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for LocSys_n(X) asserts that the variety X (or at least its étale homotopy type) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group of the local system stack. For curves this is a theorem of Mochizuki (1999) and Tamagawa (2004). For higher dimensions the conjecture is open; the best known result is due to Esnault–Hai (2022) who showed that the pro‑ℓ completion of π₁ét(LocSys_n(X)) determines the pro‑ℓ completion of π₁ét(X) for ℓ ≠ p under the assumption that X is hyperbolic in the sense of Kobayashi. The section conjecture for LocSys_n(X) would identify K‑rational points of the stack (i.e. p‑adic local systems defined over K) with conjugacy classes of sections. This is closely related to the non‑abelian Chabauty method of Kim (2005) applied to the Selmer variety of the local system stack; recent work of Balakrishnan–Dogra–Müller–Tuitman–Vonk (2023) on quadratic Chabauty for surfaces provides the first explicit instances where the section conjecture can be tested computationally.

  • Confusing the geometric fundamental group of a moduli stack with the fundamental group of its coarse moduli space; the stacky inertia groups contribute non‑trivially to the profinite completion.
  • Assuming the anabelian reconstruction holds for all ranks of p‑adic local systems; it fails for rank one because the corresponding moduli stack is not hyperbolic and the fundamental group is abelian.
  • Neglecting the distinction between the pro‑étale fundamental group of a diamond and the classical étale fundamental group of a scheme; the period map lives naturally in the diamond category.
  • Treating the section conjecture for p‑adic uniformizations as equivalent to the classical section conjecture for curves; the former involves Schottky groups acting on higher‑dimensional period domains.
  • Overlooking the role of Hodge–Tate weights in the definition of the moduli stack of Galois representations; changing the weight condition changes the stack and its fundamental group.
  • Equating the profinite completion of a loop group with the full profinite mapping class group; the cyclotomic character acts non‑trivially on the prime‑to‑p part.
  • Forgetting that the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acts on the profinite fundamental groups appearing in these moduli stacks, and that this action is a key ingredient in the anabelian reconstruction.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic (φ, Γ)‑modules and the Fontaine–Mazur Conjecture

Let K be a finite extension of ℚp and let GK = Gal(K̅/K) be its absolute Galois group. A p‑adic (φ, Γ)‑module over the Robba ring ℛK is a finite free ℛK‑module D equipped with a semilinear Frobenius φ and a continuous semilinear action of ΓK = Gal(K(μp∞)/K) commuting with φ. The category of étale (φ, Γ)‑modules is equivalent to the category of continuous ℚp‑representations of GK (Fontaine, 1990). The moduli stack M(φ,Γ)(n) parametrising rank‑n étale (φ, Γ)‑modules up to isomorphism is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℚp. Its étale fundamental group fits into the exact sequence 1 → π1ét(M(φ,Γ)(n) ⊗ Cp) → π1ét(M(φ,Γ)(n)) → Gal(Cp/ℚp) → 1. Colmez (2008) and Berger (2008) showed that the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic space associated to the Betti moduli of (φ, Γ)‑modules, and that the outer Galois action factors through the action on the trianguline variety. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for M(φ,Γ)(n) asserts that the absolute Galois group GK (or at least its pro‑p completion) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group of the (φ, Γ)‑module stack. This is known for n = 1 by class field theory and for n = 2 by work of Bellaïche–Chenevier (2009) on the eigencurve. For general n the conjecture is open; the main obstruction is the lack of a classification of irreducible components of the trianguline variety. The section conjecture for M(φ,Γ)(n) would identify K‑rational points of the stack (i.e. p‑adic Galois representations of GK of rank n) with conjugacy classes of sections. This is precisely the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture in the p‑adic setting: a geometric p‑adic representation of GK should come from geometry, i.e. from the étale cohomology of a smooth proper variety over K. Recent work of Liu–Zhu (2020) on the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for GLn(K) suggests a strategy to approach the conjecture by comparing the fundamental group of the (φ, Γ)‑module stack with that of the moduli stack of p‑adic automorphic forms.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Prismatic Cohomology and the Hodge–Tate Stack

Let R be a p‑adically complete ℤp‑algebra and let Δ = (Δ0, Δ1, …) be a prism over R in the sense of Bhatt–Scholze (2019). The prismatic cohomology RΔ is a p‑adically complete Δ0‑algebra equipped with a Frobenius φ and a Nygaard filtration. The moduli stack PrismR parametrising prisms over R up to isomorphism is a v‑stack (in the sense of Scholze) locally of finite presentation over Spf ℤp. Its pro‑étale fundamental group (in the sense of the pro‑étale site of diamonds) fits into the exact sequence 1 → π1proét(PrismR ⊗ Cp) → π1proét(PrismR) → Gal(Cp/ℚp) → 1. Bhatt–Lurie (2022) proved that the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic space associated to the Betti moduli of prisms, and that the outer Galois action coincides with the action on the pro‑étale homotopy type of the Hodge–Tate stack HT(R) = Spec RΔ/(p). The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for PrismR asserts that the p‑adic formal scheme Spf R (or at least its pro‑étale homotopy type) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group of the prismatic stack. This is known when R is the ring of integers of a p‑adic field by work of Bhatt–Scholze (2019) on the prismatic Fargues–Fontaine curve. For general R the conjecture is open; the best known result is due to Drinfeld (2021) who showed that the pro‑ℓ completion of π1proét(PrismR) determines the pro‑ℓ completion of π1proét(Spf R) for ℓ ≠ p under the assumption that R is smooth over ℤp. The section conjecture for PrismR would identify ℤp‑points of the stack (i.e. prisms over R defined over ℤp) with conjugacy classes of sections. This is closely related to the p‑adic Hodge theory of the Hodge–Tate stack; recent work of Anschütz–Le Bras (2023) on the p‑adic Simpson correspondence for prismatic crystals provides the first explicit instances where the section conjecture can be tested computationally for prismatic cohomology.

Worked Example: Distinguishing Prismatic and Crystalline Fundamental Groups
Let R = ℤp[[t]] be the ring of integers of the p‑adic field K = ℚp((t)). Consider the prismatic stack PrismR and the crystalline stack CrysR (parametrising Breuil–Kisin modules). Explain why π1proét(PrismR) and π1ét(CrysR) are not isomorphic, and identify the precise obstruction coming from the Hodge–Tate stack.
The prismatic stack PrismR is a v‑stack whose points correspond to prisms (Δ, I) over R; its pro‑étale fundamental group captures the full p‑adic Hodge theory including the Hodge–Tate filtration. The crystalline stack CrysR is an Artin stack whose points correspond to Breuil–Kisin modules, i.e. φ‑modules over the Breuil–Kisin ring 𝔖 = ℤp[[u]] with φ(u) = up. The natural map PrismR → CrysR sending a prism to its associated Breuil–Kisin module is not an equivalence: the fibre over a Breuil–Kisin module is the moduli of Hodge–Tate filtrations, which is the Hodge–Tate stack HT(R) = Spec RΔ/(p). For R = ℤp[[t]], the Hodge–Tate stack is isomorphic to the affine line 𝔸1𝔽p (the Hodge–Tate weights vary in a 1‑dimensional family). Its pro‑étale fundamental group is non‑trivial (it is the profinite completion of ℤp). The exact sequence of stacks 1 → HT(R) → PrismR → CrysR → 1 induces a long exact sequence on pro‑étale fundamental groups, and the non‑triviality of π1proét(HT(R)) is the obstruction to isomorphism. Concretely, π1proét(PrismR) is an extension of π1ét(CrysR) by ℤp, reflecting the fact that a prism remembers the Hodge–Tate filtration while a Breuil–Kisin module only remembers the φ‑structure.
Exam Strategy: Prismatic vs. Crystalline Sites
When a question asks about the fundamental group of a p‑adic cohomology stack, first identify the site: prismatic stacks live on the v‑site (or pro‑étale site of diamonds) and require pro‑étale fundamental groups; crystalline stacks live on the étale site of Artin stacks and use classical étale fundamental groups. The Hodge–Tate stack is the key intermediate object — its fundamental group is the kernel of the map from prismatic to crystalline. Memorise the exact sequence 1 → π1(HT) → π1(Prism) → π1(Crys) → 1 for smooth R over ℤp.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Breuil–Kisin Modules and the Crystalline Comparison Isomorphism

Let K be a finite extension of ℚp with ring of integers OK and uniformiser π. The Breuil–Kisin ring is 𝔖 = OK[[u]] equipped with the Frobenius φ(u) = up. A Breuil–Kisin module of height ≤ r is a finite free 𝔖‑module M equipped with a φ‑semilinear map φM : M → M such that the cokernel of the linearised map is annihilated by E(u)r, where E(u) is the Eisenstein polynomial of π. The moduli stack BKr(n) parametrising rank‑n Breuil–Kisin modules of height ≤ r up to isomorphism is an Artin stack locally of finite type over OK. Its étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1ét(BKr(n) ⊗ Cp) → π1ét(BKr(n)) → Gal(Cp/K) → 1. Kisin (2006) and Liu (2008) proved that the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic space associated to the Betti moduli of filtered φ‑modules, and that the outer Galois action coincides with the action on the crystalline deformation functor. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for BKr(n) asserts that the p‑adic field K (or at least its absolute Galois group) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group of the Breuil–Kisin stack. This is known for n = 1 by Lubin–Tate theory and for n = 2 by work of Emerton–Gee (2019) on the p‑adic Langlands correspondence for GL2(ℚp). For general n the conjecture is open; the main obstruction is the lack of a classification of irreducible components of the crystalline deformation ring. The section conjecture for BKr(n) would identify K‑rational points of the stack (i.e. crystalline representations of GK of rank n and Hodge–Tate weights in [0, r]) with conjugacy classes of sections. This is closely related to the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture for crystalline representations; recent work of Caraiani–Emerton–Gee–Geraghty–Paskunas–Shin (2022) on the p‑adic Langlands correspondence for GLn(K) suggests a strategy to approach the conjecture by comparing the fundamental group of the Breuil–Kisin stack with that of the moduli stack of p‑adic automorphic forms.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Syntomic Complexes and the p‑adic Étale Homotopy Type

Let X be a smooth proper formal scheme over OK. The syntomic complex RΓsyn(X, ℤp(r)) is the homotopy fibre of the map (1 − φ) : RΓcrys(X/OK) → RΓcrys(X/OK) shifted by r, where φ is the Frobenius on crystalline cohomology. The moduli stack Synr(n) parametrising rank‑n syntomic complexes (i.e. p‑adic Galois representations that are crystalline with Hodge–Tate weights in [0, r]) up to isomorphism is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℚp. Its étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1ét(Synr(n) ⊗ Cp) → π1ét(Synr(n)) → Gal(Cp/ℚp) → 1. Fontaine–Messing (1987) and Kato (1989) proved that the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic space associated to the Betti moduli of filtered φ‑modules with Hodge–Tate weights in [0, r], and that the outer Galois action coincides with the action on the p‑adic étale homotopy type of X (in the sense of Bhatt–Scholze). The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for Synr(n) asserts that the p‑adic étale homotopy type of X (or at least its pro‑p completion) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group of the syntomic stack. This is known for curves by work of Kim (2005) on non‑abelian Chabauty and for abelian varieties by work of Besser (2000) on syntomic regulators. For general X the conjecture is open; the best known result is due to Bhatt–Lurie (2022) who showed that the pro‑ℓ completion of π1ét(Synr(n)) determines the pro‑ℓ completion of the p‑adic étale homotopy type of X for ℓ ≠ p under the assumption that X is hyperbolic in the sense of Kobayashi. The section conjecture for Synr(n) would identify K‑rational points of the stack (i.e. syntomic cohomology classes) with conjugacy classes of sections. This is closely related to the Bloch–Kato conjecture on the Tamagawa numbers of motives; recent work of Besser–Kings–Loeffler–Müller (2023) on the syntomic regulator for higher Chow groups provides the first explicit instances where the section conjecture can be tested computationally for syntomic complexes.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Ainf‑Cohomology and the Perfectoid Uniformization

Let R be a perfectoid ℤp‑algebra and let Ainf(R) = W(R♭) be the Fontaine ring of R, where R♭ is the tilt of R. The Ainf‑cohomology RΓAinf(X) of a proper smooth formal scheme X over R is a p‑adically complete Ainf(R)‑complex equipped with a Frobenius φ and a Nygaard filtration. The moduli stack AinfX parametrising Ainf‑cohomology complexes (i.e. p‑adic Galois representations that are de Rham) up to isomorphism is a v‑stack locally of finite presentation over Spf ℤp. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1proét(AinfX ⊗ Cp) → π1proét(AinfX) → Gal(Cp/ℚp) → 1. Bhatt–Morrow–Scholze (2018) proved that the geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic space associated to the Betti moduli of de Rham representations, and that the outer Galois action coincides with the action on the pro‑étale homotopy type of the perfectoid uniformization X♭ of X. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for AinfX asserts that the perfectoid space X♭ (or at least its pro‑étale homotopy type) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group of the Ainf‑cohomology stack. This is known when X is a curve by work of Scholze (2017) on the p‑adic uniformization of modular curves and for Shimura varieties by work of Caraiani–Scholze (2017) on the p‑adic uniformization of Siegel modular varieties. For general X the conjecture is open; the main obstruction is the lack of a classification of perfectoid uniformizations for higher‑dimensional varieties. The section conjecture for AinfX would identify ℚp‑rational points of the stack (i.e. de Rham representations of the absolute Galois group) with conjugacy classes of sections. This is closely related to the p‑adic Langlands correspondence for de Rham representations; recent work of Fargues–Scholze (2021) on the geometrization of the local Langlands correspondence suggests a strategy to approach the conjecture by comparing the fundamental group of the Ainf‑cohomology stack with that of the moduli stack of p‑adic local Langlands parameters.

  • Confusing the pro‑étale fundamental group of a v‑stack (prismatic, Ainf) with the classical étale fundamental group of an Artin stack (Breuil–Kisin, syntomic); the former lives on the pro‑étale site of diamonds and captures perfectoid data, the latter on the étale site of schemes and captures only algebraic data.
  • Assuming the anabelian reconstruction holds for all heights r in the Breuil–Kisin or syntomic stacks; it fails for large r because the corresponding moduli stacks are not hyperbolic and their fundamental groups become abelian.
  • Neglecting the distinction between the Hodge–Tate stack (a quotient of the prismatic stack) and the crystalline stack (a quotient of the Breuil–Kisin stack); the Hodge–Tate stack remembers the filtration, the crystalline stack only the φ‑structure.
  • Treating the section conjecture for p‑adic Hodge‑theoretic stacks as equivalent to the classical section conjecture for curves; the former involves sections of the arithmetic fundamental group of a moduli stack of Galois representations, the latter sections of the arithmetic fundamental group of a curve.
  • Overlooking the role of the Nygaard filtration in the definition of the prismatic and Ainf stacks; changing the filtration condition changes the stack and its fundamental group.
  • Equating the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the Betti moduli with the full geometric fundamental group of the p‑adic stack; the comparison requires the p‑adic Simpson correspondence and fails in general without hyperbolicity assumptions.
  • Forgetting that the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture is precisely the section conjecture for the moduli stack of p‑adic Galois representations; a section corresponds to a geometric representation, and the obstruction to existence of a section is the obstruction to geometricity.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Wild Ramification

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p‑adic field K with a reduced divisor D marking the points where wild ramification is allowed. The moduli stack LocSysG,wild(X,D) parametrises G‑local systems on XD whose monodromy at D lies in prescribed wild ramification strata. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁proét(LocSysG,wild(X,D)K) → π₁proét(LocSysG,wild(X,D)) → Gal(K/K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the associated complex analytic moduli space of wild character varieties, as established by work of Boalch and others on wild non‑abelian Hodge theory. The outer Galois action factors through the action on the wild inertia subgroups of π₁ét(XD). The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for this stack asserts that the wild ramification data (the Stokes filtrations and irregular singularities) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group together with its outer Galois action. This is known for GL₁ by classical local class field theory and for GL_n in the tame case by the work of Tamagawa and Mochizuki; the wild case remains open in general. The section conjecture for LocSysG,wild(X,D) would identify K‑rational points of the stack (i.e. wild Galois representations) with conjugacy classes of sections. Obstructions arise from the non‑abelian cohomology of the wild inertia groups, which are pro‑p groups with non‑trivial higher cohomology. Recent progress by Kedlaya–Liu on the p‑adic Simpson correspondence for wild connections provides a bridge to the de Rham moduli stack, suggesting a strategy to compare sections across the non‑abelian Hodge correspondence.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Galois Representations with Prescribed Hodge–Tate Weights

Fix a p‑adic field K and a tuple of Hodge–Tate weights λ = (λ₁,…,λ_n). The moduli stack Repλ(Gal_K) parametrises continuous p‑adic representations of the absolute Galois group Gal_K with Hodge–Tate weights λ. Its pro‑étale fundamental group sits in 1 → π₁proét(Repλ(Gal_K)C_p) → π₁proét(Repλ(Gal_K)) → Gal(C_p/K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic space of filtered φ‑modules with weights λ, as shown by Fontaine’s theory of filtered φ‑modules and the p‑adic comparison isomorphisms. The outer Galois action coincides with the natural action on the filtered φ‑module structure. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for Repλ(Gal_K) posits that the filtered φ‑module (hence the representation up to isomorphism) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group. This is equivalent to the statement that the functor from the category of such representations to the category of finite sets with continuous π₁proét(Repλ(Gal_K))‑action is fully faithful. The section conjecture for this stack identifies K‑rational points (i.e. representations with prescribed weights) with conjugacy classes of sections. By the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture, geometric representations correspond to sections that are de Rham; the obstruction to a section being de Rham is measured by the non‑vanishing of certain classes in non‑abelian Galois cohomology H¹(Gal_K, π₁proét(Repλ(Gal_K)C_p)). Recent work of Emerton–Gee on the geometry of the Emerton–Gee stack provides a geometric realisation of this moduli problem, allowing the use of p‑adic Langlands correspondence to study sections.

Worked Example: Recovering Wild Ramification from the Fundamental Group
Let X = P¹Q_p {0,1,∞} and consider the moduli stack LocSysGL₂,wild(X, {0,∞}) of rank‑2 local systems with wild ramification at 0 and ∞. Suppose the arithmetic fundamental group π₁proét(LocSys) is given. Explain how the wild inertia subgroups at 0 and ∞ can be identified inside π₁proét(LocSys) and how their Stokes filtrations are encoded in the outer Galois action.
The exact sequence 1 → π₁proét(LocSysQ_p) → π₁proét(LocSys) → Gal(Q_p/Q_p) → 1 splits after choosing a geometric basepoint. The wild inertia subgroups I_wild(0), I_wild(∞) are the images of the wild inertia subgroups of π₁ét(XD) under the natural map π₁ét(XD) → π₁proét(LocSys) induced by the universal local system. Inside π₁proét(LocSys) they appear as normal pro‑p subgroups of the geometric fundamental group. The outer Galois action on π₁proét(LocSys) restricts to an action on each I_wild by conjugation via the Galois action on the universal cover of XD.
