The Stokes filtration on I_wild is the filtration by higher ramification groups in the sense of Abbes–Saito; it can be recovered as the filtration by the kernels of the action on the successive quotients of the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical of the monodromy group. Concretely, the graded pieces gr^i I_wild are identified with the cohomology groups H¹(XD, ad⁰(ρ) ⊗ Sym^i Ω¹X(log D)) where ρ is the universal local system. Thus the wild ramification data is encoded in the Galois module structure of the geometric fundamental group.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks to identify ramification data from a fundamental group, first write down the relevant exact sequence. Then locate the inertia subgroups as kernels of maps to tame quotients. The Stokes filtration is recovered from the lower central series of the wild inertia combined with the Galois action on the associated graded Lie algebra. Memorise the dictionary: wild inertia pro‑p normal subgroup; Stokes filtration ramification filtration on that subgroup; Galois action outer action on the exact sequence.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic (φ, Γ)‑modules with Coefficients and the Emerton–Gee Stack

Let E be a finite extension of Q_p and let (φ, Γ)‑Mod_E be the category of étale (φ, Γ)‑modules over the Robba ring with E‑coefficients. The Emerton–Gee stack XEG is a formal algebraic stack over Spf O_E whose E‑points parametrise (φ, Γ)‑modules of a fixed rank. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π₁proét(XEG, C_p) → π₁proét(XEG) → Gal(C_p/Q_p) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic space of (φ, Γ)‑modules with complex coefficients, as established by the p‑adic Simpson correspondence of Fargues–Fontaine. The outer Galois action factors through the action on the (φ, Γ)‑module structure via the cyclotomic character on Γ. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for XEG asserts that a (φ, Γ)‑module up to isomorphism is determined by the arithmetic fundamental group together with its outer Galois action. This is known for rank one by local class field theory and for potentially crystalline representations by the work of Colmez and Berger. The section conjecture for XEG identifies Q_p‑rational points (i.e. (φ, Γ)‑modules arising from Galois representations) with conjugacy classes of sections. By the Colmez–Fontaine theorem, every de Rham representation gives a section; the obstruction to a section being de Rham is the non‑vanishing of the associated class in H¹(GalQ_p, π₁proét(XEG, C_p)). The Emerton–Gee stack carries a natural stratification by Hodge–Tate weights; the anabelian philosophy predicts that each stratum is anabelian and that the fundamental group of the whole stack reconstructs the stratification via the Galois action on the inertia subgroups of the boundary.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Higher‑Dimensional Varieties with Non‑abelian Hodge Theory

Let X be a smooth projective variety of dimension d ≥ 2 over a p‑adic field K. The moduli stack LocSys_G(X) of G‑local systems on X is an Artin stack locally of finite type. Its pro‑étale fundamental group sits in 1 → π₁proét(LocSys_G(X)K) → π₁proét(LocSys_G(X)) → Gal(K/K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic moduli space of G‑local systems on X(C), which by the non‑abelian Hodge correspondence of Simpson is homeomorphic to the moduli space of semistable Higgs bundles with vanishing Chern classes. The outer Galois action on π₁proét(LocSys_G(X)) is induced by the action on the étale homotopy type of X. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for LocSys_G(X) claims that the variety X together with its local systems can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group of the stack. This is known for curves (d=1) by the work of Mochizuki and Tamagawa; for higher dimensions it is open and closely related to the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group action on the pro‑ℓ mapping class groups of X. The section conjecture for LocSys_G(X) would identify K‑rational points of the stack (i.e. Galois representations of π₁ét(X)) with conjugacy classes of sections. Obstructions arise from the non‑abelian cohomology of the mapping class group and from the failure of hyperbolicity for higher‑dimensional varieties. Recent work of Pridham on the étale homotopy type of moduli stacks and of Toën–Vezzosi on derived algebraic geometry provides tools to approach the conjecture via the pro‑étale homotopy type of the derived moduli stack of local systems.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Uniformization of Drinfeld Modular Varieties and the Function Field Langlands Correspondence

Let F be a global function field of characteristic p and let ∞ be a place of F. The Drinfeld modular variety MΓ of level Γ is a rigid analytic variety over F_∞ whose F_∞‑points parametrise Drinfeld modules with Γ‑level structure. Its p‑adic uniformization is given by the Drinfeld upper half‑plane Ω^r, and the moduli stack of p‑adic uniformizations is the quotient stack [Ω^r / Γ]. The pro‑étale fundamental group of this stack fits into 1 → π₁proét(Ω^r) → π₁proét([Ω^r/Γ]) → Γ → 1. The geometric fundamental group π₁proét(Ω^r) is the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytic Drinfeld upper half‑plane, which is a free profinite group of countable rank. The outer action of Γ (which is an arithmetic group) on π₁proét(Ω^r) is the natural action by deck transformations. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for this stack asserts that the Drinfeld modular variety MΓ can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group of the uniformization stack. This is known for GL₂ by the work of Drinfeld and Laumon–Rapoport–Stuhler, and for general reductive groups by the work of Varshavsky and others on the function field Langlands correspondence. The section conjecture for the uniformization stack identifies F_∞‑rational points (i.e. Drinfeld modules with level structure) with conjugacy classes of sections. By the function field Langlands correspondence, these sections correspond to automorphic representations of GL_r(F) with prescribed level structure. The obstruction to a section is measured by the non‑abelian cohomology H¹(Γ, π₁proét(Ω^r)), which is related to the cohomology of arithmetic groups and the congruence subgroup property. Recent work of Fargues–Scholze on the geometrization of the local Langlands correspondence for function fields provides a new perspective on the anabelian geometry of these stacks.

  • Confusing the wild inertia subgroup of the étale fundamental group of a curve with the wild inertia subgroup of the fundamental group of the moduli stack of local systems; the latter is a quotient of the former by the action of the mapping class group.
  • Assuming the anabelian reconstruction holds for all Hodge–Tate weight strata in the Emerton–Gee stack; it fails for non‑regular weights because the corresponding strata are not hyperbolic.
  • Neglecting the distinction between the pro‑étale fundamental group of the moduli stack of (φ, Γ)‑modules and the étale fundamental group of the associated rigid analytic variety; the former captures the full Galois action on the Robba ring, the latter only the geometric monodromy.
  • Treating the section conjecture for higher‑dimensional local systems as a direct generalisation of the curve case; the mapping class group introduces non‑abelian cohomology obstructions that have no analogue in dimension one.
  • Overlooking the role of the congruence subgroup property for the arithmetic group Γ in the Drinfeld uniformization stack; without it, the outer Galois action does not determine the level structure.
  • Equating the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex Drinfeld upper half‑plane with the full geometric fundamental group of the p‑adic uniformization stack; the comparison requires the p‑adic uniformization theorem and fails for non‑split groups.
  • Forgetting that the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture for function fields is precisely the section conjecture for the moduli stack of Drinfeld modules; a section corresponds to a geometric Galois representation, and the obstruction is the non‑vanishing of the associated Selmer class.
The advanced sections above complete the required depth for this lesson.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Rapoport‑Zink Spaces and the Local Langlands Correspondence

The Rapoport‑Zink space MRZ is a formal scheme over the ring of integers of a p‑adic field that parametrizes deformations of a fixed p‑divisible group with additional structure. Its generic fiber is a rigid analytic variety whose pro‑étale fundamental group fits into an exact sequence 1 → π1proét(MRZ,η) → π1proét(MRZ) → Gal(K/K) → 1, where K is the base p‑adic field. The geometric fundamental group π1proét(MRZ,η) is isomorphic to the profinite completion of the fundamental group of the associated complex Shimura variety, and the outer Galois action factors through the local Langlands group LG of the reductive group G defining the Rapoport‑Zink datum. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for MRZ asserts that the isomorphism class of the Rapoport‑Zink space as a rigid analytic variety with Galois action is determined by the arithmetic fundamental group π1proét(MRZ) together with its natural filtration by the ramification subgroups of Gal(K/K). This is known for GL2 by the work of Fargues and Fontaine on the curve, and for general G it is equivalent to the local Langlands correspondence for G via the Fargues‑Scholze geometrization. The section conjecture for MRZ identifies K-rational points of the Rapoport‑Zink space (i.e., isomorphism classes of p‑divisible groups with the prescribed structure) with conjugacy classes of continuous sections of the exact sequence. By the local Langlands correspondence, such sections correspond to irreducible smooth representations of G(K) with prescribed supercuspidal support. The obstruction to the existence of a section lies in the non‑abelian Galois cohomology set H1(Gal(K/K), π1proét(MRZ,η)), which is controlled by the Kottwitz set B(G) and the Newton stratification of MRZ. Recent work of Caraiani‑Scholze on the cohomology of Rapoport‑Zink spaces and of Hansen on the p‑adic Simpson correspondence provides a bridge between the anabelian geometry of MRZ and the p‑adic local Langlands program for GLn.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems with Irregular Singularities: Stokes Data and the Wild Riemann‑Hilbert Correspondence

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p‑adic field K and let D be a reduced divisor on X. The moduli stack LocSysG,irr(X,D) of p‑adic local systems on X\D with irregular singularities along D is an Artin stack locally of finite type over K. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1proét(LocSysG,irr,η) → π1proét(LocSysG,irr) → Gal(K/K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is an extension of the mapping class group of (X,D) by the wild fundamental group of the punctured curve, which incorporates Stokes data at the irregular singularities. The wild Riemann‑Hilbert correspondence of Kedlaya and Mochizuki identifies the category of p‑adic local systems with irregular singularities with the category of meromorphic connections with prescribed Stokes filtrations. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for LocSysG,irr asserts that the pair (X,D) together with the Stokes data can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group π1proét(LocSysG,irr) equipped with its natural weight filtration coming from the p‑adic Hodge theory of the irregular connections. The section conjecture for this stack identifies K-rational points (i.e., Galois representations of π1ét(X\D) with irregular singularities) with conjugacy classes of sections. Obstructions arise from the non‑abelian cohomology of the wild mapping class group and from the non‑vanishing of the irregularity invariant in the p‑adic de Rham cohomology of the connection. The work of Diao‑Lan‑Liu‑Zhu on the p‑adic Riemann‑Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections and of Heuer on the anabelian geometry of wild fundamental groups provides the technical foundation for this program. The comparison with the complex analytic wild fundamental group via the p‑adic Simpson correspondence of Heuer‑Lan‑Liu‑Zhu reveals that the Stokes matrices appear as the monodromy of the p‑adic period map on the moduli stack of irregular connections.

Worked Example: Section Obstruction in Wild Anabelian Geometry
Let X = ℙ1K {0, 1, ∞} over a p‑adic field K with p > 2. Consider the moduli stack LocSysSL2,irr(X, {0, ∞}) of rank‑2 p‑adic local systems with irregular singularity of Poincaré rank 1 at 0 and regular singularity at ∞. Describe the obstruction class in H1(Gal(K/K), π1proét(LocSysSL2,irr,η)) for a section corresponding to a Galois representation with prescribed Stokes matrix S = (1 1; 0 1) at 0.
The geometric fundamental group π1proét(LocSysSL2,irr,η) is an extension of the mapping class group Γ0,3 (which is trivial for ℙ1 with three punctures) by the wild fundamental group π1wild(X, {0, ∞}). The latter is generated by the tame inertia at ∞ and the wild inertia at 0, with the Stokes matrix S encoding the wild monodromy. A section s : Gal(K/K) → π1proét(LocSysSL2,irr) lifts the outer Galois action. The obstruction class obs(s) ∈ H1(Gal(K/K), π1proét(LocSysSL2,irr,η)) is computed via the Kummer map associated to the exact sequence. Concretely, the prescribed Stokes matrix S determines a 1‑cocycle c : Gal(K/K) → π1wild by c(σ) = s(σ) · sgeom(σ)−1, where sgeom is a fixed geometric section. The cohomology class of c vanishes iff the Galois representation extends to a global section. For S = (1 1; 0 1), the obstruction lies in the subspace H1(Gal(K/K), ℤp(1)) corresponding to the upper‑triangular entry, and its non‑vanishing is equivalent to the non‑triviality of the associated p‑adic Kummer extension. By the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for GL2(K), this obstruction class matches the extension class of the corresponding (φ, Γ)‑module over the Robba ring.
Exam Strategy: Wild vs. Tame Anabelian Obstructions
When a question asks about obstructions to the section conjecture for curves with irregular singularities, distinguish the tame part (controlled by the usual mapping class group and the tame fundamental group) from the wild part (controlled by Stokes data and the wild inertia). The wild obstruction lives in the non‑abelian cohomology of the wild fundamental group, which is a pro‑p group; its abelianization gives p‑adic Kummer classes. Remember that the wild Riemann‑Hilbert correspondence identifies Stokes matrices with monodromy of the p‑adic period map — this is the key link between anabelian geometry and p‑adic Hodge theory in the irregular setting.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of Perfectoid Shimura Varieties at Infinite Level and the Kottwitz Conjecture

Let Sh(G, X) be a Shimura variety of Hodge type associated to a reductive group G over ℚ and a Shimura datum X. For a prime p where G is unramified, the inverse limit Sh(G, X)∞ = lim← Sh(G, X)Kp over all compact open subgroups Kp ⊂ G(ℚp) is a perfectoid space over the p‑adic completion of the reflex field. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1proét(Sh∞,η) → π1proét(Sh∞) → Gal(E/E) → 1, where E is the reflex field. The geometric fundamental group π1proét(Sh∞,η) is isomorphic to the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the associated complex Shimura variety, and the outer Galois action factors through the motivic Galois group of the Tannakian category of motives over E. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for Sh∞ asserts that the perfectoid Shimura variety at infinite level is determined by its arithmetic fundamental group together with the Hodge‑Tate period map πHT : Sh∞ → FlG,μ to the flag variety. This is equivalent to the Kottwitz conjecture on the description of the ℓ‑adic cohomology of Shimura varieties in terms of automorphic representations. The section conjecture for Sh∞ identifies E-rational points (i.e., special points on the Shimura variety) with conjugacy classes of sections. By the work of Kottwitz and Harris‑Taylor, such sections correspond to automorphic representations of G(𝔸) with prescribed infinitesimal character. The obstruction to a section lies in the non‑abelian Galois cohomology H1(Gal(E/E), π1proét(Sh∞,η)), which is controlled by the Kottwitz set B(G) and the Newton stratification of the special fiber. The work of Scholze on the p‑adic Langlands correspondence for GLn and of Caraiani‑Scholze on the cohomology of Shimura varieties provides the bridge between the anabelian geometry of Sh∞ and the Langlands program. The comparison with the complex uniformization via the p‑adic uniformization theorem of Rapoport‑Zink shows that the anabelian fundamental group of Sh∞ encodes the full Galois action on the tower of Shimura varieties, including the Hecke action at p.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic (φ, Γ)‑modules over the Robba Ring: The Colmez Functor and the Breuil‑Schneider Conjecture

Let K be a finite extension of ℚp and let RK be the Robba ring over K. The moduli stack (φ, Γ)‑ModK of étale (φ, Γ)‑modules over RK is an Artin stack locally of finite type over K whose K-points correspond to p‑adic Galois representations of Gal(K/K). Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1proét((φ, Γ)‑ModK,η) → π1proét((φ, Γ)‑ModK) → Gal(K/K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is isomorphic to the profinite completion of the fundamental group of the associated complex analytic space of (φ, Γ)‑modules, which is a classifying space for the group of automorphisms of the Robba ring. The Colmez functor D† : Repℚp(Gal(K/K)) → (φ, Γ)‑ModK induces a morphism of fundamental groups that is compatible with the outer Galois action. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for (φ, Γ)‑ModK asserts that the stack is determined by its arithmetic fundamental group together with the natural action of the monoid of Frobenius operators φn. This is equivalent to the Fontaine‑Mazur conjecture for p‑adic Galois representations. The section conjecture for (φ, Γ)‑ModK identifies K-rational points (i.e., isomorphism classes of étale (φ, Γ)‑modules) with conjugacy classes of sections. The Breuil‑Schneider conjecture predicts that the obstruction to a section lies in the non‑abelian Galois cohomology H1(Gal(K/K), π1proét((φ, Γ)‑ModK,η)), which is controlled by the trianguline variety and the eigenvariety of K. The work of Colmez on the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for GL2(ℚp), of Berger on the trianguline (φ, Γ)‑modules, and of Emerton‑Gee on the moduli stack of (φ, Γ)‑modules provides the technical foundation. The comparison with the p‑adic Simpson correspondence of Faltings and of Abbes‑Gros‑Tsuji shows that the anabelian fundamental group of (φ, Γ)‑ModK encodes the full p‑adic Hodge theory of Galois representations, including the Hodge‑Tate weights and the filtered (φ, N)‑module structure.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Higher‑Dimensional Varieties: The Non‑abelian Poincaré Lemma and Simpson's Correspondence

Let X be a smooth projective variety of dimension d ≥ 2 over a p‑adic field K. The moduli stack LocSysG(X) of p‑adic local systems on X is an Artin stack locally of finite type over K. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1proét(LocSysG,η) → π1proét(LocSysG) → Gal(K/K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is an extension of the mapping class group of X (which is trivial for simply connected X) by the pro‑étale fundamental group of the moduli stack of local systems on the universal cover of X. The non‑abelian Poincaré lemma of Simpson and its p‑adic analogue of Diao‑Lan‑Liu‑Zhu identify the category of p‑adic local systems on X with the category of Higgs bundles with vanishing p‑adic Chern classes. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for LocSysG(X) asserts that the variety X can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group of the moduli stack together with the natural action of the Hecke correspondences. This fails in general for d ≥ 2 because the moduli stack is not hyperbolic; the obstruction is measured by the non‑vanishing of the higher homotopy groups of the moduli stack. The section conjecture for LocSysG(X) identifies K-rational points (i.e., Galois representations of π1ét(X)) with conjugacy classes of sections. Obstructions arise from the non‑abelian cohomology of the mapping class group and from the failure of the p‑adic Simpson correspondence to be fully faithful for non‑rigid local systems. The work of Pridham on the étale homotopy type of moduli stacks, of Toën‑Vezzosi on derived algebraic geometry, and of Fargues‑Scholze on the geometrization of the local Langlands correspondence provides tools to approach the conjecture via the pro‑étale homotopy type of the derived moduli stack of local systems. The comparison with the complex non‑abelian Hodge theory of Simpson shows that the anabelian fundamental group of LocSysG(X) encodes the mixed Hodge structure on the cohomology of X and the action of the motivic Galois group.

  • Confusing the pro‑étale fundamental group of the Rapoport‑Zink space at infinite level with the étale fundamental group of a finite‑level Shimura variety; the former is perfectoid and captures the full Hecke action at p, the latter only the prime‑to‑p Hecke action.
  • Assuming the wild Riemann‑Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections is a straightforward extension of the regular singular case; the Stokes data introduces a non‑abelian cohomology obstruction that has no analogue in the tame setting.
  • Treating the Kottwitz conjecture as a purely cohomological statement without its anabelian interpretation; the conjecture is equivalent to the anabelian reconstruction of the perfectoid Shimura variety at infinite level from its fundamental group.
  • Neglecting the role of the Colmez functor in the anabelian geometry of (φ, Γ)‑modules; it is not merely an equivalence of categories but a morphism of moduli stacks that induces a map on fundamental groups compatible with the Galois action.
  • Equating the anabelian fundamental group of the moduli stack of local systems on a higher‑dimensional variety with the fundamental group of the variety itself; the former is a much larger object that encodes the mapping class group and the non‑abelian Hodge theory of the variety.
  • Overlooking the distinction between the Breuil‑Schneider conjecture (on the trianguline variety) and the Fontaine‑Mazur conjecture (on geometric Galois representations); the former is a local statement about (φ, Γ)‑modules, the latter a global statement about Galois representations of number fields.
  • Forgetting that the non‑abelian Poincaré lemma in p‑adic Hodge theory requires the variety to be proper and smooth; for non‑proper varieties, the logarithmic version introduces additional monodromy data at the boundary.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Shtukas on the Fargues–Fontaine Curve and the Geometrization of the Local Langlands Correspondence

The Fargues–Fontaine curve XFF = XC♭ / φℤ attached to a perfectoid field C over ℚp is a complete, regular, noetherian scheme of dimension one whose closed points correspond to untilts of C. The moduli stack ShtG,μ(XFF) of G-shtukas with one leg bounded by a minuscule cocharacter μ is a locally spatial diamond over Spd ℚp whose ℓ-adic cohomology realizes the local Langlands correspondence for the reductive group G over ℚp. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into an exact sequence 1 → π1proét(ShtG,μ, η̄) → π1proét(ShtG,μ) → Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) → 1 where the geometric fundamental group encodes the Hecke action at p and the Bernstein center. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for this stack asserts that the pair (G, μ) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group together with the natural action of the Hecke correspondences HG and the Frobenius correspondence. This is known for G = GLn by the work of Fargues–Scholze on the geometrization of the local Langlands correspondence, which identifies the category of smooth representations of G(ℚp) on ℚ̄ℓ-vector spaces with the category of ℓ-adic sheaves on the stack of G-shtukas. The fundamental group of the moduli stack of shtukas on the Fargues–Fontaine curve thus provides an anabelian avatar of the local Langlands correspondence, where the Galois action on the fundamental group corresponds to the L-parameter of the representation. The section conjecture for this stack identifies ℚp-rational points (i.e., L-parameters) with conjugacy classes of sections of the fundamental group exact sequence, and the obstruction to the existence of a section is measured by the non‑abelian cohomology of the geometric fundamental group with coefficients in the center of the dual group Ĝ.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Eigenvarieties for Reductive Groups and the p‑adic Langlands Program

For a reductive group G over ℚp and a tame level Kp ⊂ G(𝔸fp), the eigenvariety ℰG(Kp) is a rigid analytic space over ℚp parameterizing finite‑slope systems of Hecke eigenvalues occurring in the coherent cohomology of the locally symmetric space for G. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1proét(ℰG, η̄) → π1proét(ℰG) → Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is an extension of the Hecke algebra at p (completed at the finite‑slope condition) by the fundamental group of the universal cover of the eigenvariety, which is related to the Galois deformation ring of the associated Galois representation. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for eigenvarieties asserts that the eigenvariety can be recovered from its arithmetic fundamental group together with the action of the Hecke operators at p and the weight space 𝒲 = Homcont(T(ℤp), 𝔾m) where T is a maximal torus of G. This is closely related to the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence: the fiber of the eigenvariety at a classical point corresponds to the p‑adic Banach space representation of G(ℚp) attached to the Galois representation by the correspondence. The section conjecture for the eigenvariety identifies classical points (i.e., automorphic Galois representations) with conjugacy classes of sections that are compatible with the weight map ℰG → 𝒲. Obstructions arise from the non‑abelian cohomology of the geometric fundamental group with coefficients in the adjoint representation of the dual group, and from the failure of the eigenvariety to be proper (the boundary of the weight space introduces additional monodromy). The work of Emerton on the p‑adic Langlands program for GL2(ℚp), of Bellaïche–Chenevier on the eigenvariety for GLn, and of Hansen on the geometry of eigenvarieties provides the technical foundation. The comparison with the complex eigenvariety (the Coleman–Mazur eigencurve) shows that the anabelian fundamental group of the p‑adic eigenvariety encodes the p‑adic variation of Hodge–Tate weights and the p‑adic L-functions interpolating critical values of complex L-functions.

Worked Example: Section Obstruction on the Eigencurve
Let ℰ be the Coleman–Mazur eigencurve for GL2/ℚ at tame level 1, and let x ∈ ℰ(ℚ̄p) be a classical point corresponding to a p‑ordinary eigenform f of weight k ≥ 2. The associated Galois representation ρf : Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) → GL2(ℚ̄p) is crystalline at p with Hodge–Tate weights (0, k−1). Explain why the section sx : Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) → π1proét(ℰ) induced by x may fail to extend to a global section over Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ), and identify the cohomological obstruction.
The point x gives a local section sx over the decomposition group at p. To extend it globally, one must lift it across the exact sequence 1 → π1proét(ℰℚ̄) → π1proét(ℰ) → Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ) → 1. The obstruction lies in the non‑abelian cohomology set H1(Gal(ℚ̄/ℚ), π1proét(ℰℚ̄)). Concretely, π1proét(ℰℚ̄) is an extension of the completed Hecke algebra at p by the fundamental group of the universal cover. The obstruction class measures the failure of the local Galois representation ρf|Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) to be the restriction of a global automorphic representation satisfying the finite‑slope condition at all primes. By the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture, this obstruction vanishes precisely when ρf is geometric (unramified outside a finite set, de Rham at p). For p‑ordinary forms, the obstruction is controlled by the Selmer group H1f(ℚ, ad0 ρf) where ad0 is the trace‑zero adjoint representation. If this Selmer group is non‑zero, there exist deformations of ρf that are locally geometric but not globally automorphic, and the section sx does not extend.
Exam Strategy: Eigenvariety Fundamental Groups
When a question asks about the fundamental group of an eigenvariety, focus on three structural facts: (1) the exact sequence with Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp), (2) the geometric fundamental group as an extension of the completed Hecke algebra by the universal cover's fundamental group, (3) the section conjecture identifying classical points with sections compatible with the weight map. Do not confuse the eigenvariety's fundamental group with that of the Shimura variety — the former captures p‑adic variation, the latter prime‑to‑p Hecke action. Remember that the obstruction to global sections is measured by Selmer groups of the adjoint representation, linking anabelian geometry to the Bloch–Kato conjecture.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic (φ, Γ)-modules over the Robba Ring with the Kedlaya Slope Filtration and the Berger–Colmez Equivalence

The Robba ring ℛ = ℛℚp is the ring of germs of analytic functions on an open annulus of inner radius < 1 and outer radius 1 over ℚp. The category (φ, Γ)-Modℛ of étale (φ, Γ)-modules over ℛ is equivalent to the category of p‑adic Galois representations of Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) via the Fontaine equivalence, refined by Berger and Colmez. The moduli stack ℳ(φ,Γ) of (φ, Γ)-modules over ℛ is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℚp whose points correspond to (φ, Γ)-modules with fixed rank. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1proét(ℳ(φ,Γ), η̄) → π1proét(ℳ(φ,Γ)) → Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is governed by the Kedlaya slope filtration theorem: every (φ, Γ)-module over ℛ admits a unique filtration by saturated submodules whose successive quotients are pure of a given slope. This filtration induces a stratification of the moduli stack by Harder–Narasimhan polygons, and the fundamental group of each stratum is an extension of a torus (the slope torus) by a unipotent group (the monodromy of the filtration). The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for ℳ(φ,Γ) asserts that the Robba ring ℛ and the operators (φ, Γ) can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group together with the natural action of the slope filtration monoid. The section conjecture identifies ℚp-rational points (i.e., Galois representations) with conjugacy classes of sections that respect the slope filtration. Obstructions arise from the non‑abelian cohomology of the slope torus and from the failure of the slope filtration to split globally. The work of Kedlaya on the slope filtration, of Berger on the equivalence with (φ, Γ)-modules over the Robba ring, and of Colmez on the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence for GL2(ℚp) provides the technical foundation. The comparison with the moduli stack of filtered (φ, N)-modules (the Breuil–Kisin stack) shows that the anabelian fundamental group of ℳ(φ,Γ) encodes the full p‑adic Hodge theory of Galois representations, including the Hodge–Tate weights, the filtered (φ, N)-module structure, and the monodromy operator N.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Galois Representations of the Absolute Galois Group of a Number Field and the Fontaine–Mazur Conjecture

Let K be a number field and G a reductive group over ℚp. The moduli stack ℛG(K) of continuous representations ρ : Gal(K̄/K) → G(ℚ̄p) that are unramified outside a finite set of places and de Rham at all places above p is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℚp. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1proét(ℛG, η̄) → π1proét(ℛG) → Gal(K̄/K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is an extension of the global Hecke algebra (away from p) by the fundamental group of the universal deformation ring of the residual representation. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for ℛG(K) asserts that the number field K and the group G can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group together with the action of the Hecke operators at all places and the local deformation conditions at places above p. This is the anabelian formulation of the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture: a p‑adic Galois representation of Gal(K̄/K) comes from geometry (i.e., occurs in the étale cohomology of a smooth projective variety over K) if and only if the corresponding point of ℛG(K) lifts to a section of the fundamental group exact sequence that is compatible with the local p‑adic Hodge theory conditions (crystalline, semi‑stable, de Rham) at all places above p. The section conjecture for ℛG(K) identifies geometric Galois representations with conjugacy classes of sections that satisfy the local p‑adic Hodge theory constraints. Obstructions arise from the non‑abelian cohomology of the geometric fundamental group with coefficients in the adjoint representation of the dual group, and from the failure of the local‑global principle for Galois deformations (the Selmer group H1f(K, ad ρ)). The work of Mazur on deformation rings, of Kisin on the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture for GL2, and of Calegari–Geraghty on the Taylor–Wiles method for higher rank provides the technical foundation. The comparison with the moduli stack of automorphic representations (the eigenvariety) shows that the anabelian fundamental group of ℛG(K) encodes the global Langlands correspondence, the p‑adic L-functions, and the Bloch–Kato Selmer groups.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Period Domains for Hodge–Tate Representations and the Griffiths Period Map in p‑adic Hodge Theory

Let G be a reductive group over ℚp and μ a minuscule cocharacter of G. The p‑adic period domain ℱG,μ is the flag variety of G over ℚp parameterizing filtrations on the standard representation of G of type μ. The moduli stack ℳHT(G, μ) of Hodge–Tate representations with Hodge–Tate weights given by μ is an Artin stack over ℚp whose points correspond to pairs (ρ, Fil) where ρ : Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) → G(ℚ̄p) is a de Rham representation and Fil is the Hodge filtration on the associated filtered (φ, N)-module. Its pro‑étale fundamental group fits into 1 → π1proét(ℳHT, η̄) → π1proét(ℳHT) → Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is an extension of the Griffiths period map ℱG,μ → ℬdR+ (where ℬdR is Fontaine's de Rham period ring) by the fundamental group of the moduli stack of filtered (φ, N)-modules. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for ℳHT asserts that the pair (G, μ) and the period domain ℱG,μ can be recovered from the arithmetic fundamental group together with the natural action of the Griffiths period map and the Hodge–Tate weight monoid. The section conjecture identifies de Rham representations with Hodge–Tate weights μ with conjugacy classes of sections that are compatible with the period map. Obstructions arise from the non‑abelian cohomology of the geometric fundamental group with coefficients in the Lie algebra of G, and from the failure of the period map to be injective (the Griffiths transversality condition). The work of Fontaine on period rings, of Faltings on the p‑adic Simpson correspondence, and of Scholze on the p‑adic period map for Shimura varieties provides the technical foundation. The comparison with the complex period domain (the Griffiths period map for variations of Hodge structure) shows that the anabelian fundamental group of ℳHT encodes the p‑adic Hodge theory of Galois representations, including the Hodge–Tate decomposition, the filtered (φ, N)-module structure, and the monodromy operator N.

  • Confusing the moduli stack of p‑adic shtukas on the Fargues–Fontaine curve with the moduli stack of local shtukas over function fields; the former is a diamond over Spd ℚp and captures the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence, the latter is a scheme over a finite field and captures the function field Langlands correspondence.
  • Assuming the eigenvariety is proper or that its fundamental group is simply the Galois group of the base field; the eigenvariety is non‑proper, its boundary contributes monodromy, and its geometric fundamental group contains the completed Hecke algebra at p.
  • Neglecting the Kedlaya slope filtration when analyzing the fundamental group of the moduli stack of (φ, Γ)-modules; the slope filtration induces a stratification whose strata have fundamental groups that are extensions of slope tori by unipotent groups, and this structure is essential for anabelian reconstruction.
  • Equating the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture with the statement that all geometric Galois representations are automorphic; the Fontaine–Mazur conjecture is a necessary condition (geometric implies automorphic), but the converse (automorphic implies geometric) is the Langlands correspondence, and the anabelian formulation distinguishes these via the section conjecture on the moduli stack of Galois representations.
  • Overlooking the role of the Griffiths period map in the anabelian geometry of Hodge–Tate period domains; the period map is not merely a morphism of varieties but a map of moduli stacks that induces a map on fundamental groups compatible with the Hodge filtration, and its non‑injectivity (Griffiths transversality) is the source of the obstruction to the section conjecture.
  • Treating the moduli stack of p‑adic Galois representations of a number field as a fine moduli space; it is an Artin stack with non‑trivial automorphisms (the centralizer of the representation), and its fundamental group must be taken in the pro‑étale or 2‑categorical sense to capture the full anabelian information.
  • Forgetting that the anabelian fundamental group of the moduli stack of p‑adic period domains encodes the monodromy operator N of the filtered (φ, N)-module; the period domain only sees the Hodge filtration, but the fundamental group of the moduli stack of Hodge–Tate representations also remembers the monodromy via the extension structure of the geometric fundamental group.

The Pro‑unipotent Fundamental Group and p‑adic Iterated Integrals in Anabelian Geometry

The pro‑unipotent completion of the étale fundamental group provides a linearised avatar of the full anabelian fundamental group that is amenable to explicit computation via p‑adic iterated integrals. Let X be a smooth hyperbolic curve over a p‑adic field K. The pro‑unipotent étale fundamental group π₁^un(Xbar K, b) is the inverse limit of unipotent quotients of the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the associated complex curve, equipped with its natural Galois action. Its Lie algebra is the free pronilpotent Lie algebra on H¹_ét(Xbar K, ℚ_p)(1) modulo the single relation given by the cup product pairing, a consequence of the formality of the étale homotopy type of curves established by Hain and Matsumoto. The Galois action on this Lie algebra factors through the Galois representation on the Tate module of the Jacobian and the higher non‑abelian Galois cohomology groups H¹(Gal(bar K/K), 𝔲_n) where 𝔲_n is the n‑th nilpotent quotient of the Lie algebra. The p‑adic iterated integrals of Coleman provide explicit coordinates on the pro‑unipotent fundamental group: for a tuple of differential forms ω₁,…,ω_r on X, the iterated integral ∫_b^x ω₁⋯ω_r defines a locally analytic function on the p‑adic universal cover, and the collection of all such integrals yields the p‑adic period map from the pro‑unipotent fundamental group to the de Rham fundamental group. The comparison isomorphism between the étale and de Rham pro‑unipotent fundamental groups, constructed via p‑adic Hodge theory (Fontaine’s B_dR, the p‑adic Simpson correspondence of Faltings and Shiho), identifies the Galois action on the étale side with the monodromy action on the de Rham side. This identification is the engine of the non‑abelian Chabauty method of Kim: the Selmer variety Sel_n(X/K) parametrising extensions of the trivial Galois representation by the n‑th nilpotent quotient embeds into the de Rham moduli space via the p‑adic period map, and the image of rational points X(K) under the p‑adic iterated integral map lies in the intersection of the Selmer variety with the de Rham path torsor. The dimension of this intersection is controlled by the Bloch–Kato Selmer groups H¹_f(G_K, 𝔲_n), and the method succeeds when the dimension of the Selmer variety is strictly smaller than the dimension of the de Rham moduli space, a condition verified for many modular curves and Fermat curves by work of Balakrishnan, Dogra, Müller, Tuitman and others. The pro‑unipotent fundamental group also carries a natural mixed Hodge structure in the complex setting and a mixed p‑adic Hodge structure in the p‑adic setting; the weight filtration corresponds to the lower central series, and the Hodge filtration corresponds to the filtration by iterated integrals of differential forms of increasing length. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for the pro‑unipotent fundamental group asserts that the curve X can be recovered from the Galois module π₁^un(Xbar K, b) together with the Hodge filtration on its de Rham realisation; this is known for hyperbolic curves over number fields by work of Hain and Matsumoto, and for p‑adic fields by work of Kim and Wojtkowiak using the p‑adic period map and the theory of p‑adic multiple zeta values.

The Tannakian Fundamental Group and the Reconstruction of Tannakian Categories from Fundamental Groups

The Tannakian formalism provides a categorical framework that translates the anabelian reconstruction problem into the language of neutral Tannakian categories and their fundamental groups. Let k be a field of characteristic zero, and let C be a neutral Tannakian category over k with fibre functor ω: C → Vec_k. The Tannakian fundamental group π₁^Tan(C, ω) is the affine group scheme of tensor automorphisms of ω; it is a pro‑algebraic group over k, and the category C is equivalent to the category of finite‑dimensional representations of π₁^Tan(C, ω). In anabelian geometry, the fundamental group of a variety X (étale, de Rham, crystalline, or motivic) is naturally the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of corresponding local systems (ℓ‑adic, de Rham, crystalline, or motivic). The anabelian reconstruction conjecture can be reformulated as: the variety X can be recovered from the Tannakian category of its local systems together with the fibre functor given by the stalk at a base point. The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT acts on the Tannakian fundamental group of the projective line minus three points by outer automorphisms, and this action lifts to an action on the Tannakian category of mixed Tate motives over ℤ, which is the category of unipotent variations of mixed Hodge–Tate structures on ℙ¹{0,1,∞}. The Tannakian fundamental group of this category is the pro‑unipotent completion of the fundamental group of ℙ¹{0,1,∞}, and its Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on two generators modulo the Ihara relation, which is the infinitesimal version of the GT relations. The reconstruction of the Tannakian category from its fundamental group is governed by the Tannakian duality theorem of Saavedra Rivano and Deligne: a neutral Tannakian category is equivalent to Rep_k(G) for a unique affine group scheme G, and the fibre functor corresponds to the forgetful functor. In the anabelian context, the fibre functor is not canonical; it depends on the choice of a base point, and different base points give isomorphic but not canonically isomorphic fibre functors. The set of isomorphisms between two fibre functors is a torsor under the fundamental group, and this torsor structure is the categorical avatar of the path torsor in the fundamental groupoid. The anabelian reconstruction of a hyperbolic curve X from its étale fundamental group π₁^ét(X) can be seen as the reconstruction of the Tannakian category of finite étale covers of X from its fundamental group, which is automatic by Galois theory. The deeper problem is the reconstruction of X from the Tannakian category of ℓ‑adic local systems on X, which is equivalent to the category of continuous representations of π₁^ét(X) on finite‑dimensional ℚ_ℓ‑vector spaces. The Neukirch–Uchida theorem for number fields and the Mochizuki theorem for hyperbolic curves over p‑adic fields show that the absolute Galois group (resp. the arithmetic fundamental group) determines the field (resp. the curve) up to isomorphism. In the Tannakian language, this means that the Tannakian category of Galois representations (resp. arithmetic local systems) with its fibre functor determines the base scheme. The section conjecture for the Tannakian fundamental group asks whether a section of the exact sequence 1 → π₁^geom → π₁^arith → Gal(k) → 1 corresponds to a rational point; in the Tannakian setting, a section gives a fibre functor on the geometric category that is Galois‑equivariant, and the conjecture asserts that such fibre functors come from rational points. The work of Deligne on the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of mixed Tate motives, of Goncharov on the motivic fundamental group and multiple zeta values, and of Brown on the Tannakian structure of the category of mixed Tate motives over ℤ provides the motivic foundation for the anabelian programme.

Worked Example: Pro‑unipotent Fundamental Group of ℙ¹{0,1,∞}
Compute the Lie algebra of the pro‑unipotent étale fundamental group of X = ℙ¹{0,1,∞} over ℚ_p, describe its Galois action, and explain how the p‑adic iterated integrals ∫0^z (dt/t)^a (dt/(1-t))^b give coordinates on the de Rham fundamental group.
The topological fundamental group of X(ℂ) is the free group F₂ on two loops γ₀ around 0 and γ₁ around 1. Its pro‑unipotent completion over ℚ_p has Lie algebra 𝔩 = Lie(π₁^un(Xbar ℚ_p, b)) which is the free pronilpotent Lie algebra on two generators e₀, e₁ (dual to the differential forms dt/t and dt/(1-t)) modulo the single relation [e₀, e₁] = 0 in the abelianisation, but the full Lie algebra is free on e₀, e₁ because H²(X, ℚ_p) = 0. The lower central series filtration gives weight grading: weight n piece is spanned by iterated commutators of length n. The Galois group G_ℚ = Gal(bar ℚ/ℚ) acts on π₁^un via its action on the étale fundamental group; on the Lie algebra this action factors through the cyclotomic character on the abelianisation (e₀, e₁ have weight 1 and transform by χ_cyc) and through higher non‑abelian Galois cohomology on higher weights. The p‑adic iterated integrals: for a word w in {0,1}, define I_w(z) = ∫0^z ω_w where ω0 = dt/t, ω1 = dt/(1-t). These converge for z ∈ ℙ¹(ℂ_p){0,1,∞} and satisfy the shuffle product relations. The map z ↦ (I_w(z))_w gives the p‑adic period map from X(ℂ_p) to the de Rham fundamental group (the affine scheme of group‑like series in ℚ_p⟨⟨e₀,e₁⟩⟩). The comparison isomorphism identifies the Galois action on the étale side with the monodromy action on the de Rham side: for σ ∈ G_ℚ, σ(I_w(z)) = I_w(σ(z)) + (periods of σ), where the periods are p‑adic multiple zeta values. The Selmer variety Sel_n(X/ℚ) is the subscheme of the de Rham moduli space cut out by the condition that the corresponding Galois representation is crystalline at p and unramified outside p; Kim's non‑abelian Chabauty shows X(ℚ) maps into Sel_n ∩ path torsor.
Exam Strategy: Tannakian vs. Pro‑unipotent
When a question asks for the "Tannakian fundamental group", it expects the affine group scheme of tensor automorphisms of a fibre functor on a neutral Tannakian category. When it asks for the "pro‑unipotent fundamental group", it expects the inverse limit of unipotent quotients of the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group, or its Malcev completion. The two coincide for the category of unipotent local systems, but the Tannakian formalism applies to any neutral Tannakian category (e.g., all ℓ‑adic local systems, mixed Tate motives). Memorise: Tannakian = categorical, pro‑unipotent = analytic/linearised. The GT group acts on the pro‑unipotent fundamental group of ℙ¹{0,1,∞} by outer automorphisms; this action is the same as its action on the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of mixed Tate motives.

The Nori Fundamental Group and the Category of Nori Motives in Anabelian Geometry

The Nori fundamental group provides a purely algebraic construction of a fundamental group scheme that classifies finite‑dimensional representations arising from algebraic vector bundles with connection, without any analytic or ℓ‑adic input. Let X be a smooth variety over a field k of characteristic zero, and let x be a k‑rational point. Nori considers the category N(X, x) whose objects are pairs (E, ∇, v) where (E, ∇) is a vector bundle with an integrable connection on X, and v ∈ E_x is a horizontal section generating E as an O_X‑module under the action of the connection. Morphisms are morphisms of vector bundles with connection preserving the generating sections. This category is a neutral Tannakian category over k with fibre functor ω(E, ∇, v) = the fibre of E at x (or simply the vector space generated by v). The Nori fundamental group π₁^N(X, x) is the Tannakian fundamental group of this category; it is a pro‑algebraic group scheme over k. The crucial theorem of Nori states that the category of finite‑dimensional representations of π₁^N(X, x) is equivalent to the category of "Nori motives" M^N(X) — the full subcategory of the category of algebraic vector bundles with connection generated by the objects (E, ∇, v) as above. In particular, π₁^N(X, x) is the motivic fundamental group in the sense of Nori. For a hyperbolic curve X over a number field, the Nori fundamental group is conjecturally related to the motivic fundamental group of Deligne–Goncharov and the pro‑unipotent étale fundamental group. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for the Nori fundamental group asks whether X can be recovered from π₁^N(X, x) as a Tannakian category with fibre functor. Nori proved that for a smooth projective curve of genus g ≥ 2 over ℂ, the Nori fundamental group determines the curve up to isomorphism, using the fact that the category of vector bundles with connection on a curve is equivalent to the category of representations of the topological fundamental group (Riemann–Hilbert correspondence). In positive characteristic, the Nori fundamental group is replaced by the fundamental group scheme of the category of stratified bundles (vector bundles with a compatible system of Frobenius pullbacks), and the anabelian reconstruction fails in general due to the existence of non‑trivial stratified bundles with trivial monodromy (the "Frobenius descent" obstruction). The Nori fundamental group also appears in the function field anabelian geometry of Tamagawa: for a hyperbolic curve over a finite field, the Nori fundamental group scheme (or its tame version) captures the tame fundamental group and the action of Frobenius. The relationship between the Nori fundamental group and the étale fundamental group is mediated by the comparison isomorphism in p‑adic Hodge theory: for a variety over a p‑adic field, the category of de Rham local systems is a full subcategory of the category of vector bundles with connection, and the Nori fundamental group maps to the de Rham fundamental group. The section conjecture for the Nori fundamental group would assert that rational points correspond to splittings of the exact sequence 1 → π₁^N(Xbar k, x) → π₁^N(X, x) → Gal(k) → 1 that are compatible with the Hodge filtration. This is a motivic version of the Grothendieck section conjecture, and it is known in very few cases (e.g., for ℙ¹{0,1,∞} by work of Deligne and Goncharov on multiple zeta values). The Nori fundamental group is also central to the construction of the motivic fundamental group of Ayoub in stable motivic homotopy theory, where it appears as the fundamental group of the motivic homotopy type of X.

The Crystalline Fundamental Group and the Hyodo–Kato Isomorphism in Anabelian Geometry

The crystalline fundamental group provides a p‑adic Hodge‑theoretic fundamental group that captures the crystalline Galois representations of the fundamental group of a variety over a p‑adic field. Let X be a smooth proper variety over a p‑adic field K with ring of integers O_K and perfect residue field k. Assume X has semi‑stable reduction over O_K, so there exists a semi‑stable model 𝔛 over O_K with special fibre X_k a reduced divisor with normal crossings. The log‑crystalline cohomology of X_k/W(k) (where W(k) is the ring of Witt vectors) computes the p‑adic étale cohomology of Xbar K via the Hyodo–Kato isomorphism: H^i_log‑crys(X_k/W(k)) ⊗W(k) B_st ≅ H^i_ét(Xbar K, ℚ_p) ⊗ℚ_p B_st, compatible with Galois action, Frobenius, and monodromy operator N. The crystalline fundamental group π₁^crys(X/K, x) is the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of crystalline local systems on X — i.e., ℓ‑adic local systems that are crystalline at all places above p — or equivalently, the category of isocrystals with Frobenius and monodromy on the log‑crystalline site of X_k. Its Lie algebra is a filtered (φ, N)‑module over K, and the Hyodo–Kato isomorphism identifies it with the de Rham fundamental group π₁^dR(X/K, x) equipped with its Hodge filtration and the monodromy operator N coming from the log structure. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for the crystalline fundamental group asserts that the semi‑stable model 𝔛 (and hence the variety X) can be recovered from the crystalline fundamental group together with its (φ, N)‑module structure and the Hyodo–Kato isomorphism to the de Rham fundamental group. This is known for curves by work of Shiho, who constructed the crystalline fundamental group as a pro‑algebraic group scheme over K and proved that it determines the curve when the reduction is semi‑stable. The section conjecture for the crystalline fundamental group asks whether a section of the exact sequence 1 → π₁^crys(Xbar K, x) → π₁^crys(X/K, x) → Gal(K) → 1 corresponds to a K‑rational point. The obstruction lies in the non‑abelian Galois cohomology H¹(Gal(K), π₁^crys(Xbar K, x)), which is controlled by the filtered (φ, N)‑module structure via the Bloch–Kato exponential map. The crystalline fundamental group is also the natural home for the p‑adic period map of Faltings: the period map from the moduli space of crystalline representations to the flag variety of Hodge filtrations factors through the crystalline fundamental group. In the context of the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence, the crystalline fundamental group of the modular curve (or the eigencurve) encodes the crystalline Galois representations associated to modular forms, and the Hyodo–Kato isomorphism relates their (φ, N)‑module structure to the Hodge filtration on the de Rham cohomology of the modular curve. The work of Fontaine on period rings, of Kato on log‑crystalline cohomology, of Hyodo on the isomorphism, and of Faltings on the p‑adic Simpson correspondence provides the technical foundation. A common pitfall is to confuse the crystalline fundamental group (which sees only crystalline representations) with the de Rham fundamental group (which sees all de Rham representations); the former is a quotient of the latter, and the kernel is the fundamental group of the moduli space of non‑crystalline de Rham representations. Another pitfall is to assume the Hyodo–Kato isomorphism holds without semi‑stable reduction; it requires a semi‑stable model, and for general varieties one must use the theory of log‑crystalline cohomology with the log structure given by the divisor at infinity.

The Ayoub Motivic Fundamental Group and Stable Motivic Homotopy Theory in Anabelian Geometry

The Ayoub motivic fundamental group places the anabelian fundamental group inside the framework of stable motivic homotopy theory, where it becomes the fundamental group of the motivic homotopy type of a scheme. Let k be a perfect field, and let SH(k) be the stable motivic homotopy category of Morel–Voevodsky. For a smooth scheme X over k with a rational point x, the motivic homotopy type M(X) is an object in the unstable motivic homotopy category H(k), and its suspension spectrum Σ^∞_T M(X) is an object in SH(k). Ayoub constructs the motivic fundamental group π₁^mot(X, x) as the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of "motivic local systems" on X — i.e., the category of modules over the motivic homotopy type in SH(k) that are locally constant in the Nisnevich topology. This category is a neutral Tannakian category over the motivic coefficient ring (the endomorphism ring of the unit in SH(k)), and its Tannakian fundamental group is a pro‑algebraic group scheme over this ring. The key theorem of Ayoub is that the motivic fundamental group admits realisation functors to the étale fundamental group (via the ℓ‑adic realisation functor SH(k) → D(ℤ_ℓ)), the de Rham fundamental group (via the de Rham realisation), the crystalline fundamental group (via the crystalline realisation), and the Betti fundamental group (via the Betti realisation when k ⊂ ℂ). These realisations are compatible with the comparison isomorphisms of p‑adic Hodge theory. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture in this setting asserts that the motivic homotopy type M(X) (and hence the scheme X) can be recovered from the motivic fundamental group π₁^mot(X, x) together with its realisations. This is a vast generalisation of the Grothendieck anabelian conjecture, which only concerns the étale fundamental group. The section conjecture for the motivic fundamental group asks whether a section of the exact sequence 1 → π₁^mot(Xbar k, x) → π₁^mot(X, x) → Gal(k) → 1 corresponds to a rational point. The motivic fundamental group of ℙ¹{0,1,∞} is the motivic avatar of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group; its Lie algebra is the free Lie algebra on two generators modulo the motivic Ihara relation, and its coordinate ring is the ring of motivic multiple zeta values of Brown. The action of the motivic Galois group (the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of mixed Tate motives over ℤ) on the motivic fundamental group of ℙ¹{0,1,∞} is the motivic version of the GT action. The work of Ayoub, Cisinski–Déglise, and Hoyois on the six‑functor formalism in SH(k) provides the technical foundation for constructing the motivic fundamental group and its realisations. A common trap is to confuse the motivic fundamental group (which lives in the stable motivic homotopy category and sees all cohomology theories simultaneously) with the pro‑unipotent fundamental group (which is a single realisation, usually de Rham or étale). Another trap is to assume the motivic fundamental group is always pro‑unipotent; it is pro‑unipotent only for varieties whose motivic homotopy type is mixed Tate (e.g., ℙ¹{0,1,∞}, moduli spaces M0,n), but for general hyperbolic curves it has a non‑unipotent part coming from the Jacobian. The motivic fundamental group also carries a weight filtration (from the motivic t‑structure) and a Hodge filtration (from the de Rham realisation), and the anabelian reconstruction uses both.

  • Confusing the pro‑unipotent fundamental group (a linearised quotient of the étale fundamental group) with the full étale fundamental group (which is profinite and non‑linear); the pro‑unipotent completion loses all finite non‑abelian information.
  • Assuming the Tannakian fundamental group of the category of all ℓ‑adic local systems is the same as the pro‑algebraic completion of the étale fundamental group; the former is the Tannakian fundamental group of Repℚ_ℓ(π₁^ét), which is the pro‑algebraic completion only when the category is generated by finite‑dimensional representations (true for curves, false for higher‑dimensional varieties with infinite π₁).
  • Neglecting the dependence of the Nori fundamental group on the choice of base point and the generating section v; different choices give non‑canonically isomorphic Tannakian categories, and the anabelian reconstruction must account for the torsor of isomorphisms.
  • Thinking the Hyodo–Kato isomorphism holds for any proper variety over a p‑adic field; it requires a semi‑stable model, and without it one must use the log‑crystalline cohomology of a log‑smooth compactification, where the monodromy operator N may not be nilpotent.
  • Equating the crystalline fundamental group with the de Rham fundamental group; the crystalline fundamental group classifies only crystalline representations (Frobenius‑equivariant), while the de Rham fundamental group classifies all de Rham representations (no Frobenius required). The former is a quotient of the latter.
  • Assuming the motivic fundamental group is always pro‑unipotent; it is pro‑unipotent only for mixed Tate motives (e.g., ℙ¹{0,1,∞}, M0,n), but for a general hyperbolic curve it has a reductive part coming from the motivic fundamental group of the Jacobian.
  • Overlooking that the section conjecture for the crystalline fundamental group involves the Bloch–Kato Selmer group H¹_f(G_K, π₁^crys) and not just the naive Galois cohomology; the local condition at p (crystalline) is essential and cuts down the dimension of the Selmer variety.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Parabolic Structure and the p‑adic Non‑abelian Hodge Correspondence

The moduli stack LocGpar(X, D) of p‑adic local systems on a smooth projective curve X over a p‑adic field K with parabolic structure along a reduced divisor D = Σ xi classifies continuous representations ρ : π₁ét(X D, x̄) → G(ℚp) whose local monodromy at each xi lies in a prescribed conjugacy class of parabolic subgroups of G. The étale fundamental group of this stack, π₁ét(LocGpar(X, D), ρ), fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocGpar(XK̄, D), ρ) → π₁ét(LocGpar(X, D), ρ) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group π₁ét(LocGpar(XK̄, D), ρ) is a pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex character variety, which carries a natural mixed Hodge structure. The p‑adic non‑abelian Hodge correspondence, established by Liu–Zhu and extended to parabolic structures by Heuer, identifies the p‑adic pro‑étale homotopy type of LocGpar(X, D) with the de Rham moduli stack of parabolic Higgs bundles HiggsGpar(X, D) via the p‑adic Simpson correspondence. This equivalence is compatible with the weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion of π₁ét(LocGpar(XK̄, D), ρ) and the Hodge filtration on the de Rham fundamental group of HiggsGpar(X, D). The anabelian reconstruction conjecture in this setting asserts that the pair (X, D) together with the parabolic weights can be recovered from the Galois representation on π₁ét(LocGpar(XK̄, D), ρ) together with its Hodge–Tate decomposition. A key technical point is that the parabolic structure introduces a non‑trivial monodromy weight filtration on the nearby cycles complex, which is reflected in the graded pieces of the weight filtration on the fundamental group. The Galois action on the graded pieces recovers the parabolic weights via the p‑adic monodromy theorem of Fontaine. When G = GLn, the stack LocGLnpar(X, D) is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℤp, and its fundamental group is a pro‑algebraic group over ℚp whose Lie algebra is the parabolic Atiyah algebra of the universal local system. The section conjecture for this stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group exact sequence corresponds to a K‑point of LocGpar(X, D), i.e., a p‑adic local system with parabolic structure defined over K. The obstruction to such a section lies in the parabolic Bloch–Kato Selmer group H¹f,par(GK, π₁ét(LocGpar(XK̄, D), ρ)), which is cut out by the crystalline condition at p and the parabolic condition at the punctures.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic (φ, Γ)‑modules with Logarithmic Growth and the p‑adic Simpson Correspondence for Logarithmic Connections

The Robba ring ℛK over a p‑adic field K is the ring of functions convergent on an open annulus 0 < r < |T| < 1. A (φ, Γ)‑module over ℛK with logarithmic growth is a finite free ℛK‑module D equipped with a semilinear Frobenius φ and a continuous semilinear action of Γ = Gal(K(μp∞)/K) such that the matrix of φ and the Lie algebra action of Γ have entries in the subring ℛKlog of functions with at most logarithmic growth at the boundary |T| = 1. The moduli stack Mφ,Γlog of such objects is an Artin stack locally of finite presentation over ℚp. Its étale fundamental group π₁ét(Mφ,Γlog, D) fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(Mφ,ΓlogK̄, D) → π₁ét(Mφ,Γlog, D) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is closely related to the pro‑ℓ completion of the fundamental group of the complex moduli space of logarithmic connections on the punctured disc. The p‑adic Simpson correspondence for logarithmic connections, due to Diao–Lan–Liu–Zhu, constructs an equivalence between the derived category of (φ, Γ)‑modules with logarithmic growth and the derived category of logarithmic connections on the Fargues–Fontaine curve XFF with prescribed residues at the "infinite" point ∞. This equivalence induces an isomorphism of pro‑étale homotopy types, hence an isomorphism of étale fundamental groups π₁ét(Mφ,Γlog) ≃ π₁ét(Connlog(XFF, ∞)). The anabelian philosophy predicts that the stack Mφ,Γlog can be reconstructed from the Galois representation on π₁ét(Mφ,ΓlogK̄, D) together with its Hodge–Tate decomposition. The logarithmic growth condition corresponds under the correspondence to the condition that the connection has regular singularities at ∞ with residues in a fixed alcove of the cocharacter lattice. The Galois action on the graded pieces of the weight filtration on π₁ét(Mφ,ΓlogK̄, D) recovers these residues via the p‑adic local monodromy theorem. The Breuil–Schneider conjecture, proved by Hellmann–Schraen in the crystalline case and extended to the logarithmic growth case by Ding, asserts that the functor from p‑adic Galois representations to (φ, Γ)‑modules with logarithmic growth is fully faithful on the subcategory of de Rham representations with Hodge–Tate weights in [0, h]. The anabelian reconstruction of Mφ,Γlog from its fundamental group uses the Tannakian formalism: the category of finite‑dimensional ℚp‑representations of π₁ét(Mφ,ΓlogK̄, D) is equivalent to the category of (φ, Γ)‑modules with logarithmic growth that are "geometric" in the sense of Fontaine–Mazur. The section conjecture for this stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group exact sequence corresponds to a K‑point of Mφ,Γlog, i.e., a (φ, Γ)‑module with logarithmic growth defined over K. The obstruction lies in the Selmer variety H¹f,log(GK, π₁ét(Mφ,ΓlogK̄, D)), defined by the crystalline condition at p and the logarithmic growth condition at the boundary.

Worked Example: Parabolic Weights from Galois Action
Let X = ℙ¹ℚp {0, 1, ∞} and let ρ : π₁ét(X, x̄) → GL2(ℚp) be a p‑adic local system with parabolic structure at 0, 1, ∞ given by unipotent monodromy with a single Jordan block. The parabolic weights are α0 = α1 = α∞ = (0, 1). Explain how the Galois action on the weight filtration of π₁ét(LocGL2par(X, {0,1,∞}), ρ) recovers these weights.
The moduli stack LocGL2par(X, {0,1,∞}) classifies rank‑2 local systems with unipotent monodromy at the three punctures. Its geometric fundamental group π₁ét(LocGL2par(Xℚ̄p, {0,1,∞}), ρ) is a pro‑ℓ group whose pro‑unipotent completion carries a weight filtration W• coming from the mixed Hodge structure on the complex character variety. The graded piece Gr−1W is isomorphic to H¹(X, ad⁰ρ) where ad⁰ρ is the trace‑zero adjoint local system. The parabolic structure imposes that the residue of the connection at each puncture lies in the nilpotent cone, which translates to the condition that the monodromy weight filtration on the nearby cycles has length 2. The Galois group Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) acts on Gr−1W via the p‑adic monodromy theorem: the action of the inertia subgroup Ip is quasi‑unipotent, and the logarithm of the monodromy operator N = log(ρ(Ip)) recovers the residue of the connection. For unipotent monodromy with a single Jordan block, N has rank 1 and its eigenvalue on the graded piece is the parabolic weight. Concretely, the Hodge–Tate decomposition of Gr−1W ⊗ ℂp yields Gr−1W ⊗ ℂp ≃ ⊕i ℂp(−αi) where αi are the parabolic weights. The Galois action on ℂp(−αi) is via the cyclotomic character χpαi. Thus the weights (0, 1) at each puncture appear as the exponents of χp in the Hodge–Tate decomposition of the graded pieces of the weight filtration on the fundamental group. The anabelian reconstruction reads off the weights from the Galois representation on π₁ét(LocGL2par(Xℚ̄p, {0,1,∞}), ρ) without reference to the original curve.
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Parabolic from Irregular
In questions comparing parabolic and irregular singularities, remember: parabolic structure means regular singularities with prescribed monodromy conjugacy classes (or residues in a fixed alcove), while irregular singularities involve Stokes data and exponential growth. The p‑adic non‑abelian Hodge correspondence for parabolic structures (Liu–Zhu, Heuer) uses the usual de Rham moduli stack of parabolic Higgs bundles; for irregular singularities (Diao–Lan–Liu–Zhu) it uses the wild de Rham stack with Stokes filtrations. The weight filtration on the fundamental group is finite for parabolic, but the Stokes filtration introduces an additional grading. The Selmer condition for parabolic local systems is "crystalline at p and parabolic at punctures"; for irregular it is "crystalline at p and Stokes‑graded at punctures". Do not conflate the two.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Period Domains for de Rham Representations with Prescribed Hodge‑Tate Weights and the p‑adic Period Map

The p‑adic period domain 𝒟μ attached to a cocharacter μ : 𝔾m → G over a p‑adic field K is a rigid analytic space over K that parametrizes filtrations on the fiber functor of a reductive group G compatible with the Hodge–Tate weights prescribed by μ. When G = GLn and μ = (μ1 ≥ … ≥ μn), 𝒟μ is the flag variety of filtrations Fil• on Kn with dim Fili = #{j | μj ≥ i}. The moduli stack RepGdR,μ(ΓK) of de Rham representations of the absolute Galois group ΓK = Gal(K̄/K) with Hodge–Tate weights μ is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℚp. The p‑adic period map πHT : RepGdR,μ(ΓK) → 𝒟μ sends a de Rham representation to its Hodge–Tate filtration. The étale fundamental group π₁ét(RepGdR,μ(ΓK), ρ) fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(RepGdR,μ(ΓK̄), ρ) → π₁ét(RepGdR,μ(ΓK), ρ) → ΓK → 1. The geometric fundamental group is a pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex period domain, which is a homogeneous space G(ℂ)/P(ℂ) for a parabolic subgroup P determined by μ. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture asserts that the period domain 𝒟μ and the period map πHT can be recovered from the Galois representation on π₁ét(RepGdR,μ(ΓK̄), ρ) together with its Hodge–Tate decomposition. The key mechanism is the p‑adic Hodge theory of the universal de Rham representation: the Hodge–Tate filtration on the universal representation gives a map from the pro‑étale homotopy type of RepGdR,μ(ΓK) to the pro‑étale homotopy type of 𝒟μ. This map is an isomorphism on π₁ after pro‑ℓ completion because the fibers of πHT are simply connected (they are affine spaces). The Galois action on the graded pieces of the weight filtration on π₁ét(RepGdR,μ(ΓK̄), ρ) recovers the Hodge–Tate weights μ via the Hodge–Tate decomposition of the universal representation. Specifically, the Lie algebra of the pro‑unipotent completion of π₁ét(RepGdR,μ(ΓK̄), ρ) is the free Lie algebra on H¹(ΓK, ad⁰ρ) modulo the relations coming from the cup product, and the Hodge–Tate weights of ad⁰ρ are the differences μi − μj. The section conjecture for this stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group exact sequence corresponds to a K‑point of RepGdR,μ(ΓK), i.e., a de Rham representation with Hodge–Tate weights μ defined over K. The obstruction lies in the Bloch–Kato Selmer group H¹f(ΓK, π₁ét(RepGdR,μ(ΓK̄), ρ)), which is cut out by the crystalline condition at p. When μ is regular (all weights distinct), the period domain is a flag variety and the anabelian reconstruction is equivalent to the reconstruction of the flag variety from its fundamental group with Galois action, a result of Mochizuki in the hyperbolic curve case extended to period domains by Caraiani–Scholze.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on the Fargues–Fontaine Curve with Parabolic Structure at the "Infinite" Point and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

The Fargues–Fontaine curve XFF = Proj(⊕n≥0 BdR+ / ξn) is a complete regular curve over ℚp whose closed points correspond to untilts of perfectoid fields. The moduli stack BunGpar(XFF, ∞) of G‑bundles on XFF with parabolic structure at the "infinite" point ∞ (the point corresponding to the untilt C♭ of a fixed algebraically closed perfectoid field C) classifies G‑bundles E on XFF together with a reduction of the fiber E∞ to a parabolic subgroup P ⊂ G. The étale fundamental group π₁ét(BunGpar(XFF, ∞), E) fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(BunGpar(XFF, ∞)ℚ̄p, E) → π₁ét(BunGpar(XFF, ∞), E) → Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is closely related to the pro‑ℓ completion of the affine Grassmannian GrG = G(ℚp((t)))/G(ℚp[[t]]), which is the moduli space of G‑bundles on the formal disc with trivialization at the generic point. The parabolic structure at ∞ corresponds to a reduction to a parabolic subgroup of the loop group, i.e., a point in the partial affine flag variety. The geometric Langlands correspondence for the Fargues–Fontaine curve, conjectured by Fargues and proved in many cases by Fargues–Scholze, identifies the category of ℓ‑adic sheaves on BunG(XFF) with the category of representations of the Langlands dual group LG. The parabolic version identifies sheaves on BunGpar(XFF, ∞) with representations of LG equipped with a Whittaker model for the dual parabolic. The anabelian philosophy predicts that the stack BunGpar(XFF, ∞) can be reconstructed from the Galois representation on π₁ét(BunGpar(XFF, ∞)ℚ̄p, E) together with its Hodge–Tate decomposition. The Galois action on the graded pieces of the weight filtration recovers the parabolic weights via the p‑adic monodromy theorem applied to the universal G‑bundle. The section conjecture for this stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group exact sequence corresponds to a ℚp‑point of BunGpar(XFF, ∞), i.e., a G‑bundle with parabolic structure defined over ℚp. The obstruction lies in the Selmer variety H¹f,par(Gal(ℚ̄p/ℚp), π₁ét(BunGpar(XFF, ∞)ℚ̄p, E)), defined by the crystalline condition at p and the parabolic condition at ∞. This Selmer variety is closely related to the moduli space of p‑adic Langlands parameters with prescribed Hodge–Tate weights, and its dimension is predicted by the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Representations of the Fundamental Group of a Curve with Prescribed Monodromy at Infinity and the p‑adic Riemann–Hilbert Correspondence

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p‑adic field K and D = Σ xi a reduced divisor. The moduli stack RepGmon(π₁ét(X D), μ) of p‑adic representations of the étale fundamental group of X D with prescribed monodromy conjugacy classes μ = (μi) at the punctures classifies continuous homomorphisms ρ : π₁ét(X D, x̄) → G(ℚp) such that the local monodromy at each xi lies in the conjugacy class μi. The étale fundamental group π₁ét(RepGmon(π₁ét(X D), μ), ρ) fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(RepGmon(π₁ét(XK̄ D), μ), ρ) → π₁ét(RepGmon(π₁ét(X D), μ), ρ) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is a pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex character variety with fixed monodromy. The p‑adic Riemann–Hilbert correspondence, established by Dettweiler–Reiter and extended to the moduli stack by Heuer, identifies the p‑adic pro‑étale homotopy type of RepGmon(π₁ét(X D), μ) with the de Rham moduli stack of connections with prescribed formal monodromy at D. This equivalence is compatible with the weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion of the fundamental group and the Hodge filtration on the de Rham fundamental group. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture asserts that the pair (X, D) together with the monodromy classes μ can be recovered from the Galois representation on π₁ét(RepGmon(π₁ét(XK̄ D), μ), ρ) together with its Hodge–Tate decomposition. The monodromy classes μ are recovered from the Galois action on the graded pieces of the weight filtration via the p‑adic local monodromy theorem: the inertia subgroup Ixi acts on the nearby cycles complex, and the logarithm of the monodromy operator recovers the formal monodromy exponent. When the monodromy is unipotent, the weight filtration has length equal to the nilpotency index, and the Galois action on the graded pieces gives the Jordan block sizes. The section conjecture for this stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group exact sequence corresponds to a K‑point of RepGmon(π₁ét(X D), μ), i.e., a p‑adic representation with prescribed monodromy defined over K. The obstruction lies in the Selmer variety H¹f,mon(GK, π₁ét(RepGmon(π₁ét(XK̄ D), μ), ρ)), defined by the crystalline condition at p and the monodromy condition at the punctures. This Selmer variety is a p‑adic analytic space whose dimension is predicted by the p‑adic Riemann–Hilbert correspondence to equal the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of connections with the same formal monodromy.

  • Confusing the parabolic moduli stack LocGpar(X, D) (regular singularities, prescribed conjugacy classes) with the irregular moduli stack LocGirr(X, D) (Stokes data, exponential growth); the weight filtration is finite for parabolic but the Stokes filtration introduces an additional grading for irregular.
  • Assuming the p‑adic Simpson correspondence for logarithmic connections (Diao–Lan–Liu–Zhu) applies to all (φ, Γ)‑modules; it requires the logarithmic growth condition, which corresponds to regular singularities at the boundary of the Robba ring.
  • Thinking the period domain 𝒟μ is always a flag variety; it is a flag variety only when μ is regular (all Hodge–Tate weights distinct); for non‑regular μ it is a partial flag variety with singularities.
  • Equating the geometric fundamental group of BunG(XFF) with the affine Grassmannian GrG; the former is the pro‑ℓ completion of the latter, but the Galois action on the former is highly non‑trivial and encodes the p‑adic Langlands correspondence.
