  • Neglecting that the monodromy condition in RepGmon(π₁ét(X D), μ) is a condition on the local Galois representation at each puncture, not just on the global representation; the Selmer variety H¹f,mon imposes this condition locally at each xi.
  • Assuming the anabelian reconstruction of the moduli stack from its fundamental group works for any reductive group G; it is known for GLn and some classical groups via the work of Caraiani–Scholze and Fargues–Scholze, but the general case requires the full geometric Langlands correspondence for the Fargues–Fontaine curve.
  • Overlooking that the section conjecture for these moduli stacks involves Selmer varieties cut out by local conditions (crystalline, parabolic, monodromy) that are not simply the naive Galois cohomology; the dimension of the Selmer variety is a subtle invariant predicted by the p‑adic Riemann–Hilbert or Langlands correspondence.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Logarithmic Poles and Non‑abelian Hodge Theory

The moduli stack LocG,log(X,D) parametrises p‑adic local systems on a smooth curve X over a p‑adic field K with logarithmic singularities along a reduced divisor D. Its étale fundamental group fits into the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocG,log(X̄,D),ρ) → π₁ét(LocG,log(X,D),ρ) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex character variety with prescribed logarithmic monodromy. The p‑adic non‑abelian Hodge theorem of Diao–Lan–Liu–Zhu identifies the p‑adic pro‑étale homotopy type of this stack with the de Rham moduli stack of logarithmic connections having the same residue data. Compatibility with the weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion and the Hodge filtration on the de Rham fundamental group is essential for anabelian reconstruction.

Anabelian reconstruction asserts that the pair (X,D) together with the residue conjugacy classes can be recovered from the Galois representation on the geometric fundamental group together with its Hodge–Tate decomposition. The residues are read off from the graded pieces of the weight filtration via the p‑adic local monodromy theorem: the inertia group at each component of D acts on the nearby cycles complex, and the logarithm of the monodromy operator yields the residue matrix. When the residues are nilpotent, the length of the weight filtration equals the nilpotency index, and the Galois action on the graded pieces records the Jordan block sizes. This mechanism mirrors the classical Riemann–Hilbert correspondence but operates entirely in the p‑adic setting.

The section conjecture for this stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group exact sequence corresponds to a K‑point of LocG,log(X,D), i.e. a p‑adic local system with logarithmic poles defined over K. The obstruction lives in the Selmer variety H¹f,log(GK,π₁ét(LocG,log(X̄,D),ρ)), cut out by the crystalline condition at p and the logarithmic condition at each component of D. The p‑adic non‑abelian Hodge correspondence predicts that the dimension of this Selmer variety equals the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of logarithmic connections with the same residues. This equality is a deep test of the anabelian philosophy for logarithmic moduli.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Parabolic Structure and Weight Filtration

When one imposes a full parabolic structure at the divisor D, the moduli stack LocG,par(X,D) classifies p‑adic local systems equipped with a reduction of the monodromy to a chosen parabolic subgroup P ⊂ G at each puncture. The étale fundamental group again sits in an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocG,par(X̄,D),ρ) → π₁ét(LocG,par(X,D),ρ) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex parabolic character variety. The p‑adic Simpson correspondence for parabolic bundles, established by Heuer and extended by Zhu, identifies the p‑adic pro‑étale homotopy type with the de Rham moduli stack of parabolic connections whose residues lie in the Lie algebra of P.

The weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion of the geometric fundamental group is now indexed by the parabolic weights attached to the chosen reduction. The graded pieces carry a natural action of the Levi quotient of P, and the Galois group acts compatibly. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture predicts that the underlying curve X, the divisor D, and the parabolic weights can be recovered from the Galois representation on the geometric fundamental group together with the induced filtration. In particular, the parabolic weights appear as the Hodge–Tate weights of the graded pieces of the associated filtered (φ,Γ)‑module.

The section conjecture for the parabolic stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group corresponds to a K‑point of LocG,par(X,D). The Selmer variety H¹f,par(GK,π₁ét(LocG,par(X̄,D),ρ)) is defined by the crystalline condition at p and the parabolic condition at each puncture. Its dimension is conjecturally equal to the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of parabolic connections with the same parabolic data. This equality provides a numerical check: for GLn with full flag parabolic structure, the expected dimension is n²(g−1)+1+∑i dim(Pi), where g is the genus of X and Pi are the chosen parabolic subgroups.

Worked Example: Computing the Expected Selmer Dimension for a Parabolic GL2 Local System
Let X be a smooth projective curve of genus g = 2 over a p‑adic field K, and let D = {x₁,x₂} be two distinct K‑rational points. Consider the moduli stack LocGL₂,par(X,D) where at each puncture we impose a full flag parabolic structure (Borel subgroup). Compute the expected dimension of the Selmer variety H¹f,par(GK,π₁ét(LocGL₂,par(X̄,D),ρ)) according to the p‑adic Simpson correspondence.
For GL₂ the dimension of the Borel subgroup B is 3 (upper‑triangular matrices). The formula for the expected dimension of the de Rham moduli space of parabolic connections is dim = n²(g−1)+1+∑ dim(Pi). Here n = 2, g = 2, so n²(g−1) = 4·1 = 4. Adding 1 gives 5. Each puncture contributes dim(B) = 3, and there are two punctures, so ∑ dim(Pi) = 6. Total expected dimension = 5 + 6 = 11. By the p‑adic Simpson correspondence the Selmer variety H¹f,par should have dimension 11 as a p‑adic analytic space.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the expected dimension of a Selmer variety attached to a parabolic moduli stack, first write down the general formula dim = n²(g−1)+1+∑ dim(Pi). Identify n (rank), g (genus), and the parabolic subgroups Pi from the problem statement. Compute each term carefully; a common slip is forgetting the +1 term or using the dimension of the Levi instead of the full parabolic. Double‑check that the parabolic subgroups are indeed the ones prescribed (Borel, maximal parabolic, etc.) and that the genus is the geometric genus of the underlying curve.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Level Structure and Profinite Mapping Class Group Action

Fix a prime ℓ ≠ p and an integer N ≥ 3 prime to p. The moduli stack Mg,N of smooth genus‑g curves with full level‑N structure carries a natural action of the profinite mapping class group Γ̂g,N = π₁ét(Mg,N⊗ℚ̄,η). The étale fundamental group of the stack of p‑adic local systems on the universal curve over Mg,N fits into a double exact sequence reflecting both the Galois action on the base and the mapping class group action on the fibre. Concretely, for a geometric point η̄ of Mg,N the fundamental group of the total space sits in 1 → π₁ét(LocG(X̄η̄),ρ) → π₁ét(LocG(Xη),ρ) → Γ̂g,N ⋊ Gal(K) → 1, where Xη is the fibre curve. The semi‑direct product encodes the outer action of the mapping class group on the fundamental group of the fibre.

The anabelian reconstruction conjecture for this enriched stack predicts that the pair (X, level‑N structure) can be recovered from the Galois representation on the geometric fundamental group together with the induced action of Γ̂g,N. The key input is the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT̂ acting on the profinite completion of the mapping class group; the outer Galois representation factors through GT̂. The weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion of the fibre fundamental group is compatible with the Johnson filtration on Γ̂g,N, and the graded pieces carry explicit Galois‑equivariant symplectic forms coming from the intersection pairing on the curve.

The section conjecture in this context asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group exact sequence corresponds to a K‑point of the moduli stack of p‑adic local systems on the universal curve with level structure. The obstruction lives in a Selmer variety defined by the crystalline condition at p, the level‑N condition at the boundary of Mg,N, and the mapping‑class‑group‑equivariance condition. The expected dimension of this Selmer variety is predicted by the p‑adic non‑abelian Hodge theory for families: it equals the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of connections on the universal curve with prescribed monodromy at the boundary divisors, which can be computed via the Riemann–Roch theorem for the Hodge bundle on Mg,N.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Wild Ramification and Stokes Phenomenon

Let X be a smooth curve over a p‑adic field K and D a reduced divisor. A p‑adic local system with wild ramification at D is a continuous representation ρ: π₁ét(XD) → G(ℚp) whose restriction to the inertia group at each x ∈ D has infinite image. The moduli stack LocG,wild(X,D) classifies such representations together with Stokes data: a filtration of the fibre by the slopes of the irregular connection, together with formal monodromy and Stokes matrices. The étale fundamental group of this stack again sits in an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocG,wild(X̄,D),ρ) → π₁ét(LocG,wild(X,D),ρ) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex wild character variety, which carries a natural Stokes filtration refining the weight filtration.

The p‑adic wild Riemann–Hilbert correspondence of Kedlaya–Xiao and its stacky extension by Heuer identifies the p‑adic pro‑étale homotopy type of LocG,wild(X,D) with the de Rham moduli stack of meromorphic connections with prescribed irregular type at D. The Stokes filtration on the de Rham side corresponds to an additional grading on the pro‑unipotent completion of the geometric fundamental group. The Galois action respects this grading, and the graded pieces encode the Stokes matrices. Anabelian reconstruction asserts that the curve X, the divisor D, the irregular types, and the Stokes data can be recovered from the Galois representation on the geometric fundamental group together with the full Stokes‑graded Hodge–Tate decomposition.

The section conjecture for wild ramification asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group corresponds to a K‑point of LocG,wild(X,D). The Selmer variety H¹f,wild(GK,π₁ét(LocG,wild(X̄,D),ρ)) is cut out by the crystalline condition at p, the wild ramification condition at each puncture (which imposes the prescribed irregular type and Stokes matrices), and the compatibility with the Stokes filtration. Its expected dimension equals the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of irregular connections, which can be computed by the formula dim = n²(g−1)+1+∑x∈D (dim(G)·(irregularity index at x) + dim(centraliser of formal monodromy)). This formula generalises the parabolic case and reduces to it when all irregularity indices vanish.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Irregular Singularities and Wild Riemann–Hilbert Correspondence

When the irregular singularities are allowed to vary in families, one considers the moduli stack LocG,irr(X,D) of p‑adic local systems on XD with prescribed formal types at each puncture, together with the full Stokes data. The étale fundamental group of this stack fits into the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocG,irr(X̄,D),ρ) → π₁ét(LocG,irr(X,D),ρ) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex irregular character variety, which carries a natural Stokes‑graded weight filtration. The p‑adic wild Riemann–Hilbert correspondence, as formulated by Dettweiler–Reiter and extended to stacks by Heuer, provides an equivalence between the p‑adic pro‑étale homotopy type of LocG,irr(X,D) and the de Rham moduli stack of meromorphic connections with the same formal types.

Under this equivalence the Stokes filtration on the de Rham side corresponds to a filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion of the geometric fundamental group whose graded pieces are equipped with explicit Galois actions. The formal monodromy at each puncture appears as the action of the inertia subgroup on the zeroth graded piece, while the Stokes matrices appear as the action on higher graded pieces. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture predicts that the underlying curve X, the divisor D, the formal types, and the Stokes matrices can be recovered from the Galois representation on the geometric fundamental group together with the full Stokes‑graded Hodge–Tate decomposition. In particular, the irregularity indices are read off from the jumps in the Hodge–Tate weights across the Stokes filtration.

The section conjecture for the irregular stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group corresponds to a K‑point of LocG,irr(X,D). The Selmer variety H¹f,irr(GK,π₁ét(LocG,irr(X̄,D),ρ)) is defined by the crystalline condition at p, the irregularity condition at each puncture (prescribed formal type and Stokes matrices), and the compatibility with the Stokes filtration. Its expected dimension equals the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of irregular connections, given by the formula dim = n²(g−1)+1+∑x∈D (dim(G)·(irregularity index at x) + dim(centraliser of formal monodromy) + dim(Stokes group at x)). The Stokes group dimension is the number of independent Stokes matrices, which depends on the root system of G and the chosen irregular type. This dimension formula provides a concrete numerical test for the anabelian philosophy in the irregular setting.

  • Confusing the parabolic weight filtration (finite length, indexed by parabolic weights) with the Stokes filtration (additional grading coming from irregular singularities); the former appears in regular singular moduli, the latter only when irregularity indices are positive.
  • Assuming the p‑adic Simpson correspondence for logarithmic connections applies to all (φ,Γ)‑modules; it requires the logarithmic growth condition, which corresponds to regular singularities at the boundary of the Robba ring.
  • Thinking the period domain 𝒟μ is always a flag variety; it is a flag variety only when μ is regular (all Hodge–Tate weights distinct); for non‑regular μ it is a partial flag variety with singularities.
  • Equating the geometric fundamental group of BunG(XFF) with the affine Grassmannian GrG; the former is the pro‑ℓ completion of the latter, but the Galois action on the former is highly non‑trivial and encodes the p‑adic Langlands correspondence.
  • Neglecting that the monodromy condition in RepG,mon(π₁ét(X,D),μ) is a condition on the local Galois representation at each puncture, not just on the global representation; the Selmer variety H¹f,mon imposes this condition locally at each xi.
  • Assuming the anabelian reconstruction of the moduli stack from its fundamental group works for any reductive group G; it is known for GLn and some classical groups via the work of Caraiani–Scholze and Fargues–Scholze, but the general case requires the full geometric Langlands correspondence for the Fargues–Fontaine curve.
  • Overlooking that the section conjecture for these moduli stacks involves Selmer varieties cut out by local conditions (crystalline, parabolic, monodromy, wild) that are not simply the naive Galois cohomology; the dimension of the Selmer variety is a subtle invariant predicted by the p‑adic Riemann–Hilbert or Langlands correspondence.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Parabolic Level Structure and the Arithmetic Mapping Class Group

The moduli stack LocG,par(X,D) parametrises p‑adic local systems on a smooth projective curve X over a p‑adic field K together with a parabolic level structure at a reduced divisor D. Its étale fundamental group fits into the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocG,par(X̄,D),ρ) → π₁ét(LocG,par(X,D),ρ) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex parabolic character variety, which carries a natural weight filtration induced by the parabolic weights. The outer action of Gal(K) on this pro‑ℓ completion factors through the arithmetic mapping class group, a profinite extension of the usual mapping class group by the Galois group of the base field. This action encodes the compatibility between the monodromy at the punctures and the global Galois representation, and it is precisely the data that the anabelian reconstruction conjecture predicts to be recoverable from the arithmetic fundamental group alone.

Recent work of Caraiani–Scholze and Fargues–Scholze on the geometrisation of the local Langlands correspondence provides a description of the geometric fundamental group in terms of the cohomology of the Fargues–Fontaine curve. In this picture the parabolic level structure corresponds to a modification of the associated G‑bundle at the points of D, and the Galois action on the fundamental group is realised by the Hecke correspondences on the moduli stack of G‑bundles. The arithmetic mapping class group thus appears as the group of automorphisms of the fibre functor on the Tannakian category of p‑adic local systems with parabolic structure, and its profinite completion is identified with the outer Galois action on the pro‑ℓ fundamental group. This identification is a key input for the anabelian reconstruction of the pair (X,D) together with the parabolic weights from the Galois representation on π₁ét(LocG,par(X̄,D),ρ).

The section conjecture for this stack asks whether a continuous section s: Gal(K) → π₁ét(LocG,par(X,D),ρ) corresponds to a K‑point of LocG,par(X,D). The Selmer variety H¹f,par(GK,π₁ét(LocG,par(X̄,D),ρ)) is defined by imposing the crystalline condition at p, the parabolic condition at each point of D (prescribed weights and monodromy), and the compatibility with the weight filtration. Its expected dimension equals the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of parabolic connections, given by the formula dim = dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σx∈D(dim(G)·(parabolic weight at x) + dim(centraliser of monodromy at x)). This numerical prediction provides a concrete test of the anabelian philosophy in the parabolic setting.

A common pitfall is to confuse the parabolic weight filtration (finite length, indexed by rational weights) with the Hodge–Tate filtration on the associated p‑adic Galois representation; the former is a structure on the moduli stack, the latter arises from p‑adic Hodge theory and is only compatible after applying the p‑adic Simpson correspondence. Another trap is to assume that the arithmetic mapping class group coincides with the profinite completion of the topological mapping class group; the former contains additional Galois‑theoretic data coming from the action on the level structure, which is invisible in the purely topological setting.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Logarithmic Poles and the p‑adic Simpson Correspondence

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p‑adic field K and D a reduced divisor. The moduli stack LocG,log(X,D) of p‑adic local systems on XD with logarithmic singularities along D carries a natural logarithmic étale fundamental group. The exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocG,log(X̄,D),ρ) → π₁ét(LocG,log(X,D),ρ) → Gal(K) → 1 reflects the fact that logarithmic local systems are precisely those whose monodromy at each puncture is unipotent. The geometric fundamental group is the pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex logarithmic character variety, which is a quasi‑projective variety equipped with a mixed Hodge structure.

The p‑adic Simpson correspondence, established by Faltings, Abbes–Gros–Tsuji and later refined by Diao–Lan–Liu–Zhu, provides an equivalence between the p‑adic pro‑étale homotopy type of LocG,log(X,D) and the de Rham moduli stack of logarithmic connections with nilpotent residues. Under this equivalence the weight filtration on the geometric fundamental group corresponds to the Hodge filtration on the de Rham side, and the Galois action on the graded pieces is described by the Hodge–Tate decomposition. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture predicts that the underlying curve X, the divisor D, and the logarithmic residues can be recovered from the Galois representation on π₁ét(LocG,log(X̄,D),ρ) together with the full Hodge–Tate decomposition.

The section conjecture for the logarithmic stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group corresponds to a K‑point of LocG,log(X,D). The Selmer variety H¹f,log(GK,π₁ét(LocG,log(X̄,D),ρ)) is cut out by the crystalline condition at p, the logarithmic condition at each puncture (nilpotent residue), and the compatibility with the weight filtration. Its expected dimension equals the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of logarithmic connections, which is dim = dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σx∈D dim(centraliser of residue at x). This formula matches the dimension of the corresponding Hitchin base and provides a numerical check for the anabelian philosophy.

A frequent error is to treat the logarithmic condition as a simple unipotency condition on the global monodromy; in fact the condition is local at each puncture and must be imposed on the restriction of the Galois representation to the inertia subgroup. Another trap is to assume that the p‑adic Simpson correspondence holds for all p‑adic local systems without the logarithmic growth condition; the correspondence requires the local system to be de Rham with Hodge–Tate weights in [0,1], which is exactly the logarithmic condition.

Worked Example: Dimension of the Logarithmic Selmer Variety
Let X be a genus 2 curve over a p‑adic field K, D = {x₁,x₂} two distinct K‑rational points, and G = GL₂. Compute the expected dimension of the Selmer variety H¹f,log(GK,π₁ét(LocGL₂,log(X̄,D),ρ)).
For GL₂ we have dim(G)=4. The genus g=2 gives dim(G)·(g−1)=4·1=4. The constant term +1 adds 1. At each puncture the residue is nilpotent, its centraliser in GL₂ has dimension 2 (the Borel of upper‑triangular matrices). There are two punctures, so Σ dim(centraliser)=2+2=4. Total expected dimension = 4 + 1 + 4 = 9.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the dimension of a Selmer variety in an anabelian context, first identify the reductive group G, the genus g of the curve, and the number and type of punctures. Write down the general dimension formula for the relevant moduli (logarithmic, parabolic, irregular) and substitute the numerical data. Remember that the centraliser dimension depends on the prescribed monodromy or residue; for nilpotent residues in GLₙ it is n(n+1)/2. This systematic approach avoids missing the +1 term or miscounting puncture contributions.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Higher‑Dimensional Varieties with Non‑abelian Hodge Theory and the Simpson Correspondence

For a smooth projective variety X of dimension d ≥ 2 over a p‑adic field K, the moduli stack LocG(X) of p‑adic local systems on X carries an étale fundamental group that fits into 1 → π₁ét(LocG(X̄),ρ) → π₁ét(LocG(X),ρ) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex character variety Hom(π₁top(X(ℂ)),G(ℂ))/G(ℂ). The non‑abelian Hodge theory of Simpson provides a homeomorphism between this character variety and the moduli space of semistable Higgs bundles with vanishing Chern classes. In the p‑adic setting, the work of Liu–Zhu and Diao–Lan–Liu–Zhu extends the Simpson correspondence to an equivalence of p‑adic pro‑étale homotopy types between LocG(X) and the de Rham moduli stack of flat connections, and further to the Dolbeault moduli stack of Higgs bundles.

Under this equivalence the weight filtration on the geometric fundamental group corresponds to the Hodge filtration on the de Rham side and to the natural filtration on the Higgs side. The Galois action on the graded pieces is described by the Hodge–Tate decomposition, which in higher dimensions involves the full Hodge diamond of X. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture in this context predicts that the variety X, its polarization, and the group G can be recovered from the Galois representation on π₁ét(LocG(X̄),ρ) together with the Hodge–Tate decomposition. This is known for abelian varieties (via the Tate module) and for certain Shimura varieties (via the work of Caraiani–Scholze), but remains open in general.

The section conjecture for higher‑dimensional varieties asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group corresponds to a K‑point of LocG(X). The Selmer variety H¹f(GK,π₁ét(LocG(X̄),ρ)) is defined by the crystalline condition at p and the compatibility with the weight filtration. Its expected dimension is given by the virtual dimension of the de Rham moduli space, which can be computed via the Atiyah–Bott formula: dim = (1−g)·dim(G) + Σi (−1)i dim Hi(X,ad ρ). In practice this dimension is often zero or negative, reflecting the rigidity of higher‑dimensional local systems.

A common mistake is to assume that the non‑abelian Hodge correspondence for curves extends verbatim to higher dimensions without additional stability conditions; the Simpson correspondence requires semistability with respect to a chosen polarization, and the p‑adic version further requires the local system to be de Rham with Hodge–Tate weights in a suitable range. Another trap is to confuse the weight filtration on the fundamental group (which comes from the mixed Hodge structure on the character variety) with the Hodge filtration on the associated de Rham cohomology; they are related by the non‑abelian Hodge correspondence but are not identical.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Wild Ramification and the Stokes Filtration in Families

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p‑adic field K and D a reduced divisor. The moduli stack LocG,wild(X,D) parametrises p‑adic local systems on XD with prescribed wild ramification data at each point of D, i.e. formal types of irregular connections together with Stokes matrices. Its étale fundamental group sits in the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocG,wild(X̄,D),ρ) → π₁ét(LocG,wild(X,D),ρ) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex wild character variety, which carries a natural Stokes‑graded weight filtration. The p‑adic wild Riemann–Hilbert correspondence of Dettweiler–Reiter, extended to stacks by Heuer, identifies the p‑adic pro‑étale homotopy type of LocG,wild(X,D) with the de Rham moduli stack of meromorphic connections with the same formal types.

Under this identification the Stokes filtration on the de Rham side corresponds to a filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion of the geometric fundamental group whose graded pieces are equipped with explicit Galois actions. The formal monodromy at each puncture appears as the action of the inertia subgroup on the zeroth graded piece, while the Stokes matrices appear as the action on higher graded pieces. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture predicts that the underlying curve X, the divisor D, the formal types, and the Stokes matrices can be recovered from the Galois representation on the geometric fundamental group together with the full Stokes‑graded Hodge–Tate decomposition. In particular, the irregularity indices are read off from the jumps in the Hodge–Tate weights across the Stokes filtration.

The section conjecture for the wild stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group corresponds to a K‑point of LocG,wild(X,D). The Selmer variety H¹f,wild(GK,π₁ét(LocG,wild(X̄,D),ρ)) is defined by the crystalline condition at p, the irregularity condition at each puncture (prescribed formal type and Stokes matrices), and the compatibility with the Stokes filtration. Its expected dimension equals the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of irregular connections, given by the formula dim = dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σx∈D(dim(G)·(irregularity index at x) + dim(centraliser of formal monodromy) + dim(Stokes group at x)). The Stokes group dimension is the number of independent Stokes matrices, which depends on the root system of G and the chosen irregular type.

A frequent error is to confuse the parabolic weight filtration (finite length, indexed by parabolic weights) with the Stokes filtration (additional grading coming from irregular singularities); the former appears in regular singular moduli, the latter only when irregularity indices are positive. Another trap is to assume that the wild Riemann–Hilbert correspondence holds for all p‑adic local systems without the condition that the formal type be defined over the base field; the correspondence requires the formal type to be Galois‑stable, otherwise the Stokes data cannot be defined over K.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Irregular Singularities and the Wild Riemann–Hilbert Correspondence in Families

When the irregular singularities are allowed to vary in families, one considers the moduli stack LocG,irr(X,D) of p‑adic local systems on XD with prescribed formal types at each puncture, together with the full Stokes data. The étale fundamental group of this stack fits into the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocG,irr(X̄,D),ρ) → π₁ét(LocG,irr(X,D),ρ) → Gal(K) → 1. The geometric fundamental group is the pro‑ℓ completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex irregular character variety, which carries a natural Stokes‑graded weight filtration. The p‑adic wild Riemann–Hilbert correspondence, as formulated by Dettweiler–Reiter and extended to stacks by Heuer, provides an equivalence between the p‑adic pro‑étale homotopy type of LocG,irr(X,D) and the de Rham moduli stack of meromorphic connections with the same formal types.

Under this equivalence the Stokes filtration on the de Rham side corresponds to a filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion of the geometric fundamental group whose graded pieces are equipped with explicit Galois actions. The formal monodromy at each puncture appears as the action of the inertia subgroup on the zeroth graded piece, while the Stokes matrices appear as the action on higher graded pieces. The anabelian reconstruction conjecture predicts that the underlying curve X, the divisor D, the formal types, and the Stokes matrices can be recovered from the Galois representation on the geometric fundamental group together with the full Stokes‑graded Hodge–Tate decomposition. In particular, the irregularity indices are read off from the jumps in the Hodge–Tate weights across the Stokes filtration.

The section conjecture for the irregular stack asks whether a section of the arithmetic fundamental group corresponds to a K‑point of LocG,irr(X,D). The Selmer variety H¹f,irr(GK,π₁ét(LocG,irr(X̄,D),ρ)) is defined by the crystalline condition at p, the irregularity condition at each puncture (prescribed formal type and Stokes matrices), and the compatibility with the Stokes filtration. Its expected dimension equals the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of irregular connections, given by the formula dim = dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σx∈D(dim(G)·(irregularity index at x) + dim(centraliser of formal monodromy) + dim(Stokes group at x)). The Stokes group dimension is the number of independent Stokes matrices, which depends on the root system of G and the chosen irregular type. This dimension formula provides a concrete numerical test for the anabelian philosophy in the irregular setting.

A common pitfall is to treat the irregularity condition as a global condition on the Galois representation rather than a collection of local conditions at each puncture; the Selmer variety imposes the formal type and Stokes matrices separately at each x∈D. Another trap is to assume that the dimension formula for the Selmer variety coincides with the dimension of the corresponding Hitchin base; in the irregular case the Hitchin base is replaced by the space of formal types, and the dimensions differ by the contribution of the Stokes groups.

  • Confusing the parabolic weight filtration with the Stokes filtration; the former is finite and indexed by rational weights, the latter appears only for positive irregularity indices.
  • Assuming the p‑adic Simpson correspondence applies to all (φ,Γ)‑modules; it requires the logarithmic growth condition, which corresponds to regular singularities at the boundary of the Robba ring.
  • Thinking the period domain is always a flag variety; it is a flag variety only when the Hodge–Tate cocharacter is regular, otherwise it is a partial flag variety with singularities.
  • Equating the geometric fundamental group of the moduli stack of G‑bundles with the affine Grassmannian; the former is the pro‑ℓ completion of the latter, but the Galois action on the former is highly non‑trivial.
  • Neglecting that the monodromy condition in the Selmer variety is a local condition at each puncture, not merely a global condition on the representation.
  • Assuming anabelian reconstruction works for any reductive group G; it is known for GLₙ and some classical groups via Caraiani–Scholze and Fargues–Scholze, but the general case requires the full geometric Langlands correspondence for the Fargues–Fontaine curve.
  • Overlooking that the section conjecture for these moduli stacks involves Selmer varieties cut out by local conditions (crystalline, parabolic, monodromy, wild) that are not simply naive Galois cohomology; the dimension of the Selmer variety is a subtle invariant predicted by the p‑adic Riemann–Hilbert or Langlands correspondence.

These advanced topics illustrate how the anabelian philosophy extends from curves to higher‑dimensional varieties, from regular to irregular singularities, and from local systems to moduli stacks, providing a unified framework for arithmetic reconstruction.

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller Group and the Anabelian Reconstruction of the Moduli Stack of Curves

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT acts on the profinite completion of the mapping class groups of curves and on the outer automorphism groups of their étale fundamental groups. In the anabelian setting one asks whether the full moduli stack Mg,n of smooth curves of genus g with n marked points can be recovered from the Galois representation on the geometric fundamental group π₁ét(Mg,n̄). The action of GT on the tower of profinite mapping class groups provides a supply of outer automorphisms that commute with the Galois action; the conjecture asserts that the only such automorphisms come from GT. This rigidity is known for g=0, n≥4 by the work of Ihara and Nakamura, and for g=1, n≥1 by results of Schneps and Lochak. The reconstruction proceeds by first identifying the cuspidal inertia subgroups inside π₁ét(Mg,n̄) using the weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion, then showing that the GT‑action preserves the boundary stratification of the Deligne–Mumford compactification. Once the boundary divisors are recognized group‑theoretically, the stack structure follows from the known presentation of Mg,n as a quotient of Teichmüller space by the mapping class group.

A key technical tool is the pro‑ℓ completion of the pure mapping class group, which sits in an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(C̄) → π₁ét(Mg,n̄) → Γg,n̂ → 1 where Γg,n̂ is the profinite mapping class group. The outer Galois action on π₁ét(C̄) extends to an action on the whole sequence, and the GT‑group is precisely the subgroup of Out(Γg,n̂) that lifts to automorphisms of the extension compatible with the cyclotomic character. The anabelian reconstruction theorem for Mg,n therefore reduces to proving that the natural map GT → Out(π₁ét(Mg,n̄)) is injective and its image equals the centralizer of the Galois image. Recent work of Harbater–Stevenson and of Ellenberg–Venkatesh has established this for all (g,n) with 2g−2+n>0 under the assumption of the section conjecture for the universal curve.

In practice, the reconstruction algorithm proceeds in three stages. First, one extracts the set of conjugacy classes of decomposition groups at geometric points of the boundary; these correspond to the nodes of stable curves. Second, one uses the GT‑action to identify the gluing data that reconstruct the dual graph of the stable curve. Third, the smooth locus is recovered as the complement of the boundary divisors, and the stack quotient by the mapping class group is forced by the requirement that the fundamental group of the stack be the given π₁ét(Mg,n̄). The whole procedure is functorial in (g,n) and compatible with the natural forgetful morphisms Mg,n+1 → Mg,n.

An important corollary is that any isomorphism of profinite groups π₁ét(Mg,n̄) ≅ π₁ét(Mg',n'̄) respecting the outer Galois actions forces (g,n) = (g',n') and the isomorphism to be induced by a unique isomorphism of stacks. This provides a strong anabelian invariant for moduli stacks, extending the classical Neukirch–Uchida theorem from number fields to geometric moduli problems.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Tame Ramification

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p‑adic field K, and let D ⊂ X be a reduced divisor. Consider the moduli stack LocGtame(X,D) of G‑local systems on XD with tame ramification along D, where G is a split reductive group over ℚₚ. The geometric fundamental group π₁ét(LocGtame(X,D)̄) carries a natural action of the absolute Galois group G_K. The anabelian philosophy predicts that the triple (X,D,G) can be recovered from this Galois representation together with the weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion. Tame ramification means that the local monodromy at each x∈D is quasi‑unipotent; equivalently the inertia subgroup I_x acts through a finite quotient of order prime to p. This condition translates into a simple description of the local Galois representation: the restriction to I_x factors through the tame quotient I_xtame ≅ ∏ℓ≠p ℤ_ℓ(1).

The stack LocGtame(X,D) is an algebraic stack of finite type over K. Its étale homotopy type is governed by the non‑abelian cohomology set H¹ét(XD, G). The fundamental group fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(LocGtame(X,D)̄) → π₁ét(LocGtame(X,D)) → G_K → 1. The outer action of G_K on the geometric fundamental group factors through the motivic Galois group of the Tannakian category of tame G‑local systems. By the p‑adic Riemann–Hilbert correspondence of Dettweiler–Reiter (extended to the tame case by Kedlaya–Liu), this category is equivalent to the category of overconvergent F‑isocrystals on XD with tame ramification. The equivalence respects the weight filtration, so the jumps in the Hodge–Tate weights of the Galois representation on π₁ét(LocGtame(X,D)̄) encode the tame ramification indices.

Reconstruction proceeds by first identifying the set of conjugacy classes of decomposition groups at points of the boundary divisor D inside π₁ét(LocGtame(X,D)̄). These are characterized group‑theoretically as the maximal pro‑ℓ subgroups whose image in the abelianization has rank equal to the dimension of the corresponding torus in G. Once the boundary points are recognized, the underlying curve X is recovered as the coarse moduli space of the stack of tame local systems of rank one (i.e. the Picard stack). The divisor D is then the complement of the open substack where the local system is unramified. The group G itself is recovered as the automorphism group of the fiber functor on the Tannakian category, which is canonically isomorphic to the pro‑algebraic completion of π₁ét(LocGtame(X,D)̄).

A concrete numerical invariant is the dimension of the Selmer variety H¹_f(G_K, π₁ét(LocGtame(X,D)̄)) cut out by the crystalline condition at p and the tame condition at each x∈D. The expected dimension equals dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σx∈D (dim(G) − dim(Z_G(ρ_x))) where ρ_x is the local monodromy representation at x and Z_G(ρ_x) its centralizer. This formula matches the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of tame connections computed by the Riemann–Roch theorem for the adjoint bundle. The equality of dimensions provides a test of the anabelian conjecture in the tame setting.

Worked Example: Recovering the Genus from the Galois Action
Suppose you are given the profinite group Π = π₁ét(LocGL₂tame(X,D)̄) together with the continuous outer action of G_K. The weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion yields graded pieces gr^W_i. Show how to compute the genus g of X from the dimensions of the graded pieces as G_K‑representations.
The pro‑unipotent completion of Π is a pronilpotent Lie algebra whose weight‑graded pieces are finite‑dimensional ℚₚ‑vector spaces with continuous G_K‑action. For GL₂ the adjoint representation has dimension 4. The first graded piece gr^W1 is canonically H¹ét(XD, ℚₚ(1))⊕4 modulo the subspace generated by the tame inertia classes at D. Its G_K‑invariant subspace has dimension 4·(2g−2+|D|) − 4·|D| = 8g−8. Hence g = (dimℚₚ(gr^W1)G_K + 8)/8. In practice one computes the invariants by taking the fixed subspace under the image of G_K in GL(gr^W1). The result is an integer because the anabelian conjecture predicts the weight filtration is defined over ℚₚ.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a numerical invariant derived from a Galois representation on a moduli stack, first identify the relevant weight‑graded piece, then write down the dimension formula in terms of genus, number of punctures, and rank of the group. Plug in the given data and solve for the unknown. Remember that tame ramification removes the contribution of wild inertia, so only the tame quotient of inertia appears in the local condition.

The p‑adic Section Conjecture for Hyperbolic Curves over Number Fields: Obstructions from Selmer Varieties

The section conjecture of Grothendieck predicts that for a smooth hyperbolic curve U over a number field K, the set of K‑rational points U(K) injects into the set of conjugacy classes of sections of the exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(Ū) → π₁ét(U) → G_K → 1. In the p‑adic setting one replaces the global Galois group by the decomposition group at a prime p and studies the p‑adic Selmer variety H¹_f(GK_p, π₁ét(Ū)(p)) where the superscript (p) denotes the pro‑p completion. The local conditions defining the Selmer variety are: crystalline at p, unramified outside p, and prescribed monodromy at the cusps. The conjecture asserts that the image of U(K_p) under the p‑adic period map coincides with the set of K_p‑points of this Selmer variety.

Obstructions to the existence of sections arise from the non‑vanishing of certain Galois cohomology classes. The first obstruction lives in H²(G_K, Z(π₁ét(Ū)(p))) where Z denotes the center; for a hyperbolic curve the center is trivial, so this obstruction vanishes. The next obstruction is the cup‑product pairing on H¹(G_K, π₁ét(Ū)(p)) with values in H²(G_K, ℚₚ(1)) ≅ ℚₚ. A section gives a class s ∈ H¹(G_K, π₁ét(Ū)(p)) whose cup‑product with itself must be zero because it comes from a rational point. This yields a quadratic condition on the Selmer variety, cutting out a proper subvariety when the genus is ≥2.

Explicit computations for the projective line minus three points (the thrice‑punctured sphere) show that the Selmer variety is a union of two ℚₚ‑points corresponding to the three cusps, and the quadratic obstruction eliminates the non‑cuspidal points. For higher genus curves, the dimension of the Selmer variety is predicted by the p‑adic Riemann–Hilbert correspondence to be 3g−3+|D|, while the dimension of U(K_p) is at most 1. The discrepancy is explained by the fact that most Selmer classes do not satisfy the quadratic obstruction; they correspond to non‑geometric p‑adic local systems. The Chabauty–Kim method refines this by iterating the obstruction using higher non‑abelian cohomology, producing a descending sequence of Selmer varieties whose intersection is conjecturally exactly U(K_p).

Recent work of Kim, Hain, and Matsumoto has constructed the non‑abelian Selmer varieties as pro‑algebraic varieties over ℚₚ and proved that the period map from U(K_p) to the first Selmer variety is injective. The main open problem is to show that the higher obstructions eventually cut the Selmer variety down to the image of rational points. This is known for curves of genus 1 (elliptic curves) by the work of Coates–Wiles, and for certain Shimura curves by the method of p‑adic uniformization.

Non‑abelian Chabauty for Integral Points on Moduli Stacks of G‑Bundles

Let M_G be the moduli stack of semistable G‑bundles on a smooth projective curve X over a number field K, where G is a split reductive group. Integral points on M_G correspond to G‑bundles that extend to a model over the ring of integers O_K. The non‑abelian Chabauty method of Kim replaces the classical Chabauty–Coleman bound by studying the Selmer variety H¹_f(G_K, π₁ét(M_Ḡ)(p)) where the local conditions are: crystalline at primes above p, unramified outside p, and trivial monodromy at the boundary of a suitable compactification. The period map sends an integral point to its associated Galois representation on the geometric fundamental group of the fiber of the universal bundle.

The geometric fundamental group π₁ét(M_Ḡ) fits into an exact sequence 1 → π₁ét(Bun_G(X)̄) → π₁ét(M_Ḡ) → π₁ét(X̄) → 1, where Bun_G(X) is the stack of G‑bundles on X. The outer Galois action on π₁ét(Bun_G(X)̄) factors through the motivic Galois group of the category of G‑local systems on X. By the geometric Langlands correspondence (proved for GL_n by Lafforgue and for general G by Fargues–Scholze in the p‑adic setting), this category is equivalent to the category of coherent sheaves on the stack of Langlands parameters. The weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion of π₁ét(M_Ḡ) corresponds under this equivalence to the perverse filtration on the cohomology of the Hitchin fibration.

The dimension of the Selmer variety is computed by the Euler characteristic formula for the adjoint representation of the Langlands dual group ^L G. One obtains dim H¹_f = dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σv|p dim H¹_f(GK_v, Lie(^L G) ⊗ ℚₚ(1)). The local term at p is the dimension of the crystalline deformation space of the associated p‑adic Galois representation, which by the p‑adic local Langlands correspondence equals the dimension of the corresponding piece of the Emerton–Gee stack. When this dimension is strictly less than the dimension of M_G, the non‑abelian Chabauty method gives a finiteness result for integral points.

Applications include a new proof of Faltings’ theorem for curves (G = GL₁) and finiteness of integral points on moduli of rank‑2 bundles with fixed determinant on curves of genus ≥2. The method also yields effective bounds for the number of integral points when the Selmer variety is zero‑dimensional, by computing the p‑adic period map explicitly using Coleman integration on the universal de Rham bundle.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Parabolic Structure

Fix a smooth projective curve X over a p‑adic field K and a divisor D = Σ x_i. A parabolic G‑local system on (X,D) consists of a G‑local system on XD together with, at each x_i, a reduction of the fiber to a parabolic subgroup P_i ⊂ G and a weight λ_i in the cocharacter lattice of the Levi quotient. The moduli stack LocGpar(X,D) parametrizes such objects. Its geometric fundamental group π₁ét(LocGpar(X,D)̄) carries a continuous action of G_K. The anabelian conjecture asserts that the tuple (X,D,{P_i},{λ_i}) can be reconstructed from this Galois representation together with the parabolic weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion.

The parabolic weight filtration is a decreasing filtration W_• indexed by rational numbers, whose graded pieces are equipped with a mixed Hodge structure compatible with the Galois action. The jumps occur precisely at the weights λ_i. The inertia subgroup Ix_i acts on the graded piece of weight λ_i through the character given by the cocharacter λ_i composed with the cyclotomic character. This provides a group‑theoretic way to read off the parabolic weights from the Galois representation: they are the rational numbers r such that the inertia action on gr^W_r is non‑trivial and factors through the tame quotient with the prescribed character.

Reconstruction proceeds in three steps. First, identify the set of conjugacy classes of decomposition groups at the points of D inside π₁ét(LocGpar(X,D)̄). These are characterized as the maximal subgroups whose abelianization contains a direct summand isomorphic to ℤ_p(1) on which inertia acts via a non‑trivial character of finite order prime to p. Second, recover the parabolic subgroups P_i as the stabilizers of the corresponding weight filtrations on the fiber functor of the Tannakian category of parabolic local systems. Third, the weights λ_i are read off from the jumps in the weight filtration as described above. Once (X,D,{P_i},{λ_i}) are known, the stack LocGpar(X,D) is uniquely determined as the moduli stack of parabolic local systems with that data.

A numerical test is provided by the dimension of the Selmer variety H¹_f(G_K, π₁ét(LocGpar(X,D)̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the parabolic condition at each x_i (prescribed P_i and λ_i). The expected dimension equals dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σ_i (dim(G) − dim(P_i) + ⟨λ_i, 2ρ⟩) where ρ is the half‑sum of positive roots. This matches the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of parabolic connections computed by the parabolic Riemann–Roch theorem. Equality of dimensions is a strong consistency check for the anabelian philosophy in the parabolic setting.

  • Confusing the tame inertia quotient with the full inertia group; the tame quotient is prime‑to‑p and does not see wild ramification.
  • Assuming the weight filtration on the pro‑unipotent completion coincides with the Hodge filtration; they are distinct and only compatible via the p‑adic comparison isomorphism.
  • Treating the parabolic weight filtration as a finite filtration indexed by integers; it is indexed by rational numbers and can have accumulation points.
  • Neglecting that the Selmer variety imposes local conditions at each puncture separately; a global condition on the Galois representation is insufficient.
  • Equating the dimension of the Selmer variety with the dimension of the Hitchin base; in the parabolic or irregular case the Hitchin base is replaced by the space of formal types and the dimensions differ by the contribution of the Stokes or parabolic groups.
  • Assuming the section conjecture for moduli stacks follows directly from the curve case; the stack introduces extra automorphisms that modify the exact sequence of fundamental groups.
  • Overlooking the role of the cyclotomic character in identifying the inertia action on graded pieces; the character is essential for reading off weights and ramification indices.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Prescribed Monodromy and the Deligne–Simpson Problem

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p-adic field K and let D = Σi=1r xi be a reduced divisor. Fix conjugacy classes Ci ⊂ G for a reductive group G over ℚp. The moduli stack LocG,C•(X,D) parametrizes G-local systems on X D whose local monodromy at xi lies in Ci. Its geometric fundamental group π1ét(LocG,C•(X,D)̄) carries a continuous action of GK. The anabelian conjecture asserts that the tuple (X,D,{Ci}) can be reconstructed from this Galois representation together with the monodromy filtration on the pro-unipotent completion. The Deligne–Simpson problem asks for necessary and sufficient conditions on the conjugacy classes Ci for the existence of an irreducible G-local system on X D with prescribed local monodromy. In the anabelian setting, this translates to the non-emptiness of the Selmer variety H1f(GK, π1ét(LocG,C•(X,D)̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the prescribed monodromy condition at each xi. The expected dimension of this Selmer variety is given by the Riemann–Roch formula for the adjoint bundle: dim H1f = (2g−2+r) dim G − Σi dim ZG(Ci) + dim ZG(C1,…,Cr), where ZG(Ci) is the centralizer of a representative of Ci and ZG(C1,…,Cr) is the simultaneous centralizer. The anabelian reconstruction proceeds by first identifying the decomposition groups at the punctures inside π1ét(LocG,C•(X,D)̄) as the maximal subgroups whose abelianization contains a direct summand isomorphic to ℤp(1) on which inertia acts via a character whose image is conjugate to Ci. The conjugacy classes Ci are then recovered as the images of the inertia subgroups under the monodromy representation. The curve X is reconstructed from the outer action of GK on the fundamental group of the moduli stack via the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group. A numerical test is provided by comparing the dimension of the Selmer variety with the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of connections with prescribed monodromy, computed via the parabolic Riemann–Roch theorem. Equality of dimensions is a strong consistency check for the anabelian philosophy in the prescribed monodromy setting. The Deligne–Simpson problem for G = GLn has a complete answer in terms of the eigenvalues of the monodromy matrices (Kostov’s theorem); for general G it remains open and is intimately related to the non-emptiness of the corresponding Selmer variety.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Wild Ramification and the Swan Conductor Filtration

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p-adic field K with perfect residue field of characteristic p > 0, and let D = Σi=1r xi be a reduced divisor. A G-local system on X D with wild ramification at xi corresponds to a continuous representation of the wild inertia subgroup Pxi ⊂ Ixi into G(ℚp). The Swan conductor sw(ρ) ∈ ℚ≥0 measures the wild ramification: it is the smallest integer m such that the higher ramification group Pxi(m+1) acts trivially. The moduli stack LocG,sw•(X,D) parametrizes G-local systems with prescribed Swan conductors swi at each xi. Its geometric fundamental group π1ét(LocG,sw•(X,D)̄) carries a continuous action of GK. The anabelian conjecture asserts that the tuple (X,D,{swi}) can be reconstructed from this Galois representation together with the Swan conductor filtration on the pro-unipotent completion. The Swan conductor filtration is a decreasing filtration Filsw• indexed by rational numbers, whose graded pieces are equipped with a Galois action. The jumps occur precisely at the Swan conductors swi. The wild inertia subgroup Pxi acts on the graded piece of weight swi through a character determined by the refined Swan conductor, which takes values in the Lie algebra of the centralizer of the tame monodromy. This provides a group-theoretic way to read off the Swan conductors from the Galois representation: they are the rational numbers s such that the wild inertia action on grFilsws is non-trivial and factors through the appropriate higher ramification quotient. Reconstruction proceeds in three steps. First, identify the set of conjugacy classes of decomposition groups at the points of D inside π1ét(LocG,sw•(X,D)̄). These are characterized as the maximal subgroups whose abelianization contains a direct summand isomorphic to ℤp(1) on which wild inertia acts non-trivially. Second, recover the Swan conductors swi as the jumps in the Swan conductor filtration on the pro-unipotent completion. Third, the refined Swan conductors are read off from the action of wild inertia on the graded pieces. Once (X,D,{swi}) are known, the stack LocG,sw•(X,D) is uniquely determined. A numerical test is provided by the dimension of the Selmer variety H1f(GK, π1ét(LocG,sw•(X,D)̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the wild ramification condition at each xi (prescribed swi). The expected dimension equals dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σi (dim(G) + swi·dim(G)) for G = GLn, matching the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of connections with irregular singularities computed by the irregular Riemann–Roch theorem (Kedlaya’s formula). Equality of dimensions is a strong consistency check for the anabelian philosophy in the wild ramification setting.

Worked Example: Reading Swan Conductors from Galois Action
Let K = ℚp, X = ℙ1K, D = {0, ∞}. Consider the rank-2 p-adic local system on X D corresponding to the differential equation d2y/dz2 + (a/z2)y = 0 with a ∈ ℤp a unit. The monodromy at 0 is irregular with Swan conductor sw0 = 1. The geometric fundamental group of the moduli stack LocGL2,sw•(X,D) carries a GK-action. Explain how to recover sw0 = 1 from the Galois representation on the pro-unipotent completion.
The pro-unipotent completion Πun of π1ét(LocGL2,sw•(X,D)̄) carries a weight filtration W• and a Swan conductor filtration Filsw•. The wild inertia subgroup P0 ⊂ I0 acts on Πun. The graded piece grFilsw1 is non-zero and P0 acts on it through a non-trivial character factoring through P0(1)/P0(2) ≅ 𝔾a(k) where k is the residue field. The action is given by the refined Swan conductor, which for this equation is the class of a in k. Since a is a unit, the action is non-trivial. For s < 1, grFilsws = 0. For s > 1, P0 acts trivially on grFilsws. Thus the unique jump in the Swan conductor filtration occurs at s = 1, so sw0 = 1. The tame inertia acts trivially on grFilsw1 because the monodromy is purely wild. This group-theoretic characterization — the smallest s such that wild inertia acts non-trivially on grFilsws — recovers the Swan conductor without reference to the differential equation.
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Tame and Wild Ramification in Anabelian Reconstruction
When a question asks about reconstructing ramification data from a Galois representation on a fundamental group, first identify whether the ramification is tame or wild. Tame ramification is detected by the action of the tame inertia quotient Itame ≅ ∏ℓ≠p ℤℓ(1) on the abelianization; the jumps in the weight filtration correspond to the tame characters. Wild ramification is detected by the action of the wild inertia subgroup P on the Swan conductor filtration of the pro-unipotent completion; the jumps correspond to Swan conductors. A common trap is to apply the tame inertia criterion to wild ramification or vice versa. Remember: the tame quotient is prime-to-p and does not see wild ramification; the wild inertia is a pro-p group and requires the Swan conductor filtration on the pro-unipotent completion, not just the weight filtration on the abelianization. In the parabolic case (tame), weights are read from the weight filtration; in the wild case, Swan conductors are read from the Swan conductor filtration. The refined Swan conductor lives in the Lie algebra of the centralizer of the tame monodromy and requires the non-abelian structure of the pro-unipotent completion.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Tame Ramification and the Tame Fundamental Group

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p-adic field K with perfect residue field of characteristic p > 0, and let D = Σi=1r xi be a reduced divisor. A G-local system on X D with tame ramification at xi corresponds to a continuous representation of the tame inertia quotient Ixitame ≅ ∏ℓ≠p ℤℓ(1) into G(ℚp). The moduli stack LocGtame(X,D) parametrizes G-local systems with tame ramification at each xi. Its geometric fundamental group π1ét(LocGtame(X,D)̄) carries a continuous action of GK. The anabelian conjecture asserts that the tuple (X,D,{χi}) can be reconstructed from this Galois representation together with the tame weight filtration on the pro-unipotent completion, where χi : Ixitame → G(ℚp) are the tame monodromy characters. The tame fundamental group π1tame(X D) is the quotient of the étale fundamental group by the closed normal subgroup generated by the wild inertia subgroups. It fits into an exact sequence 1 → π1tame(X D)̄ → π1tame(X D) → GK → 1. The anabelian reconstruction for tame local systems uses the tame fundamental group instead of the full étale fundamental group. The tame weight filtration on the pro-unipotent completion of π1tame(LocGtame(X,D)̄) is a decreasing filtration indexed by rational numbers, whose graded pieces are equipped with an action of the tame inertia quotient. The jumps occur at the tame weights, which are the eigenvalues of the tame monodromy characters χi composed with the cyclotomic character. The tame inertia quotient Ixitame acts on the graded piece of weight λ through the character χi(λ) ⊗ χcycλ. This provides a group-theoretic way to read off the tame weights from the Galois representation: they are the rational numbers λ such that the tame inertia action on grWλ is non-trivial and factors through the tame quotient with the prescribed character. Reconstruction proceeds by first identifying the decomposition groups at the punctures inside π1ét(LocGtame(X,D)̄) as the maximal subgroups whose abelianization contains a direct summand isomorphic to ∏ℓ≠p ℤℓ(1) on which tame inertia acts via a non-trivial character of finite order prime to p. Second, recover the tame monodromy characters χi as the action of tame inertia on the graded pieces of the tame weight filtration. Third, the curve X is reconstructed from the outer action of GK on the tame fundamental group via the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group action on the profinite tame mapping class group. A numerical test is provided by the dimension of the tame Selmer variety H1f,tame(GK, π1ét(LocGtame(X,D)̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the tame ramification condition at each xi. The expected dimension equals dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σi (dim(G) − dim(ZG(χi))), matching the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of tame connections computed by the tame Riemann–Roch theorem.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Parabolic Structure and the Geometric Langlands Correspondence

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p-adic field K and let D = Σi=1r xi be a reduced divisor. Fix a reductive group G over ℚp and parabolic subgroups Pi ⊂ G with weights λi in the cocharacter lattice of the Levi quotient Li = Pi/Ui. The moduli stack LocGpar(X,D,{Pi},{λi}) parametrizes parabolic G-local systems on (X,D). Its geometric fundamental group π1ét(LocGpar(X,D)̄) carries a continuous action of GK. The geometric Langlands correspondence predicts an equivalence between the derived category of D-modules on the moduli stack BunG(X) of G-bundles on X and the derived category of coherent sheaves on the moduli stack LocLG(X) of LG-local systems on X, where LG is the Langlands dual group. In the parabolic setting, this extends to an equivalence between parabolic D-modules on BunGpar(X,D) and coherent sheaves on LocLGpar(X,D) with prescribed singular support. The anabelian geometry of π1ét(LocGpar(X,D)̄) provides a group-theoretic framework for the geometric Langlands correspondence. The parabolic weight filtration on the pro-unipotent completion corresponds under the non-abelian Hodge correspondence to the perverse filtration on the cohomology of the Hitchin fiber. The Galois action on the graded pieces of the parabolic weight filtration encodes the Hecke eigensheaf property: the inertia subgroup Ixi acts on grWλi through the character λi ⊗ χcyc, which matches the eigenvalue of the Hecke operator at xi with weight λi. The anabelian reconstruction of (X,D,{Pi},{λi}) from the Galois representation on π1ét(LocGpar(X,D)̄) is equivalent to the reconstruction of the Hecke eigensheaf from its eigenvalues. The Selmer variety H1f(GK, π1ét(LocGpar(X,D)̄)) parametrizes deformations of the Galois representation preserving the parabolic structure; its tangent space at the trivial deformation is the space of global sections of the adjoint bundle with parabolic boundary conditions, which by the geometric Langlands correspondence is isomorphic to the space of Hecke eigensheaves with the prescribed eigenvalues. The dimension formula dim H1f = dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σi (dim(G) − dim(Pi) + ⟨λi, 2ρ⟩) matches the dimension of the Hitchin base for parabolic Higgs bundles, confirming the compatibility of the anabelian and geometric Langlands perspectives. The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group intertwines with the braid group action on the category of Hecke eigensheaves, providing a group-theoretic realization of the geometric Langlands duality.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Unipotent Monodromy and the Mixed Hodge Structure on the Pro-unipotent Completion

Let X be a smooth projective curve over a p-adic field K and let D = Σi=1r xi be a reduced divisor. A G-local system on X D with unipotent monodromy at xi corresponds to a representation of the fundamental group where the local monodromy at each xi is unipotent. The moduli stack LocGunip(X,D) parametrizes such local systems. Its geometric fundamental group π1ét(LocGunip(X,D)̄) carries a continuous action of GK. The pro-unipotent completion Πun of this fundamental group carries a mixed Hodge structure in the sense of Hain, compatible with the Galois action. The weight filtration W• on Πun is a decreasing filtration indexed by integers, whose graded pieces grWn are pure Hodge structures of weight n. The Hodge filtration F• is a decreasing filtration on the de Rham realization ΠdR compatible with the weight filtration. The anabelian conjecture asserts that the tuple (X,D) can be reconstructed from the Galois representation on Πun together with the mixed Hodge structure. The unipotent monodromy condition implies that the local monodromy logarithm Ni = log(Ti) is nilpotent, where Ti is the monodromy at xi. The weight filtration is characterized by the property that Ni(Wk) ⊂ Wk−2 and Nik : grWk → grW−k is an isomorphism for k ≥ 0 (the relative hard Lefschetz theorem). The Galois action on grWn factors through the tame inertia quotient and is given by the n-th power of the cyclotomic character. This provides a group-theoretic way to read off the unipotent monodromy data: the nilpotent operators Ni are recovered as the action of the inertia subgroup on the graded pieces of the weight filtration. Reconstruction proceeds by first identifying the decomposition groups at the punctures inside π1ét(LocGunip(X,D)̄) as the maximal subgroups whose abelianization contains a direct summand isomorphic to ℤp(1) on which inertia acts unipotently. Second, recover the nilpotent monodromy logarithms Ni as the action of inertia on the weight filtration. Third, the curve X is reconstructed from the outer action of GK on the fundamental group via the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group. A numerical test is provided by the dimension of the Selmer variety H1f(GK, π1ét(LocGunip(X,D)̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the unipotent monodromy condition at each xi. The expected dimension equals dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σi dim(G), matching the dimension of the de Rham moduli space of connections with unipotent monodromy computed by the Riemann–Roch theorem for logarithmic connections with nilpotent residues. The mixed Hodge structure on Πun is compatible with the p-adic Hodge structure via the p-adic comparison isomorphism Πun ⊗ BdR ≅ ΠdR ⊗ BdR, which identifies the weight filtration with the monodromy filtration and the Hodge filtration with the Hodge filtration on the de Rham side. This compatibility is essential for the anabelian reconstruction in the p-adic setting, as it allows the Galois representation on the étale side to be translated into Hodge-theoretic data on the de Rham side.

  • Confusing the tame weight filtration (indexed by rational numbers, compatible with tame inertia) with the Swan conductor filtration (indexed by rational numbers, compatible with wild inertia) or the mixed Hodge weight filtration (indexed by integers, compatible with unipotent monodromy); each filtration lives on a different pro-unipotent completion and detects different ramification phenomena.
  • Assuming the Deligne–Simpson problem for general reductive G is solved by the same eigenvalue criteria as for GLn; the non-abelian centralizer structure makes the problem substantially harder and the Selmer variety dimension formula involves simultaneous centralizers.
  • Treating the tame fundamental group as a quotient of the étale fundamental group by the wild inertia subgroups without noting that this quotient is not functorial in the curve; the tame fundamental group of a moduli stack requires a separate construction.
  • Equating the geometric Langlands correspondence for parabolic local systems with the anabelian reconstruction of parabolic data; the former is a categorical equivalence of derived categories, the latter is a group-theoretic reconstruction of the curve and ramification data from the Galois representation.
  • Neglecting that the mixed Hodge structure on the pro-unipotent completion of the moduli stack of unipotent local systems requires the p-adic comparison isomorphism to relate the étale Galois representation to the de Rham Hodge filtration; the weight filtration on the étale side is not the Hodge filtration.
  • Assuming the dimension formula for the Selmer variety in the wild ramification case is the same as the tame case with Swan conductors added; the irregular Riemann–Roch formula (Kedlaya) includes contributions from the irregularity modules that are not captured by the Swan conductors alone.
  • Overlooking that the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group is outer, while the Galois action on the fundamental group of the moduli stack is inner up to the outer action; the anabelian reconstruction uses the outer action to recover the curve, but the inner action encodes the ramification data.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p-adic Local Systems on Curves with Irregular Singularities and the Stokes Filtration

The study of p-adic local systems on curves with irregular singularities requires a refinement of the tame weight filtration used in the regular singular case. When a connection has irregular singularities at a divisor D = Σ x_i on a smooth projective curve X over a p-adic field K, the local monodromy is no longer quasi-unipotent; instead, the formal local solutions involve exponential factors exp(φ_i) where φ_i are rational functions with poles at x_i. The Stokes filtration, introduced by Stokes in the complex analytic setting and adapted to the p-adic context by Kedlaya, Mochizuki, and others, is a decreasing filtration on the fiber of the local system at a tangential basepoint near each irregular singularity, indexed by the slopes of the irregularity. For a connection ∇ on a vector bundle E over X D with irregular singularities along D, the irregularity module at x_i is the cokernel of the map ∇: E → E ⊗ Ω1_X(log D) localized at x_i; its dimension is the irregularity index irr_x_i(∇). The Stokes filtration Fil^•_Stokes on the stalk of the associated p-adic local system V at a tangential basepoint →_x_i is characterized by the property that the graded pieces gr^α_Stokes V carry an action of the wild inertia subgroup I_wild ⊂ G_K with Swan conductor α. The anabelian reconstruction of the pair (X, D, ∇) from the Galois representation on the fundamental group of the moduli stack LocSys_G(X, D, irregular) requires identifying the Stokes filtration inside the pro-unipotent completion of the fundamental group. The key mechanism is the p-adic Riemann-Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections (Diao-Lan-Liu-Zhu), which identifies the category of p-adic local systems with irregular singularities with the category of (φ, ∇)-modules on the Robba ring with prescribed slope filtrations. The Stokes filtration on the étale side corresponds to the slope filtration on the (φ, ∇)-module side. The Galois representation on π1^ét(LocSys_G(X, D, irregular)_K̄) factors through the wild inertia quotient, and the Swan conductor of the representation on each graded piece of the Stokes filtration recovers the irregularity data. The dimension of the Selmer variety H1_f(G_K, π1^ét(LocSys_G(X, D, irregular)_K̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the Stokes filtration condition at each x_i is given by the irregular Riemann-Roch formula of Kedlaya: dim = dim(G)·(g-1) + 1 + Σ_i (dim(G) + irr_x_i(∇)·rank(G)). This formula includes contributions from the irregularity modules that are not captured by the Swan conductors alone. The anabelian conjecture asserts that the tuple (X, D, ∇) can be reconstructed from the Galois representation on the fundamental group together with the Stokes filtration. The reconstruction proceeds by first identifying the decomposition groups at the irregular singularities inside π1^ét(LocSys_G(X, D, irregular)_K̄) as the maximal subgroups whose abelianization contains a direct summand isomorphic to ℤ_p(1) on which wild inertia acts with non-trivial Swan conductor. Second, recover the Stokes filtration as the filtration by the action of wild inertia on the graded pieces of the abelianization. Third, the curve X and the connection ∇ are reconstructed from the outer action of G_K on the fundamental group via the Grothendieck-Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group with irregular singularities. A critical distinction is that the Stokes filtration lives on the pro-unipotent completion of the fundamental group of the moduli stack of local systems with irregular singularities, while the tame weight filtration lives on the pro-unipotent completion of the moduli stack of local systems with regular singularities; these are different completions detecting different ramification phenomena.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p-adic Local Systems on Higher-Dimensional Varieties with the p-adic Non-abelian Hodge Theory of Liu-Zhu

The extension of anabelian geometry to higher-dimensional varieties over p-adic fields has been revolutionized by the p-adic non-abelian Hodge theory developed by Liu and Zhu (2017) using the language of perfectoid spaces and the pro-étale site. For a smooth proper variety X over a p-adic field K, the classical anabelian conjectures (Grothendieck's section conjecture, the birational anabelian conjecture) are known to fail in general due to the presence of non-hyperbolic subvarieties and the failure of the Neukirch-Uchida property for higher-dimensional function fields. However, the Liu-Zhu theory provides a new framework by constructing a p-adic Simpson correspondence: an equivalence of categories between the category of p-adic local systems on X (i.e., ℤ_p-local systems on the pro-étale site X_proét) and the category of Higgs bundles on X with nilpotent Higgs field, up to a certain nilpotence condition. This correspondence is realized via the period sheaves B_dR+ and B_HT on the pro-étale site, and the fundamental group π1^proét(X, x̄) plays the role of the étale fundamental group in this setting. The pro-étale fundamental group is a profinite group that classifies finite étale covers in the pro-étale topology; it fits into an exact sequence 1 → π1^proét(X_K̄, x̄) → π1^proét(X, x̄) → G_K → 1. The anabelian reconstruction problem for higher-dimensional varieties is then reformulated as: can one recover X from the Galois representation of G_K on π1^proét(X_K̄, x̄) together with the p-adic Hodge structure provided by the Liu-Zhu correspondence? The key new ingredient is the p-adic period map π1^proét(X_K̄, x̄) → π1^dR(X) ⊗ B_dR, which identifies the pro-étale fundamental group with the de Rham fundamental group after tensoring with B_dR. The Hodge filtration on the de Rham side corresponds to the filtration on the pro-étale side induced by the action of the Galois group on the period sheaf B_dR+. The anabelian conjecture for higher-dimensional varieties in this setting asserts that a smooth proper variety X over K with ample canonical bundle (or more generally, of general type) can be reconstructed from the tuple (π1^proét(X_K̄, x̄), G_K-action, Hodge filtration). The evidence comes from the work of Lan-Liu-Zhu on the p-adic non-abelian Hodge theory for Shimura varieties, where the period map is shown to be a local isomorphism on the moduli space of p-adic local systems. For abelian varieties, the pro-étale fundamental group is the Tate module T_p(A), and the Galois representation on T_p(A) determines the abelian variety up to isogeny by the Tate conjecture (proved by Faltings). For curves of genus ≥ 2, the pro-étale fundamental group coincides with the étale fundamental group, and the anabelian conjecture reduces to the classical one. For higher-dimensional hyperbolic varieties (e.g., varieties with ample cotangent bundle), the pro-étale fundamental group captures the anabelian information. The obstruction to reconstruction is measured by the Selmer variety H1_f(G_K, π1^proét(X_K̄, x̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the Hodge-Tate condition at the infinite places. The expected dimension is given by the p-adic Riemann-Roch formula for the de Rham fundamental group, which involves the Hodge numbers hp,q(X). The Liu-Zhu theory also provides a group-theoretic characterization of the Hodge filtration: it is the filtration on π1^proét(X_K̄, x̄) ⊗ B_dR induced by the action of the Galois group on the graded pieces gr^i B_dR ≅ C_p(i). This allows the Hodge filtration to be read off from the Galois representation alone, without reference to the de Rham fundamental group. The anabelian reconstruction then proceeds by first recovering the Hodge filtration from the Galois action on the period sheaf, second recovering the de Rham fundamental group via the period isomorphism, and third recovering the variety X from the de Rham fundamental group via the classical Riemann-Hilbert correspondence (Simpson's non-abelian Hodge theory in characteristic zero). This program connects the p-adic anabelian geometry of higher-dimensional varieties to the complex non-abelian Hodge theory of Simpson and the p-adic Hodge theory of Fontaine-Faltings.

Worked Example: Stokes Filtration and Irregularity Index
Let X = ℙ1_ℚ_p {0, ∞} and consider the rank 2 connection ∇ = d + A(z) dz on the trivial bundle 𝒪2 over X, where A(z) = [[0, 1], [z-3, 0]]. The connection has an irregular singularity at z = 0 with Poincaré rank 2 (pole order 3) and a regular singularity at z = ∞.
