Compute the irregularity index at z = 0 and describe the Stokes filtration on the associated p-adic local system V at a tangential basepoint →0.
The connection matrix A(z) has a pole of order 3 at z = 0, so the Poincaré rank is r = 2. The irregularity index for a rank n connection with Poincaré rank r is irr = n·r = 2·2 = 4. The formal local solutions at z = 0 are of the form exp(φ(z)) · z^Λ · F(z) where φ(z) = diag(λ1 z-2, λ2 z-2) with λ1, λ2 the eigenvalues of the leading coefficient matrix [[0, 0], [1, 0]] which are both 0. Since the leading coefficient is nilpotent, the exponential factors are trivial (φ = 0), but the irregularity comes from the nilpotent part. The Stokes filtration Fil^α_Stokes V at →0 is indexed by slopes α ∈ (1/r)ℤ = (1/2)ℤ. The graded pieces gr^α_Stokes V correspond to the eigenspaces of the action of the wild inertia subgroup I_wild with Swan conductor α. For this connection, the Swan conductor is 2 (the Poincaré rank), so the Stokes filtration has jumps at α = 1/2, 1, 3/2, 2. The dimension of gr^α_Stokes V is 1 for each α = 1/2, 1, 3/2, 2, and 0 otherwise. The total dimension is 4 = irr. The Galois representation on V factors through the wild inertia quotient, and the action on gr^α_Stokes V is via the α-th power of the fundamental character of level 2. The anabelian reconstruction recovers the irregularity index 4 from the Swan conductor of the Galois representation on the abelianization of the decomposition group at 0, and recovers the Stokes filtration as the filtration by the action of wild inertia on the graded pieces.
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Filtrations in Anabelian Geometry
Questions often test whether you can distinguish the tame weight filtration (indexed by integers, compatible with unipotent monodromy, lives on pro-unipotent completion of moduli of regular singular local systems), the Stokes filtration (indexed by rational numbers, compatible with wild inertia/Swan conductor, lives on pro-unipotent completion of moduli of irregular singular local systems), and the mixed Hodge weight filtration (indexed by integers, compatible with Hodge theory, lives on de Rham side). Memorize the table: Tame weight filtration → regular singular, unipotent monodromy, N_i nilpotent, weight filtration W_•. Stokes filtration → irregular singular, wild inertia, Swan conductor, slope filtration. Mixed Hodge weight filtration → de Rham side, Hodge filtration F^•, weight filtration W_•, comparison isomorphism. The p-adic comparison isomorphism identifies the tame weight filtration with the monodromy filtration on the de Rham side, and the Stokes filtration with the slope filtration on the (φ, ∇)-module side. Never confuse the indexing sets (ℤ vs ℚ) or the inertia subgroups (tame vs wild).

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p-adic Local Systems on Curves with Logarithmic Connections and the Irregular Hodge Filtration

The irregular Hodge filtration, introduced by Deligne and developed by Sabbah and Mochizuki in the complex analytic setting, and adapted to the p-adic setting by Kedlaya and others, is a decreasing filtration F^•_irr on the de Rham cohomology of a meromorphic connection with irregular singularities. For a logarithmic connection ∇: E → E ⊗ Ω1_X(log D) on a vector bundle E over a smooth projective curve X over a p-adic field K, with irregular singularities along a divisor D = Σ x_i, the irregular Hodge filtration is defined on the de Rham complex DR(E, ∇) = [E → E ⊗ Ω1_X(log D)] by F^p_irr DR = Im(τ≤ -p DR → DR) where τ is the stupid filtration shifted by the irregularity data. Concretely, at each irregular singularity x_i, the connection has a formal decomposition into exponential factors exp(φi,j) times regular singular connections. The irregular Hodge filtration is the filtration induced by the pole order of the φi,j on the de Rham complex. The key property is that the graded pieces gr^p_F_irr H1_dR(X, E, ∇) carry a pure Hodge structure of weight p + 1 in the complex setting, and a pure p-adic Hodge structure of weight p + 1 in the p-adic setting via the p-adic comparison isomorphism. The anabelian significance of the irregular Hodge filtration is that it provides a Hodge-theoretic invariant of the connection that can be read off from the Galois representation on the fundamental group of the moduli stack LocSys_G(X, D, irregular). The p-adic Riemann-Hilbert correspondence for irregular connections (Diao-Lan-Liu-Zhu) identifies the category of p-adic local systems with irregular singularities with the category of (φ, ∇)-modules on the Robba ring with slope filtrations. Under this correspondence, the irregular Hodge filtration on the de Rham side corresponds to the filtration on the (φ, ∇)-module induced by the slope filtration. The Galois representation on π1^ét(LocSys_G(X, D, irregular)_K̄) determines the (φ, ∇)-module via the Fontaine functor, and the slope filtration on the (φ, ∇)-module determines the irregular Hodge filtration via the comparison isomorphism. The anabelian conjecture for irregular connections asserts that the tuple (X, D, ∇) can be reconstructed from the Galois representation on the fundamental group together with the irregular Hodge filtration. The reconstruction proceeds by first recovering the irregular Hodge filtration from the Galois action on the period sheaf B_dR (the filtration on B_dR+ induces the Hodge filtration on the de Rham cohomology via the comparison isomorphism). Second, the irregularity data (the exponential factors φi,j) are recovered as the jumps in the irregular Hodge filtration. Third, the curve X and the connection ∇ are reconstructed from the outer action of G_K on the fundamental group via the Grothendieck-Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group with irregular singularities. A numerical test is provided by the dimension of the Selmer variety H1_f(G_K, π1^ét(LocSys_G(X, D, irregular)_K̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the irregular Hodge filtration condition at each x_i. The expected dimension is given by the irregular Riemann-Roch formula of Kedlaya, which includes the irregular Hodge numbers hp,q_irr = dim gr^p_F_irr H^q_dR(X, E, ∇). The irregular Hodge numbers satisfy the symmetry hp,q_irr = hq,p_irr (irregular Hodge symmetry) and the irregular Hodge filtration satisfies the Griffiths transversality condition ∇(F^p_irr) ⊂ Fp-1_irr ⊗ Ω1_X(log D). These properties are group-theoretically detectable from the Galois representation: the irregular Hodge symmetry corresponds to the duality on the fundamental group induced by the cup product, and the Griffiths transversality corresponds to the action of the Galois group on the Lie algebra of the fundamental group. The irregular Hodge filtration is distinct from the Hodge filtration on the de Rham cohomology of the underlying vector bundle with regular singular connection; it is a refinement that captures the irregularity data. Confusing the two filtrations is a common error: the Hodge filtration is defined for any vector bundle with connection, while the irregular Hodge filtration is only defined for connections with irregular singularities and incorporates the exponential factors.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p-adic Local Systems on the Moduli Stack of Parabolic Higgs Bundles and the Non-abelian Hodge Correspondence

The non-abelian Hodge correspondence, established by Corlette, Donaldson, Hitchin, and Simpson in the complex analytic setting, and extended to the p-adic setting by Faltings, Tsuji, and Liu-Zhu, provides an equivalence between the category of semistable Higgs bundles with vanishing Chern classes on a smooth projective variety X and the category of semisimple local systems on X. For curves with parabolic structure (X, D) where D = Σ x_i is a divisor of marked points, the parabolic non-abelian Hodge correspondence (Konno, Nakajima, Biquard-Boalch) relates parabolic Higgs bundles on (X, D) with parabolic local systems on X D. A parabolic Higgs bundle is a Higgs bundle (E, θ) on X together with a flag filtration Ex_i,1 ⊂ Ex_i,2 ⊂ ... ⊂ Ex_i,l_i = E|x_i at each marked point x_i and weights 0 ≤ αi,1 < αi,2 < ... < αi,l_i < 1 such that the Higgs field θ preserves the flag and acts on the graded pieces with eigenvalues given by the weights. A parabolic local system is a local system on X D together with a quasi-unipotent monodromy at each x_i whose eigenvalues are exp(2πi αi,j). The moduli stack M_Higgs^par(X, D) of semistable parabolic Higgs bundles and the moduli stack M_Betti^par(X, D) of parabolic local systems are related by the parabolic non-abelian Hodge correspondence, which is a homeomorphism (in fact, a diffeomorphism on the smooth locus) preserving the natural symplectic structures. The anabelian geometry of the fundamental group of these moduli stacks enters through the action of the absolute Galois group G_K on the étale fundamental group π1^ét(M_Betti^par(X, D)_K̄). The parabolic section conjecture (a variant of Grothendieck's section conjecture for curves with marked points) asserts that the set of rational points of the moduli stack M_Betti^par(X, D) (which correspond to parabolic local systems arising from geometry) is in bijection with the set of splittings of the exact sequence 1 → π1^ét(M_Betti^par(X, D)_K̄) → π1^ét(M_Betti^par(X, D)) → G_K → 1 that satisfy certain local conditions at the marked points. The local conditions are encoded by the parabolic weights: a splitting corresponds to a rational point if and only if the induced map on the inertia subgroups at the marked points matches the parabolic weight data. The anabelian reconstruction of the parabolic curve (X, D, α) from the Galois representation on π1^ét(M_Betti^par(X, D)_K̄) proceeds by first identifying the decomposition groups at the marked points inside the fundamental group as the maximal subgroups whose abelianization contains a direct summand isomorphic to ℤ_p(1) on which inertia acts with eigenvalues given by the parabolic weights. Second, the parabolic weights αi,j are recovered as the logarithms of the eigenvalues of the inertia action. Third, the curve X and the divisor D are reconstructed from the outer action of G_K on the fundamental group via the Grothendieck-Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group with marked points. The dimension of the Selmer variety H1_f(G_K, π1^ét(M_Betti^par(X, D)_K̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the parabolic weight condition at each x_i is given by the parabolic Riemann-Roch formula: dim = dim(G)·(g-1) + 1 + Σ_i Σ_j (dim(G) - dim(C_G(exp(2πi αi,j)))) where C_G is the centralizer in G. This formula matches the dimension of the moduli space of parabolic Higgs bundles computed by the parabolic Riemann-Roch theorem. The p-adic non-abelian Hodge correspondence (Liu-Zhu) identifies the Galois representation on π1^ét(M_Betti^par(X, D)_K̄) with the Higgs bundle data on M_Higgs^par(X, D) via the period map. The parabolic weights correspond to the Hodge-Tate weights of the Galois representation. The anabelian conjecture for parabolic Higgs bundles asserts that the tuple (X, D, α) can be reconstructed from the Galois representation on the fundamental group of the moduli stack of parabolic Higgs bundles together with the Hodge filtration. This connects the anabelian geometry of moduli stacks to the geometric Langlands correspondence: the Hecke eigensheaves on M_Higgs^par(X, D) correspond to Galois representations on π1^ét(M_Betti^par(X, D)_K̄), and the anabelian reconstruction recovers the curve from the Galois representation.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p-adic Local Systems on Curves with Wild Ramification and the Swan Conductor Filtration

In the wild ramification case, the tame fundamental group is insufficient to capture the full ramification data; one must work with the full étale fundamental group and its Swan conductor filtration. For a smooth projective curve X over a p-adic field K with a divisor D = Σ x_i, and a p-adic local system V on X D with wild ramification at some x_i, the Swan conductor sw_x_i(V) is a non-negative integer measuring the wild ramification of V at x_i. The Swan conductor filtration is a decreasing filtration Fil^α_Swan V on the stalk of V at a geometric point over x_i, indexed by α ∈ ℚ≥0, characterized by the property that the graded pieces gr^α_Swan V are the maximal subquotients on which the wild inertia subgroup I_wild acts with Swan conductor exactly α. The Swan conductor filtration is distinct from the Stokes filtration: the Stokes filtration is defined for irregular connections (exponential factors), while the Swan conductor filtration is defined for arbitrary p-adic local systems with wild ramification (no exponential factors required). For a local system arising from a connection with regular singularities but wild ramification (e.g., a local system with non-trivial p-power monodromy), the Swan conductor filtration captures the wild ramification while the Stokes filtration is trivial. The anabelian geometry of the moduli stack LocSys_G(X, D, wild) of G-local systems with wild ramification involves the fundamental group π1^ét(LocSys_G(X, D, wild)_K̄) equipped with the Swan conductor filtration. The Galois representation of G_K on this fundamental group factors through the wild inertia quotient, and the Swan conductor of the representation on each graded piece of the filtration recovers the wild ramification data. The anabelian conjecture for wild ramification asserts that the tuple (X, D, V) can be reconstructed from the Galois representation on the fundamental group together with the Swan conductor filtration. The reconstruction proceeds by first identifying the decomposition groups at the wildly ramified points inside the fundamental group as the maximal subgroups whose abelianization contains a direct summand isomorphic to ℤ_p(1) on which wild inertia acts with non-trivial Swan conductor. Second, the Swan conductor filtration is recovered as the filtration by the action of wild inertia on the graded pieces of the abelianization. Third, the curve X and the local system V are reconstructed from the outer action of G_K on the fundamental group via the Grothendieck-Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group with wild ramification. A numerical test is provided by the dimension of the Selmer variety H1_f(G_K, π1^ét(LocSys_G(X, D, wild)_K̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the Swan conductor condition at each x_i. The expected dimension is given by the wild Riemann-Roch formula (Kedlaya): dim = dim(G)·(g-1) + 1 + Σ_i (dim(G) + sw_x_i(V)·rank(G)). This formula includes the Swan conductors sw_x_i(V) which are not captured by the tame ramification data alone. The Swan conductor filtration is compatible with the p-adic Hodge theory via the p-adic comparison isomorphism: the filtration on the étale side corresponds to the filtration on the de Rham side induced by the monodromy operator N = log(T) where T is the monodromy operator (which is not quasi-unipotent in the wild case). The graded pieces gr^α_Swan V carry an action of the wild inertia group with Swan conductor α, and the Galois action on gr^α_Swan V ⊗ B_dR identifies the Swan conductor filtration with the monodromy filtration on the de Rham side. A critical distinction is that the Swan conductor filtration lives on the pro-unipotent completion of the fundamental group of the moduli stack of local systems with wild ramification, while the tame weight filtration lives on the pro-unipotent completion of the moduli stack of local systems with tame ramification; these are different completions. The wild fundamental group is not a quotient of the étale fundamental group by the wild inertia subgroups in a functorial way; the moduli stack of local systems with wild ramification requires a separate construction using the theory of (φ, Γ)-modules or the Fargues-Fontaine curve. The anabelian geometry of wild ramification is essential for the p-adic Langlands correspondence, where the Galois representations of interest (e.g., those arising from modular forms) typically have wild ramification at p.

  • Confusing the Stokes filtration (for irregular connections, indexed by slopes of exponential factors) with the Swan conductor filtration (for wild ramification, indexed by Swan conductors of arbitrary local systems); they live on different moduli stacks and detect different phenomena.
  • Assuming the irregular Hodge filtration is the same as the Hodge filtration on the de Rham cohomology of the underlying vector bundle; the irregular Hodge filtration incorporates the exponential factors and is only defined for irregular connections.
  • Treating the pro-étale fundamental group of a higher-dimensional variety as a direct replacement for the étale fundamental group in all anabelian conjectures; the pro-étale fundamental group classifies pro-étale covers, and the anabelian reconstruction requires the additional Hodge filtration data from the Liu-Zhu period map.
  • Equating the parabolic non-abelian Hodge correspondence (a homeomorphism of moduli stacks) with the anabelian reconstruction of parabolic data from the Galois representation; the former is a geometric correspondence, the latter is a group-theoretic reconstruction using the section conjecture for moduli stacks.
  • Neglecting that the dimension formula for the Selmer variety in the wild ramification case involves Swan conductors, while in the irregular singularity case it involves irregularity indices, and in the parabolic case it involves centralizer dimensions of the monodromy eigenvalues; each formula is distinct and corresponds to a different filtration.
  • Overlooking that the Liu-Zhu p-adic non-abelian Hodge theory for higher-dimensional varieties requires the variety to be smooth and proper, and the local systems to be de Rham; the correspondence does not hold for arbitrary local systems or non-proper varieties without additional conditions.
  • Assuming the Grothendieck-Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group with wild ramification or irregular singularities is the same as the action on the tame mapping class group; the presence of wild ramification or irregular singularities modifies the mapping class group and the GT action, and the anabelian reconstruction uses the modified action.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p-adic Local Systems on Curves with Non-abelian Ramification Theory

The moduli stack LocSysG(X, D, wild) of G-local systems on a smooth projective curve X over a p-adic field K with prescribed wild ramification at a divisor D = Σ xi carries a fundamental group that encodes the anabelian data of wild ramification. Unlike the tame case where the fundamental group is a quotient of the étale fundamental group of X D, the wild fundamental group requires the theory of (φ, Γ)-modules over the Robba ring or the Fargues–Fontaine curve to capture the Swan conductor filtration. The outer action of the absolute Galois group GK on π1ét(LocSysG(X, D, wild)K̄) factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group GT acting on the profinite mapping class group with wild ramification. This action is recovered from the Galois representation on the Tate module of the Jacobian of X together with the Swan conductor data at each xi. The reconstruction proceeds in three steps. First, the inertia subgroups at each xi are identified as the stabilizers of the canonical filtration on the pro-unipotent completion of the fundamental group whose graded pieces carry a GK-action with a distinguished summand isomorphic to ℤp(1) on which wild inertia acts with non-trivial Swan conductor. Second, the Swan conductor filtration is recovered as the filtration by the action of wild inertia on the graded pieces of the abelianization. Third, the curve X and the local system V are reconstructed from the outer action of GK on the fundamental group via the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group with wild ramification. A numerical test is provided by the dimension of the Selmer variety H1f(GK, π1ét(LocSysG(X, D, wild)K̄)) defined by the crystalline condition at p and the Swan conductor condition at each xi. The expected dimension is given by the wild Riemann–Roch formula (Kedlaya): dim = dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σi (dim(G) + swxi(V)·rank(G)). This formula includes the Swan conductors swxi(V) which are not captured by the tame ramification data alone. The Swan conductor filtration is compatible with the p-adic Hodge theory via the p-adic comparison isomorphism: the filtration on the étale side corresponds to the filtration on the de Rham side induced by the monodromy operator N = log(T) where T is the monodromy operator (which is not quasi-unipotent in the wild case). The graded pieces grαSwan V carry an action of the wild inertia group with Swan conductor α, and the Galois action on grαSwan V ⊗ BdR identifies the Swan conductor filtration with the monodromy filtration on the de Rham side. A critical distinction is that the Swan conductor filtration lives on the pro-unipotent completion of the fundamental group of the moduli stack of local systems with wild ramification, while the tame weight filtration lives on the pro-unipotent completion of the moduli stack of local systems with tame ramification; these are different completions. The wild fundamental group is not a quotient of the étale fundamental group by the wild inertia subgroups in a functorial way; the moduli stack of local systems with wild ramification requires a separate construction using the theory of (φ, Γ)-modules or the Fargues–Fontaine curve. The anabelian geometry of wild ramification is essential for the p-adic Langlands correspondence, where the Galois representations of interest (e.g., those arising from modular forms) typically have wild ramification at p.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p-adic Local Systems with Irregular Singularities: Stokes Data and the Wild Riemann–Hilbert Correspondence

For a smooth projective curve X over a p-adic field K and a divisor D = Σ xi, the moduli stack ConnG(X, D, irr) of G-connections with irregular singularities at D carries a fundamental group whose anabelian geometry is governed by the Stokes data. The irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence (Kedlaya, Mochizuki) establishes an equivalence between the category of meromorphic connections with irregular singularities and the category of Stokes-filtered local systems. The Stokes filtration is indexed by the slopes of the exponential factors at each irregular singularity, and the Stokes matrices encode the non-abelian monodromy data. The fundamental group π1ét(ConnG(X, D, irr)K̄) fits into an exact sequence relating it to the fundamental group of the moduli stack of local systems with wild ramification and the Stokes data. The outer action of GK on this fundamental group factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acting on the profinite mapping class group with irregular singularities. The reconstruction of the curve X and the connection ∇ from the Galois representation proceeds by first recovering the irregular type (the formal normal form) from the action of wild inertia on the graded pieces of the Stokes filtration. The irregular Hodge filtration (introduced by Sabbah and Yu) on the de Rham cohomology of the connection incorporates the exponential factors and is distinct from the usual Hodge filtration. The dimension of the Selmer variety H1f(GK, π1ét(ConnG(X, D, irr)K̄)) is given by the irregular Riemann–Roch formula: dim = dim(G)·(g−1) + 1 + Σi (dim(G) + irrxi(∇)·rank(G)), where irrxi(∇) is the irregularity index at xi. This formula differs from the wild ramification formula by replacing Swan conductors with irregularity indices. The Stokes filtration is compatible with the p-adic Hodge theory via the p-adic irregular Riemann–Hilbert correspondence (Diao–Lan–Liu–Zhu): the Stokes filtration on the étale side corresponds to the irregular Hodge filtration on the de Rham side. The graded pieces grαStokes V carry an action of the wild inertia group with slope α, and the Galois action identifies the Stokes filtration with the monodromy filtration on the de Rham side twisted by the exponential factors. A critical distinction is that the Stokes filtration (for irregular connections, indexed by slopes of exponential factors) and the Swan conductor filtration (for wild ramification, indexed by Swan conductors of arbitrary local systems) live on different moduli stacks and detect different phenomena. The irregular Hodge filtration is not the same as the Hodge filtration on the de Rham cohomology of the underlying vector bundle; it incorporates the exponential factors and is only defined for irregular connections. The pro-étale fundamental group of a higher-dimensional variety is not a direct replacement for the étale fundamental group in all anabelian conjectures; the pro-étale fundamental group classifies pro-étale covers, and the anabelian reconstruction requires the additional Hodge filtration data from the Liu–Zhu period map. The parabolic non-abelian Hodge correspondence (a homeomorphism of moduli stacks) is not the same as the anabelian reconstruction of parabolic data from the Galois representation; the former is a geometric correspondence, the latter is a group-theoretic reconstruction using the section conjecture for moduli stacks.

Worked Example: Distinguishing Wild Ramification from Irregular Singularities in Anabelian Reconstruction
Let X = ℙ1ℚp {0, 1, ∞} and consider two G = GL2 local systems on X: (1) a local system V with wild ramification at 0 of Swan conductor 2 and tame ramification at 1, ∞; (2) a connection ∇ with an irregular singularity at 0 of irregularity index 2 (slope 1/2) and regular singularities at 1, ∞. Explain why the anabelian reconstruction of V and ∇ from the outer Galois action on their respective fundamental groups uses different filtrations and different dimension formulas for the Selmer varieties.
For V (wild ramification), the relevant moduli stack is LocSysGL2(X, {0}, wild). The fundamental group π1ét(LocSysGL2(X, {0}, wild)ℚ̄p) carries the Swan conductor filtration indexed by α ∈ ℚ≥0. The inertia subgroup at 0 is recovered as the stabilizer of the filtration whose graded piece gr2Swan V has a ℤp(1) summand with Swan conductor 2. The Selmer variety dimension is dim = 4·(0−1) + 1 + (4 + 2·2) = −4 + 1 + 8 = 5 (using g=0, dim(GL2)=4, sw0=2, rank=2). For ∇ (irregular singularity), the relevant moduli stack is ConnGL2(X, {0}, irr). The fundamental group carries the Stokes filtration indexed by slopes α ∈ ℚ>0. The irregular type at 0 (slope 1/2) is recovered from the action of wild inertia on gr1/2Stokes ∇. The Selmer variety dimension is dim = 4·(0−1) + 1 + (4 + 2·2) = 5 (using irr0=2). Although the numerical dimensions coincide in this example, the filtrations are different: the Swan filtration is on the pro-unipotent completion of LocSysGL2(X, {0}, wild), while the Stokes filtration is on the pro-unipotent completion of ConnGL2(X, {0}, irr). The Galois action on gr2Swan V identifies the Swan filtration with the monodromy filtration on the de Rham side, while the Galois action on gr1/2Stokes ∇ identifies the Stokes filtration with the monodromy filtration twisted by the exponential factor exp(z−1/2). The reconstruction of V uses the GT action on the mapping class group with wild ramification; the reconstruction of ∇ uses the GT action on the mapping class group with irregular singularities. These are distinct group actions on distinct profinite groups.
Exam Strategy: Filtration Identification
When a question asks to distinguish between wild ramification and irregular singularities in an anabelian context, always identify: (1) the moduli stack (LocSys vs Conn), (2) the filtration (Swan conductor vs Stokes), (3) the indexing set (Swan conductors α ∈ ℚ≥0 vs slopes α ∈ ℚ>0), (4) the dimension formula (Swan conductors vs irregularity indices), and (5) the de Rham counterpart (monodromy filtration vs irregular Hodge filtration twisted by exponentials). The numerical dimension may coincide accidentally; the structural difference is in the filtration and the moduli stack.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of Perfectoid Shimura Varieties at Infinite Level and the Kottwitz Conjecture

Let ShK(G, X) be a Shimura variety of Hodge type associated to a reductive group G over ℚ and a Shimura datum (G, X). The inverse limit Sh∞(G, X) = lim← K ShK(G, X) over all compact open subgroups K ⊂ G(𝔸f) is a perfectoid space (Scholze). Its étale fundamental group π1ét(Sh∞(G, X)C̄) is trivial, but the pro-étale fundamental group π1proét(Sh∞(G, X)C̄) classifies pro-étale covers and is isomorphic to the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the associated Hermitian symmetric domain. The anabelian geometry of Sh∞ is governed by the action of the absolute Galois group Gℚ on the tower of Shimura varieties. The Kottwitz conjecture (proved by Kottwitz, Harris–Taylor, Shin, and others) describes the ℓ-adic cohomology of ShK in terms of automorphic representations. In anabelian terms, the outer action of Gℚ on the fundamental group of the moduli stack of p-adic local systems on Sh∞ recovers the Langlands parameters. The moduli stack LocSysG(Sh∞, p-adic) of p-adic local systems on the perfectoid Shimura variety carries a fundamental group whose outer Galois action factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acting on the profinite mapping class group of the infinite-level Shimura variety. The reconstruction of the Shimura datum (G, X) from this Galois action proceeds via the p-adic Hodge theory of the perfectoid Shimura variety: the Hodge–Tate period map πHT: Sh∞ → FlG,μ to the flag variety is Gℚp-equivariant and its fibers are the p-adic uniformization data. The fundamental group of the moduli stack of p-adic Hodge–Tate period maps for Shimura varieties fits into an exact sequence relating it to the fundamental group of the moduli stack of p-adic local systems on Sh∞ and the fundamental group of the flag variety. The dimension of the Selmer variety H1f(Gℚ, π1ét(LocSysG(Sh∞)ℚ̄)) is given by the Kottwitz formula involving the stable trace formula and the Euler–Poincaré characteristic of the automorphic representations. The anabelian geometry of perfectoid Shimura varieties connects the section conjecture for the moduli stack of curves (via the uniformization of Shimura curves) with the Langlands program. A critical point is that the pro-étale fundamental group of Sh∞ is not the étale fundamental group; the anabelian reconstruction uses the pro-étale fundamental group together with the Hodge–Tate period map. The Kottwitz conjecture provides the numerical test for the anabelian reconstruction: the dimension of the Selmer variety matches the multiplicity of the automorphic representation in the cohomology.

Anabelian Geometry and the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p-adic (φ, Γ)-modules over the Robba Ring: The Colmez Functor and the Breuil–Schneider Conjecture

The category of p-adic Galois representations of Gℚp is equivalent to the category of étale (φ, Γ)-modules over the Robba ring ℛ (Fontaine, Cherbonnier–Colmez). The moduli stack Modφ,Γ(ℛ) of (φ, Γ)-modules of rank d carries a fundamental group whose anabelian geometry encodes the p-adic Langlands correspondence for GLd(ℚp). The Colmez functor (Colmez) associates to a trianguline (φ, Γ)-module a locally analytic representation of GLd(ℚp). The Breuil–Schneider conjecture (proved by Breuil–Schneider, extended by Colmez–Dospinescu–Paškūnas) describes the locally analytic vectors in the p-adic Banach space representations of GLd(ℚp) arising from the p-adic Langlands correspondence. In anabelian terms, the outer action of Gℚp on the fundamental group π1ét(Modφ,Γ(ℛ)ℚ̄p) factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acting on the profinite mapping class group of the Fargues–Fontaine curve. The reconstruction of a p-adic Galois representation V from the Galois action on the fundamental group of the moduli stack of (φ, Γ)-modules proceeds by first recovering the (φ, Γ)-module Drig(V) as the fiber of the universal (φ, Γ)-module over the point corresponding to V. The triangulation of Drig(V) (when it exists) is recovered from the filtration on the fundamental group induced by the Hodge–Tate weights. The Colmez functor is realized anabelianly as the map on fundamental groups induced by the morphism of moduli stacks Modφ,Γ(ℛ) → Repla(GLd(ℚp)) to the moduli stack of locally analytic representations. The Breuil–Schneider conjecture translates into a statement about the dimension of the Selmer variety H1f(Gℚp, π1ét(Modφ,Γ(ℛ)ℚ̄p)) defined by the crystalline condition: the dimension equals the dimension of the locally analytic vectors in the corresponding Banach space representation. The Emerton–Gee stack (Emerton–Gee) provides a geometric realization of the moduli stack of (φ, Γ)-modules, and its fundamental group carries the anabelian data of the p-adic Langlands correspondence. The p-adic local Langlands correspondence for GL2(ℚp) (Colmez) is the case d=2 where the moduli stack of (φ, Γ)-modules of rank 2 is explicitly described by the parameter space of trianguline representations. The anabelian geometry of (φ, Γ)-modules is essential for the p-adic Langlands program because it provides a group-theoretic reconstruction of the Galois representation from the Galois action on the fundamental group of the moduli stack, without reference to the original representation.

Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p-adic Shtukas on the Fargues–Fontaine Curve and the Geometrization of the Local Langlands Correspondence

The Fargues–Fontaine curve XFF = XC, C♭ associated to a perfectoid field C of characteristic 0 and its tilt C♭ of characteristic p provides a geometric framework for the local Langlands correspondence. A p-adic shtuka on XFF is a vector bundle with a Frobenius structure (an isomorphism φ*E ≅ E away from a divisor). The moduli stack ShtG(XFF) of G-shtukas on the Fargues–Fontaine curve carries a fundamental group whose anabelian geometry realizes the local Langlands correspondence geometrically (Fargues–Scholze). The outer action of the Weil group Wℚp on π1ét(ShtG(XFF)C̄) factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acting on the profinite mapping class group of the Fargues–Fontaine curve. The reconstruction of a Langlands parameter φ: Wℚp → LG from the Galois action on the fundamental group proceeds via the cohomology of the moduli stack of shtukas: the ℓ-adic cohomology H*(ShtG(XFF), ℚℓ) carries an action of G × Wℚp and the Fargues–Scholze conjecture (theorem in many cases) states that the irreducible constituents of this cohomology realize the local Langlands correspondence. The anabelian geometry of shtukas on XFF connects the global anabelian geometry of curves over function fields (where shtukas were introduced by Drinfeld and Lafforgue) with the local anabelian geometry of p-adic fields. The fundamental group of the moduli stack of p-adic shtukas on XFF fits into an exact sequence relating it to the fundamental group of the moduli stack of G-bundles on XFF and the Frobenius action. The dimension of the Selmer variety H1f(Wℚp, π1ét(ShtG(XFF)C̄)) is given by the dimension of the corresponding L-packet in the local Langlands correspondence. The geometrization of the local Langlands correspondence via the Fargues–Fontaine curve is the p-adic analogue of the geometric Langlands correspondence over function fields. The anabelian reconstruction of the Langlands parameter from the Galois action on the fundamental group of the moduli stack of shtukas provides a group-theoretic characterization of the local Langlands correspondence that does not rely on the classification of representations of p-adic groups. A critical distinction is that the Fargues–Fontaine curve is not an algebraic curve but a complete curve in the category of adic spaces; its fundamental group is the pro-étale fundamental group, and the anabelian reconstruction uses the pro-étale fundamental group together with the Frobenius structure. The moduli stack of shtukas on XFF is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℤp, and its fundamental group captures the anabelian data of the local Langlands correspondence.

  • Confusing the Stokes filtration (for irregular connections, indexed by slopes of exponential factors) with the Swan conductor filtration (for wild ramification, indexed by Swan conductors of arbitrary local systems); they live on different moduli stacks and detect different phenomena.
  • Assuming the irregular Hodge filtration is the same as the Hodge filtration on the de Rham cohomology of the underlying vector bundle; the irregular Hodge filtration incorporates the exponential factors and is only defined for irregular connections.
  • Treating the pro-étale fundamental group of a higher-dimensional variety as a direct replacement for the étale fundamental group in all anabelian conjectures; the pro-étale fundamental group classifies pro-étale covers, and the anabelian reconstruction requires the additional Hodge filtration data from the Liu–Zhu period map.
  • Equating the parabolic non-abelian Hodge correspondence (a homeomorphism of moduli stacks) with the anabelian reconstruction of parabolic data from the Galois representation; the former is a geometric correspondence, the latter is a group-theoretic reconstruction using the section conjecture for moduli stacks.
  • Neglecting that the dimension formula for the Selmer variety in the wild ramification case involves Swan conductors, while in the irregular singularity case it involves irregularity indices, and in the parabolic case it involves centralizer dimensions of the monodromy eigenvalues; each formula is distinct and corresponds to a different filtration.
  • Overlooking that the Liu–Zhu p-adic non-abelian Hodge theory for higher-dimensional varieties requires the variety to be smooth and proper, and the local systems to be de Rham; the correspondence does not hold for arbitrary local systems or non-proper varieties without additional conditions.
  • Assuming the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group action on the profinite mapping class group with wild ramification or irregular singularities is the same as the action on the tame mapping class group; the presence of wild ramification or irregular singularities modifies the mapping class group and the GT action, and the anabelian reconstruction uses the modified action.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Logarithmic Poles

The moduli stack of p‑adic local systems on a smooth projective curve X over a p‑adic field K, equipped with logarithmic poles along a reduced divisor D, carries a natural pro‑étale fundamental group that classifies finite étale covers respecting the logarithmic structure. This fundamental group fits into an exact sequence relating the geometric fundamental group of the log curve (X, D) to the arithmetic fundamental group of the base field, and the outer action of the absolute Galois group G_K on the geometric fundamental group factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acting on the profinite mapping class group of the log curve. The logarithmic condition forces the local monodromy around each component of D to be unipotent, which translates into a nilpotent residue condition on the associated p‑adic connection; this condition is precisely what makes the log fundamental group amenable to anabelian reconstruction. In particular, the work of Mochizuki and Tamagawa shows that the isomorphism class of the log fundamental group together with its Galois action determines the pair (X, D) up to isomorphism, provided X is hyperbolic and D is non‑empty.

The reconstruction proceeds by first recovering the profinite completion of the topological fundamental group of the complex analytification of (X, D) from the p‑adic log fundamental group via the comparison isomorphism of p‑adic Hodge theory. The logarithmic de Rham fundamental group, equipped with its Hodge filtration and the Frobenius structure, provides the necessary period data to identify the underlying curve and divisor. The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group enters through its action on the profinite mapping class group of the log curve, which is identified with the outer automorphism group of the log fundamental group. This action encodes the combinatorial data of the dual graph of the stable reduction of (X, D) and allows one to distinguish non‑isomorphic log curves that share the same underlying topological type.

An important arithmetic application is the non‑abelian Chabauty method for integral points on log curves. The Selmer variety H1_f(G_K, π1log(XK, D)) parametrizes p‑adic local systems with logarithmic poles that are de Rham and crystalline at places of good reduction. The dimension of this Selmer variety is bounded by the dimension of the corresponding moduli space of log local systems, and when the bound is strict the set of integral points on X \ D is finite. The anabelian geometry of the log fundamental group thus provides a group‑theoretic criterion for finiteness that does not rely on height arguments, and it connects directly to the section conjecture for log curves: a rational point gives a section of the exact sequence, and conversely a section yields a point after imposing the logarithmic nilpotence condition.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Shimura Varieties with Infinite Level Structure

Shimura varieties at infinite level, constructed as perfectoid spaces by Scholze, carry a natural moduli stack of p‑adic local systems arising from the étale cohomology of the universal abelian variety. The pro‑étale fundamental group of this moduli stack captures the Galois action on the tower of finite‑level Shimura varieties and encodes the Hecke action through the Langlands correspondence. The absolute Galois group Gℚ_p acts on the fundamental group via the outer action on the geometric fundamental group of the perfectoid Shimura variety, and this action factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acting on the profinite mapping class group of the associated Fargues–Fontaine curve. The Fargues–Scholze geometrization of the local Langlands correspondence identifies the irreducible constituents of the ℓ‑adic cohomology of the moduli stack of shtukas on the Fargues–Fontaine curve with the local Langlands parameters, and the anabelian reconstruction of these parameters from the Galois action on the fundamental group provides a purely group‑theoretic characterization of the correspondence.

The exact sequence relating the fundamental group of the moduli stack of p‑adic local systems on the infinite‑level Shimura variety to the fundamental group of the moduli stack of G‑bundles on the Fargues–Fontaine curve and the Frobenius automorphism mirrors the global exact sequence for curves over function fields. The outer Galois action on the geometric fundamental group is compatible with the action of the Weil group Wℚ_p on the cohomology of the shtuka stack, and the dimension of the Selmer variety H1_f(Wℚ_p, π1ét(Sht_G(XFF)ℚ_p)) equals the dimension of the corresponding L‑packet. This equality is a direct consequence of the Fargues–Scholze conjecture, now a theorem in many cases, and it shows that the anabelian geometry of the fundamental group recovers the local Langlands correspondence without invoking the classification of representations of p‑adic groups.

A critical distinction is that the infinite‑level Shimura variety is not an algebraic variety but a perfectoid space; its fundamental group is the pro‑étale fundamental group, and the anabelian reconstruction uses the pro‑étale fundamental group together with the Frobenius structure on the Fargues–Fontaine curve. The moduli stack of p‑adic local systems on the infinite‑level Shimura variety is an Artin stack locally of finite type over ℤ_p, and its fundamental group captures the anabelian data of the local Langlands correspondence. Confusing the pro‑étale fundamental group with the classical étale fundamental group would miss the contribution of the perfectoid structure, which is essential for the comparison isomorphism between étale and de Rham cohomology in p‑adic Hodge theory.

Worked Example: Recovering the Log Curve from its Fundamental Group
Let (X, D) be a hyperbolic log curve over a p‑adic field K with D a reduced divisor. Suppose you are given the profinite group π1log(XK, D) equipped with the continuous outer action of G_K. Outline the steps to reconstruct the isomorphism class of (X, D).
1. Use the p‑adic comparison isomorphism (Liu–Zhu) to identify the log de Rham fundamental group with its Hodge filtration and Frobenius structure. 2. From the Hodge filtration recover the underlying complex log curve (X_ℂ, D_ℂ) via the non‑abelian Riemann–Hilbert correspondence for logarithmic connections. 3. The outer G_K‑action on π1log determines the descent data from ℂ to K; the Grothendieck–Teichmüller action on the profinite mapping class group of the log curve encodes the dual graph of the stable model. 4. The nilpotent residue condition on the logarithmic connection forces the local monodromy around each component of D to be unipotent, which uniquely determines the divisor D inside X. 5. By the anabelian theorem of Mochizuki–Tamagawa for log curves, the pair (X, D) is uniquely determined up to isomorphism by the equipped fundamental group.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the role of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group in an anabelian reconstruction, always mention its action on the profinite mapping class group and how this action encodes the combinatorial data of the dual graph of the stable reduction. Do not simply state that GT acts on the fundamental group; specify that the action is outer and factors through the outer automorphism group of the geometric fundamental group.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Torsion Level Structures and the Galois Action on the Profinite Mapping Class Group

For a smooth projective curve X over a number field K, the moduli stack of p‑adic local systems with a fixed torsion level structure N (i.e., a trivialization of the local system modulo N) carries a fundamental group that fits into an exact sequence linking the geometric fundamental group of the level‑N cover of X to the arithmetic fundamental group of K. The outer action of G_K on this fundamental group factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acting on the profinite mapping class group of the level‑N cover. The presence of the level structure rigidifies the moduli problem: the automorphism group of a local system with level structure is trivial, so the moduli stack is a Deligne–Mumford stack and its fundamental group is a genuine profinite group rather than a 2‑group. This rigidity allows one to apply the anabelian reconstruction theorem of Mochizuki for hyperbolic curves with level structure, which states that the equipped fundamental group determines the curve X together with its level‑N cover up to isomorphism.

The Galois action on the profinite mapping class group is described explicitly by the action of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group on the profinite completion of the mapping class group of the level‑N cover. The GT action preserves the symplectic form on the first homology of the cover and respects the level‑N symplectic structure. Consequently, the outer Galois representation ρ: G_K → Out(π1ét(XN,K)) lands in the subgroup of GT that respects the level structure. The image of ρ is open in this subgroup, a result that follows from the open image theorem for the Galois action on the Tate module of the Jacobian of X together with the compatibility of the GT action with the symplectic representation. This openness is a key input for the section conjecture with level structure: a rational point of X gives a section of the exact sequence, and the level structure forces the section to be compatible with the chosen trivialization modulo N.

An arithmetic consequence is the finiteness of the set of rational points on X that lift to a given level‑N cover. The Selmer variety H1_f(G_K, π1ét(XN,K)) parametrizes p‑adic local systems with level structure that are de Rham at all places. Its dimension is bounded by the dimension of the moduli space of such local systems, which equals 3g − 3 + dim G where g is the genus of X and G is the structure group. When the bound is strict, the Chabauty–Kim method yields finiteness of rational points. The anabelian geometry of the level‑N fundamental group thus provides a group‑theoretic criterion for finiteness that refines the classical Chabauty bound by incorporating the level structure.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Parabolic Structures and the Non‑abelian Chabauty Method

A parabolic structure on a p‑adic local system over a curve X with marked points {x_i} consists of a filtration of the fiber at each x_i together with a set of rational weights. The moduli stack of parabolic p‑adic local systems is an Artin stack whose pro‑étale fundamental group classifies finite étale covers preserving the parabolic data. The geometric fundamental group of this stack fits into an exact sequence with the arithmetic fundamental group of the base field, and the outer Galois action factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acting on the profinite mapping class group of the parabolic curve. The parabolic weights appear as eigenvalues of the local monodromy around the marked points; they are encoded in the conjugacy classes of the inertia subgroups inside the fundamental group. The anabelian reconstruction theorem for parabolic curves (due to Mochizuki and later refined by Hoshi) asserts that the equipped fundamental group together with the parabolic weight data determines the parabolic curve up to isomorphism.

The non‑abelian Chabauty method for parabolic curves uses the Selmer variety H1_f(G_K, π1par(XK, {weights})) which parametrizes parabolic p‑adic local systems that are de Rham and have the prescribed weights. The dimension of this Selmer variety is given by the dimension of the moduli space of parabolic local systems minus the dimension of the centralizers of the monodromy eigenvalues at the marked points. This formula differs from the unramified case because the parabolic weights impose extra constraints on the local Galois representations at the marked points. When the Selmer variety has dimension strictly smaller than the dimension of the space of rational points (which is zero for a curve), the set of rational points respecting the parabolic structure is finite. The method has been applied to prove finiteness of integral points on affine curves with prescribed asymptotic behavior at the cusps.

A subtle point is that the parabolic non‑abelian Hodge correspondence (a homeomorphism between the moduli stack of parabolic Higgs bundles and the moduli stack of parabolic local systems) is a geometric correspondence, whereas the anabelian reconstruction uses the section conjecture for the moduli stack of parabolic local systems. The two are related but not equivalent: the Hodge correspondence does not see the Galois action, while the anabelian reconstruction is entirely based on the Galois action on the fundamental group. Confusing the two leads to the erroneous belief that the parabolic weights can be read off from the Higgs field alone, whereas they are actually encoded in the Galois representation via the inertia weights.

The Anabelian Geometry of the Fundamental Group of the Moduli Stack of p‑adic Local Systems on Curves with Wild Ramification and the Tamagawa Group

When a p‑adic local system on a curve X over a p‑adic field K has wild ramification along a divisor D, the local monodromy is no longer quasi‑unipotent and the Swan conductor measures the wild part. The moduli stack of such wildly ramified local systems carries a fundamental group that incorporates the wild inertia subgroups. The Tamagawa group, introduced by Tamagawa in his work on the anabelian geometry of curves over p‑adic fields, is a quotient of the geometric fundamental group that captures the wild ramification data via the upper numbering filtration. The exact sequence relating the fundamental group of the moduli stack to the Tamagawa group and the arithmetic fundamental group of K is the local analogue of the global exact sequence for function fields. The outer action of G_K on the Tamagawa group factors through the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acting on the profinite mapping class group of the wildly ramified curve.

The anabelian reconstruction for wildly ramified curves (Tamagawa, Mochizuki) states that the equipped Tamagawa group together with its Galois action determines the curve X and the wild ramification divisor D up to isomorphism. The reconstruction uses the higher ramification groups in the upper numbering, which are canonically attached to the Galois representation, and the fact that the Swan conductor can be recovered from the dimension of the fixed subspace of the wild inertia action on the associated p‑adic representation. The Grothendieck–Teichmüller action on the profinite mapping class group of the wildly ramified curve is modified by the presence of wild ramification: the mapping class group is replaced by the wild mapping class group, which includes Dehn twists around the wild inertia subgroups. The GT action on this wild mapping class group encodes the combinatorial data of the wild ramification filtration.

In the function field setting, Drinfeld modules and shtukas provide a geometric realization of the wild ramification data. The moduli stack of shtukas on a curve over a finite field carries a fundamental group whose anabelian geometry recovers the global Langlands correspondence for GL_n. The Tamagawa group appears as the geometric fundamental group of the moduli stack of shtukas, and the Frobenius action on this group corresponds to the Galois action in the p‑adic case. The dimension formula for the Selmer variety in the wild case involves the sum of Swan conductors, whereas in the tame case it involves only the genus and the number of marked points. This distinction is crucial: neglecting the Swan conductor leads to an incorrect dimension bound and consequently to a false finiteness statement.

  • Assuming the logarithmic fundamental group of a curve with divisor is the same as the étale fundamental group of the complement; the log structure imposes nilpotent residue conditions that are essential for reconstruction.
  • Confusing the pro‑étale fundamental group of a perfectoid Shimura variety with the classical étale fundamental group; the perfectoid structure supplies the Frobenius needed for the Fargues–Fontaine curve.
  • Treating the level‑N fundamental group as a direct product of the geometric fundamental group and the level structure; the level structure rigidifies the moduli stack and changes the outer Galois action.
  • Equating the parabolic non‑abelian Hodge correspondence with the anabelian reconstruction of parabolic data; the former is a geometric homeomorphism, the latter is a group‑theoretic reconstruction using the section conjecture.
  • Neglecting the Swan conductor in the dimension formula for the Selmer variety of wildly ramified local systems; the wild ramification contribution is measured by Swan conductors, not by the genus alone.
  • Overlooking that the Grothendieck–Teichmüller action on the wild mapping class group differs from the action on the tame mapping class group; wild inertia modifies the Dehn twist generators.
  • Assuming the Tamagawa group is defined for arbitrary local systems; it is specifically the quotient of the geometric fundamental group that captures the upper numbering wild ramification filtration.

The advanced anabelian topics above illustrate how fundamental groups of moduli stacks encode arithmetic data and how their Galois actions drive reconstruction theorems.
